# The facilitation ecology — the entries

This file holds the reference material behind the survey at `ops/facilitation ecology.md`, in five parts:
- Part 1 holds the 299 outside techniques, grouped by their main scale and sorted by name within each scale;
- Part 2 holds the 61 single moves, grouped by what they do, as in section 7 of the survey;
- Part 3 holds the 37 stances a facilitator can hold;
- Part 4 holds the 24 mental models;
- Part 5 lists the vault's 264 holdings by the first scale at which each works.

Each technique has a table of its coordinates and, where the researchers recorded them, a second table of its practitioners' own words with plain meanings. Those words are the vocabulary the author asked for. The last rows of each table say where the entry came from and whether it was verified there. The row "In the vault" names the vault file where the technique appears, and a technique counts as met when the vault names it anywhere, including in a working file such as the reading queue.

The row "Who decides" uses the survey's five positions. At A the person decides all the content; at B the facilitator shapes the process and the people supply the content; at C the facilitator offers content as an option the person may take or leave; at D the facilitator directs or interprets with authority; and at E no live facilitator is present, and a structure, a text, or a custom does the work.

The same entries can be filtered by scale, subject, mental model, and who decides on the explorable page at https://claude.ai/artifact/DuVyj9ecaSZuBzXXgBEXHG.

## Part 1. The techniques, by main scale

### One person alone

This scale is the main one for 27 techniques, and the vault has met 6 of them.

#### Centering Prayer

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | William Meninger, Basil Pennington, and Thomas Keating (Trappist monks of St. Joseph's Abbey, Spencer, Massachusetts) |
| Date | 1970s |
| Scale | one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person chooses a sacred word as the symbol of their intention to consent to God's presence and action within, sits with eyes closed, and silently introduces the word; whenever they become aware of thoughts, they 'return ever-so-gently' to the sacred word, and at the end they remain in silence for a couple of minutes. |
| What it assumes | Change is attributed to God's action, to which the person consents in silence; the method only arranges the conditions for that consent. |
| Limits and critiques | Some Christian critics argue that it departs from the sources it cites, such as Teresa of Ávila and The Cloud of Unknowing, or that it is empty of content compared with traditional prayer. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `ops/reading queue.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centering_prayer; https://contemplativeoutreach-phoenix.org/media/attachments/2024/04/03/the-method-of-centering-prayer-by-thomas-keating.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sacred word* | A short word such as 'God', 'Jesus', or 'Abba', chosen as the symbol of the intention to consent to God's presence. |
| *consent* | The inner act the prayer consists of: agreeing to God's presence and action within. |
| *return ever-so-gently* | The instruction for what to do on noticing thoughts: go back to the sacred word without struggle. |

#### Dream incubation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ancient Greek healing sanctuaries of Asclepius (enkoimesis); in modern psychology, studied by Deirdre Barrett |
| Date | 5th–4th century BCE (Greek sanctuaries); modern study 1993 |
| Scale | one person alone, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Before sleep the person keeps a chosen problem or question in mind as the last thought of the day, sometimes placing an object that represents it by the bed, and on waking lies still and records any dream. In the ancient Greek form, a sick person purified themselves, made offerings, and slept in the sanctuary's restricted dormitory in order to meet the god in a dream. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from setting a question before sleep, on the expectation that dreams can offer guidance or solutions that waking thought has not produced. |
| Limits and critiques | Results in Barrett's study were uneven: only some participants dreamed about their problem, and personal problems were judged solved more often than objective ones. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological. The ancient form is temple ritual; the modern form is a secular self-help technique |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, decision choice, spirit ultimate, body |
| Medium | silence, ritual object, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream_incubation; https://www.namuseum.gr/en/monthly_artefact/the-magical-healing-sleep/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *enkoimesis* | Ancient Greek temple sleep: sleeping in a sanctuary in order to receive a healing dream. |
| *abaton* | The restricted sleeping hall of an Asclepius sanctuary, entered after purification, sacrifice, and prayer. |
| *committee of sleep* | The title of Barrett's study and later book on using dreams to solve problems. |

#### Evening self-examination (the Stoic evening review)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Quintus Sextius, as reported by Seneca; Seneca |
| Date | 1st century CE (Seneca, De Ira 3.36) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | At night, when the lamp is out and the house is quiet, the person goes over their whole day, reviewing what they did and said and hiding nothing from themselves. Sextius's version asked three questions: what fault of yours have you cured today, what vice have you resisted, and in what respect are you better? |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a daily habit of reviewing one's own conduct without self-deception, which Seneca says also brings calm, deep sleep. |
| Mental model | other. Hellenistic philosophy practised as a daily discipline of life |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, thought belief, emotion |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Sen.+Ira+3.36&lang=original |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *speculator sui censorque secretus* | Seneca's phrase for the person acting as the secret observer and judge of themselves during the review. |
| *totum diem meum scrutor* | 'I examine my whole day': Seneca's description of his own nightly practice. |
| *Quod hodie malum tuum sanasti?* | 'What fault of yours have you cured today?': the first of Sextius's three nightly questions. |

#### Expressive writing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | James W. Pennebaker (first study co-authored with Beall) |
| Date | 1986 |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The writer spends fifteen to twenty minutes a day, on three or four days, writing continuously about their deepest thoughts and feelings concerning a stressful or traumatic experience, without attending to spelling or grammar. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from putting an unspoken upsetting experience into words over several sessions; the early explanation was that disclosure ends the strain of holding it back. |
| Limits and critiques | Writers often feel worse right after a session, benefits vary by population (reviews of studies with cancer patients found mostly non-significant results), and the inhibition explanation is only partly supported. |
| Mental model | other. Experimental health psychology: disclosure of emotional experience and its effect on health |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, memory past, trauma healing |
| Medium | writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3830620/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressive_writing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *expressive writing* | Writing about one's deepest thoughts and feelings concerning an upsetting experience, compared in studies with writing about neutral topics. |
| *deepest thoughts and feelings* | The standard instruction's phrase for what the writer explores, which goes beyond a factual account of the events. |
| *inhibition* | Pennebaker's early explanation: actively holding back thoughts and feelings is a strain on the body, and disclosure relieves it. |

#### Focusing, practised alone

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Eugene Gendlin |
| Date | research from the 1950s–1960s; the book Focusing, 1978 |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person clears a space by setting their concerns aside one by one, chooses one, and waits for its unclear bodily feel to form; they then find a word or image that fits it, check the word against the feeling, ask what the feeling needs, and receive whatever shift comes. |
| What it assumes | Change happens in the body's implicit sense of a situation, which moves forward when it is attended to and put into fitting words. |
| Limits and critiques | Gendlin wrote in 1964 that focusing alone is possible but unsteady and difficult and that an understanding listener helps it move, which the vault records as an objection to the solitary form. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness, thought belief |
| Medium | breath body, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/the solo practice reaches the felt shift and the primitives then surface by themselves.md` |
| Sources | https://www.focusing.org.uk/an-introduction-to-focusing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *felt sense* | A vague, not-yet-worded bodily sense of a whole situation or problem. |
| *handle* | A word, phrase, or image that fits the quality of the felt sense. |
| *resonating* | Checking the handle against the felt sense until they match. |
| *felt shift* | An opening or release in the body, sometimes with a sigh, when the felt sense moves. |

#### Hitbodedut (secluded prayer)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Rabbi Nachman of Breslov (Breslov Hasidism) |
| Date | c. 1800 (Nachman's teachings in Likutey Moharan) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Every day the person sets aside time, at least an hour by Nachman's counsel, to pray alone and speak to God in their own words and in the language they understand best, about everything they are going through; even a single sincere word counts. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a daily, personal conversation with God in which a person says what is actually happening in their life. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, emotion, relationship absent |
| Medium | talk, silence, nature place |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://breslov.org/hitbodedut/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hitbodedut* | Secluded prayer: private, unstructured conversation with God, alone. |
| *in your own words* | Nachman's instruction to express oneself to God in one's own words, in the language one understands best. |

#### Intensive Journal method

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ira Progoff (Dialogue House) |
| Date | 1966 (Dialogue House founded); At a Journal Workshop, 1975 |
| Scale | one person alone, one person and their parts, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person keeps a structured workbook with separate sections, such as a list of the major phases of their life, written dialogues with people, their body, their work, and events, and records of dreams and imagery, and writes entries without commentary or analysis. Entries in one section are then used to prompt writing in other sections, a process Progoff called Journal Feedback; the method is usually learned at a workshop and then continued alone. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recording one's life without judgment and letting the recorded material prompt further material, until the direction of one's life becomes clearer. |
| Mental model | other. Depth psychology without interpretation: entries are recorded without commentary or analysis and allowed to prompt one another |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, meaning purpose, identity self, relationship absent |
| Medium | writing, silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.intensivejournal.org/articles/Learnmore_Articles_AboutMethod-InterviewWithIra.php |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Steppingstones* | A short list of the major phases and turning points of one's life, written in the Life/Time section. |
| *Journal Feedback* | The process of letting what is written in one section of the journal prompt new writing in another section. |
| *Dialogue Dimension* | The group of sections in which the writer composes dialogues with persons, their work, their body, and events. |
| *Roads Taken and Not Taken* | A section for recording important choices and the paths not followed. |

#### Ladder of inference

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Chris Argyris; popularized by Peter Senge and colleagues in The Fifth Discipline Fieldbook |
| Date | 1980s–1990s |
| Scale | one person alone, an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A person traces a conclusion back down the steps by which they reached it, from the observable data, to the details they selected, to the meanings and assumptions they added, and then checks each step with others by asking what they saw and how they reasoned. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from making one's reasoning visible, so that conclusions can be tested against the data other people see. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | nested levels; a single move |
| Subjects | thought belief, conflict, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladder_of_inference |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ladder of inference* | A model of the steps by which a person moves, usually without noticing, from what they observe to conclusions, beliefs, and actions. |
| *observable data* | What could be recorded by a camera, the bottom rung from which inference starts. |
| *reflexive loop* | The way a person's beliefs shape which data they select next, which reinforces the beliefs. |

#### Lectio divina (practised alone)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Christian monastic tradition (the Rule of Benedict); four stages named by Guigo II, Carthusian prior of the Grande Chartreuse |
| Date | 6th century (Benedictine practice); four-stage form 12th century |
| Scale | one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person reads a short passage of scripture slowly, reflects at length on a word or phrase from it, answers God in prayer, and then rests in silent attention to God. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from reading a text slowly enough that understanding leads into prayer and then into silent contact with God. |
| Limits and critiques | The tradition treats lectio divina as a practice of a monastic community, so solitary use separates it from the community that originally practised it. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness, meaning purpose |
| Medium | reading text, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/both poles of the convergence refuse the engineered epiphany.md` |
| Sources | http://www.ldysinger.com/@texts2/1180_guigo-2/02_lad_sel-lec.htm; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lectio_Divina |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *lectio* | Reading: slow, attentive reading of a scripture passage. |
| *meditatio* | Meditation: working out the passage's meaning with the mind, which Guigo compares to chewing food. |
| *oratio* | Prayer: the heart turning to God in response to the reading. |
| *contemplatio* | Contemplation: the mind raised above itself in God, which Guigo describes as tasting sweetness. |

#### Life review

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Robert N. Butler |
| Date | 1963 (published paper; one encyclopedia dates Butler's essay to 1961) |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | An older person, often prompted by nearing the end of life, returns to memories from across their life and evaluates them, including painful experiences and unresolved conflicts, rather than only recalling pleasant times. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from facing and re-evaluating one's whole life as death approaches, which Butler held can resolve old conflicts. |
| Limits and critiques | Butler also linked the process to late-life depression, so reviewing a life can bring despair as well as resolution. |
| Mental model | developmental |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, death finitude, meaning purpose, identity self |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Neil_Butler; https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/00332747.1963.11023339 |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *life review* | Butler's name for the natural late-life process of recalling and re-evaluating one's whole past, including its conflicts. |
| *reminiscence* | Recalling personal memories; Butler argued that reminiscence in older people is not a meaningless process. |

#### Maraṇasati (mindfulness of death) and the Five Remembrances

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Early Buddhism (Pali canon: the Maraṇassati Sutta, AN 6.19, and the Upajjhaṭṭhana Sutta, AN 5.57) |
| Date | ancient |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The practitioner reflects often, even daily, that they are subject to aging, illness, and death, that they will be separated from all that is dear, and that they are the owner and heir of their actions; in the Maraṇassati Sutta the practice is judged most earnest when one reflects that one may not live even for the span of one breath. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from keeping one's own death and impermanence in mind, which is meant to make one heedful and to reduce attachment. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Early Buddhist and non-theistic rather than strictly nondual |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | death finitude, grief, attention awareness |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | `notes/buddhist impermanence faces loss as real rather than as veiled presence.md` |
| Sources | https://www.accesstoinsight.org/tipitaka/an/an06/an06.019.than.html; https://www.accesstoinsight.org/tipitaka/an/an05/an05.057.than.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *maraṇasati* | Mindfulness or recollection of death. |
| *the Five Remembrances* | Five facts to reflect on often: aging, illness, death, separation from what is dear, and ownership of one's actions. |

#### Mental noting (Mahasi insight meditation)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Mahasi Sayadaw (Burmese Theravada Buddhism) |
| Date | 1940s–1950s (first group instructed at the Sasana Yeiktha, Rangoon, 1949) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The meditator attends to the rise and fall of the abdomen and silently labels each movement 'rising' or 'falling'; whenever something else takes over, such as a thought or an intention, they label it in one word ('thinking', 'intending', 'wandering') and return to the abdomen. The same labeling continues during slow walking and daily activities. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from observing each moment of experience closely and continuously, which is meant to produce direct insight into how experience arises and passes. |
| Limits and critiques | Jack Engler (1984) argued that insight meditation is an uncovering technique that presupposes a cohesive sense of self and may be contraindicated for some severe psychological conditions. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Theravada Buddhist insight practice; non-theistic rather than strictly nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | attention awareness, body, thought belief |
| Medium | silence, breath body |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md |
| Sources | https://mahajana.net/en/texts/mahasit1.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mental note* | A single silent word that names the experience happening now, such as 'rising' or 'thinking'. |
| *rising, falling* | The labels for the abdomen's movement with the in-breath and out-breath, used as the main object of attention. |
| *vipassanā* | Insight meditation: close observation of experience as it happens, which noting is designed to support. |

#### Mettā bhāvanā (loving-kindness meditation)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Theravada Buddhism (the Karaṇīya Mettā Sutta); method set out by Buddhaghosa in the Visuddhimagga |
| Date | ancient; Visuddhimagga, 5th century CE |
| Scale | one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The meditator silently wishes well-being first to themselves, then to a respected person, a dear person, a neutral person, and a hostile person, continuing until the goodwill is felt equally for all of them. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from repeatedly generating goodwill toward more and more people, including difficult ones, until the mind no longer divides them into favored and disfavored. |
| Limits and critiques | The tradition itself warns that attached affection can pass for goodwill, so a feeling of warmth is not by itself evidence of the quality. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Buddhist and non-theistic rather than strictly nondual |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship absent, conflict |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.accesstoinsight.org/lib/authors/buddharakkhita/wheel365.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mettā* | Goodwill or loving-kindness: the wish for the welfare of others. |
| *near enemy* | A counterfeit that resembles the real quality; for mettā it is greed, lust, and worldly affection, which feel like love but are self-seeking. |
| *remote enemy* | The obvious opposite of the quality; for mettā it is ill-will, anger, and hatred. |
| *breaking down the barriers* | The point in the practice at which goodwill no longer distinguishes between oneself, a revered person, a dear person, a neutral person, and an enemy. |

#### Morning Pages

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Julia Cameron |
| Date | 1992 (in The Artist's Way) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | On waking, before anything else, the person writes three pages by hand of whatever passes through their mind, without judging or correcting it. The pages are not shown to anyone, and during Cameron's course they are not reread until later. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the daily habit of writing down whatever is in the mind without judgment, which Cameron presents as making it easier to begin creative work. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a single move |
| Subjects | creativity play, thought belief, attention awareness |
| Medium | writing |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://juliacameronlive.com/; https://juliacameronlive.com/faq/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Artist%27s_Way |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Morning Pages* | Three handwritten pages of stream-of-consciousness writing done first thing in the morning, about anything at all. |
| *Artist Date* | The companion practice: a weekly outing taken alone to do something enjoyable. |

#### Muḥāsaba (self-accounting)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Islamic ethics and Sufism; a saying attributed to Umar ibn al-Khattab; developed by al-Ḥārith al-Muḥāsibī and later al-Ghazālī |

In the classical sequence self-accounting follows setting conditions for the soul in advance and watchfulness over oneself during the day. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from holding oneself to account now, before the final reckoning, which protects the soul from heedlessness. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, memory past, decision choice |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |

|---|---|
| *muḥāsaba* | Taking account of one's own actions; self-examination. |
| *murāqaba* | Watchfulness: attending to oneself during the day, the stage before self-accounting. |
| *mushāraṭa* | Setting conditions for the soul in advance, the first stage of the sequence. |

#### Naikan

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Yoshimoto Ishin (Jōdo Shinshū Buddhist priest and businessman) |
| Date | 1940s |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person reviews their relationships systematically, one person and one period of life at a time from childhood onward, asking what they received from that person, what they returned to them, and what troubles and worries they caused them. In the intensive form they spend about a week in extended solitary reflection, interrupted by brief interviews with a guide who hears what they recalled. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recalling concretely what one has received from others, given to them, and caused them, which changes how one understands one's relationships. |
| Limits and critiques | The traditional focus on family relationships may suit people from fragmented or seriously dysfunctional families less well, although practitioners can adapt it. |
| Mental model | other. Japanese Buddhist-derived introspection, now also used as a secular therapy |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | relationship absent, memory past, identity self |
| Medium | silence, talk |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naikan |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *naikan* | Japanese for 'introspection'. |
| *the three questions* | What did I receive from this person? What did I return to this person? What troubles and worries did I cause this person? |

#### Poustinia

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Russian Orthodox tradition; brought to North America by Catherine de Hueck Doherty (Madonna House Apostolate) |
| Date | traditional; Doherty's book Poustinia, 1975 |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person goes alone to a small, bare cabin furnished with a cot, a table, and a chair, taking only a Bible, and stays there in silence without communication, eating only bread with tea or coffee, to listen for God; they may simply rest if they need to, and then return to ordinary life. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from silence, solitude, fasting, and scripture, which allow a person to meet God experientially. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | silence, reading text, nature place |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://publications.madonnahouse.org/products/poustinia; https://www.hermitary.com/bookreviews/doherty.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *poustinia* | Russian for 'desert': a place of silence, solitude, and prayer where one goes to meet God. |
| *poustinik* | A person who lives in or goes to the poustinia. |

#### Praemeditatio malorum (premeditation of future evils)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Cyrenaic philosophers, as reported by Cicero; taken up by the Stoics, including Seneca |
| Date | 1st century BCE (Cicero, Tusculan Disputations III) and 1st century CE (Seneca), with earlier Cyrenaic roots |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person deliberately imagines, in advance, losses and misfortunes that could happen, such as the death of someone dear, and reminds themselves that such things belong to human life, so that when a loss comes it is not a surprise. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from foreseeing loss before it happens, which is thought to lessen its impact when it does. |
| Mental model | other. Hellenistic philosophy practised as a discipline of life |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | death finitude, future vision, emotion, grief |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.thelatinlibrary.com/cicero/tusc3.shtml |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *praemeditatio futurorum malorum* | Cicero's Latin for 'premeditation of future evils', which he says eases their arrival when they are foreseen long before. |
| *sciebam me genuisse mortalem* | 'I knew I had fathered a mortal': the words Cicero attributes to Anaxagoras on hearing of his son's death, given as the model of a foreseen loss. |

#### RAIN (Recognize, Allow, Investigate, Nurture)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Michele McDonald (Insight Meditation Society teacher); adapted and popularized by Tara Brach |
| Date | contemporary; the date of the original acronym was not confirmed |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | When caught in a difficult emotion, the person first recognizes what is happening, then allows it to be as it is, investigates how it feels in the body with kind attention, and finally either rests in awareness without identifying with the emotion (the original last step) or deliberately offers themselves care (Brach's version). |
| What it assumes | Change comes from meeting a difficult emotion with mindful and kind attention instead of reacting to it, which reduces one's identification with it. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Western insight-meditation teaching adapted for working with difficult emotions |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, attention awareness, identity self |
| Medium | silence, breath body |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.tarabrach.com/blog-two-versions-of-acronym-rain/; https://tricycle.org/magazine/rain/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Recognize* | Noticing and naming what is happening inside at this moment. |
| *Allow* | Letting the experience be as it is, without trying to fix it or push it away. |
| *Investigate* | Exploring the experience, especially how it feels in the body, with gentle attention. |
| *Non-identification / Nurture* | The two versions of the last step: McDonald's original, not taking the experience as who one is, and Brach's, deliberately offering oneself kindness. |

#### Self-Compassion Break

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kristin Neff |
| Date | 2000s (Neff's definition of self-compassion, 2003; Mindful Self-Compassion program with Christopher Germer, 2013) |
| Scale | one person alone, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | In a stressful moment the person says three kinds of phrase to themselves: one that acknowledges the pain ('This is a moment of suffering'), one that recalls that pain is shared ('Suffering is a part of life'), and one that offers kindness ('May I be kind to myself'). |
| What it assumes | Change comes from meeting one's own suffering with mindful awareness, a sense of shared humanity, and kindness instead of self-criticism. |
| Mental model | other. Secular psychology drawing on Buddhist teaching about compassion |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a single move |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://ggia.berkeley.edu/practice/self_compassion_break; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-compassion |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mindfulness* | In Neff's model, holding painful feelings in balanced awareness, neither suppressing nor exaggerating them. |
| *common humanity* | Recognizing that suffering and failure are part of shared human experience rather than isolating. |
| *self-kindness* | Being warm toward oneself in pain or failure instead of self-critical. |

#### Self-enquiry (ātma-vichāra), 'Who am I?'

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ramana Maharshi |
| Date | c. 1902 (the questions later published as Nan Yar?) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The practitioner turns attention to the sense of 'I' and keeps it there; when other thoughts arise, instead of following them, they ask 'To whom do they arise?', which returns attention from the thought to the one having it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from tracing the sense of 'I' back to its source, where, Ramana taught, the 'I'-thought itself subsides and only the non-dual Self remains. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | identity self, attention awareness, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.gururamana.org/Teachings/who_am_I.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Nan Yar? (Who am I?)* | The title of the set of questions and answers, first put to Ramana around 1902, that sets out the practice. |
| *the 'I'-thought* | Ramana's name for the first thought to arise in the mind, the sense of being a separate self, from which all other thoughts follow. |
| *To whom do they arise?* | The counter-question asked whenever a distracting thought appears, to return attention to its source. |

#### Shikoku henro (the 88-temple pilgrimage), walked alone

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Japanese Shingon Buddhist tradition associated with Kūkai (Kōbō Daishi) |
| Date | traditional; associated with Kūkai (born 774); pilgrim travel regulated by permits in the Edo period |

| What happens | The pilgrim walks the roughly 1,400-kilometre circuit of 88 temples around the island of Shikoku in a white jacket and sedge hat, with a staff, collecting a temple seal in a book at each stop. Although walking alone, the pilgrim is understood to be accompanied by Kōbō Daishi, and local residents give pilgrims gifts along the way. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the long walk itself, the repeated temple visits, and the felt company of the saint and of the local people who support pilgrims. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological. A Shingon Buddhist devotional pilgrimage |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, body, meaning purpose |
| Medium | movement dance, nature place, ritual object |
| Time | residential |

|---|---|

| *henro* | Pilgrim; local people address pilgrims as o-henro-san. |
| *osettai* | Gifts or alms that residents give to passing pilgrims. |
| *nōkyōchō* | The book in which the pilgrim collects each temple's stamp and seal. |

#### The Daily Examen

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ignatius of Loyola (Society of Jesus) |
| Date | 16th century (Spiritual Exercises) |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Once or twice a day, at noon and at night in the Jesuit rule, the person asks God for light, gives thanks, reviews the hours just past, faces their shortcomings, and looks ahead to the next day. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from noticing, each day and in God's presence, how one actually responded to the day's events, so that the next day is lived with more awareness. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, memory past, emotion, decision choice |
| Medium | silence |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.ignatianspirituality.com/ignatian-prayer/the-examen/; https://www.ignatianspirituality.com/the-steps-of-the-examen/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Examination_of_conscience |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

|---|---|
| *examen* | The short daily prayer of reviewing one's day, which Ignatius required Jesuits to make twice a day. |
| *particular examen* | A form of the examen that aims to change one particular fault or habit. |
| *examen of consciousness* | A broader form that reviews how God was present in the day and how one responded, not only one's faults. |

#### The Jesus Prayer (hesychast prayer)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Eastern Orthodox hesychast monks; texts gathered in the Philokalia by Nikodemos the Hagiorite and Makarios of Corinth |
| Date | Byzantine period onward; Philokalia published in Venice, 1782 |
| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person repeats the short prayer 'Lord Jesus Christ, Son of God, have mercy on me' continually, aloud or silently and often in time with the breath, seeking inner stillness. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from unceasing prayer that brings the mind to stillness in the presence of God. |
| Limits and critiques | Translators of the Philokalia warned that practising the prayer without adequate guidance could lead to spiritual delusion, and some Orthodox teachers discouraged the breathing techniques altogether. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness, body |
| Medium | silence, breath body |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://orthodoxwiki.org/Hesychasm; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philokalia |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

|---|---|
| *hesychia* | Stillness: the inner quiet the prayer aims at. |
| *hesychasm* | The Eastern Christian tradition of stillness prayer centered on the Jesus Prayer. |
| *Philokalia* | The anthology of Eastern Christian texts on prayer compiled by Nikodemos and Makarios and published in 1782, which spread the practice. |

#### The Work of Byron Katie

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Byron Katie |
| Date | 1986 |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person writes stressful judgments about someone on a worksheet in short, uncensored sentences and tests each thought with four questions: Is it true? Can you absolutely know that it's true? How do you react, what happens, when you believe that thought? Who would you be without that thought? Then they turn the thought around, toward themselves, toward the other person, and to its opposite, and look for real examples of how each turnaround could be as true. |
| What it assumes | Suffering comes from believing stressful thoughts, and change comes from questioning them until the person no longer believes them automatically. |
| Mental model | other. Self-inquiry into beliefs, which Katie describes as a meditation; its questions resemble cognitive therapy's testing of beliefs |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, conflict, relationship absent |
| Medium | writing, silence, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md` |
| Sources | https://thework.com/instruction-the-work-byron-katie/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Judge-Your-Neighbor Worksheet* | The form on which one writes down one's judgments about a person in a specific stressful situation. |
| *the four questions* | The fixed questions against which each written thought is tested. |
| *turnaround* | Restating the thought in its opposites, toward oneself, toward the other, and to the opposite, and finding examples of each. |

#### Tonglen (sending and taking)

| Scale | one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | After connecting with the wish to awaken for the benefit of others, the practitioner breathes in while imagining taking in the suffering of others, and their own, and breathes out while imagining sending them happiness, relief, and love. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from deliberately turning toward suffering instead of away from it, which is meant to awaken compassion. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Mahayana Buddhist compassion practice |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship absent, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | breath body, silence |
| Time | session |
