| *tonglen* | Tibetan for 'taking and sending': taking in suffering on the in-breath and sending out relief on the out-breath. |
| *lojong* | Tibetan 'mind training': the body of teachings, attributed to Atiśa, of which tonglen is a part. |
| *bodhichitta* | The awakened heart-mind: the aspiration to awaken for the benefit of all beings, with which the practice begins. |

#### Unsent letter (letter to the absent or dead)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | No single originator; a common technique in grief counseling and thanatology, drawing on expressive writing |
| Date | undated; in wide use in bereavement work |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person writes a letter to someone who is dead, absent, or unreachable, saying what they could not say, whether grief, love, anger, regret, or thanks, with no intention of sending it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from addressing the absent person directly in writing, which lets unfinished feelings be expressed and the relationship be reworked. |
| Limits and critiques | Research has not yet established letter writing's effect as a standalone intervention; the evidence comes mostly from its use within wider grief programs. |
| Mental model | other. Draws on expressive-writing research and on grief theory about continuing bonds with the dead |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a single move |
| Subjects | relationship absent, grief, emotion |
| Medium | writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12009924/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unsent letter* | A letter written to express what one could not say, with no intention of sending it. |
| *continuing bonds* | The grief-research idea that mourners keep a symbolic relationship with the dead; letters to the deceased are one way of doing so. |

### One person and their parts

This scale is the main one for 17 techniques, and the vault has met 2 of them.

#### 3-2-1 Shadow Process

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Integral Institute (Ken Wilber and colleagues), drawing on Jungian shadow work |
| Date | 2000s (Integral Life Practice) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person picks someone or something that strongly disturbs or attracts them and first describes it in the third person ('face it'), then speaks to it directly in the second person ('talk to it'), and finally speaks as it in the first person ('be it'), claiming the quality as their own. |
| What it assumes | Disowned qualities are pushed away by a move from first to second to third person, and reversing that order brings them back as one's own. |
| Mental model | transpersonal. Ken Wilber's integral theory |
| Structure | moving through stages; a single move |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, conflict |
| Medium | writing, talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://integrallife.com/the-3-2-1-shadow-process/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *shadow* | Qualities of oneself that have been disowned and are often seen in others instead. |
| *3: face it* | Describing the disturbing person or quality in the third person, as 'he', 'she', or 'it'. |
| *2: talk to it* | Addressing it directly as 'you' in dialogue. |
| *1: be it* | Speaking as the quality in the first person, 'I', to take it back as one's own. |

#### Active imagination

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Gustav Jung |
| Date | from 1913 (Jung's own confrontation with the unconscious) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | While awake, the person lets an image, figure, or mood arise from the unconscious, watches it with receptive attention, and then engages it by speaking with it, writing the exchange down, or painting it. Jungians add a final step: treating what was learned as an ethical obligation in one's life. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a real exchange between the conscious ego and images from the unconscious, in which the images are met as partners rather than explained from outside. |
| Limits and critiques | The IAAP account stresses that insight from the images must be turned into ethical responsibility, and that failing to do so has destructive effects on the person and on others. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Jungian analytical psychology |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, creativity play, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | art image, writing, silence |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://iaap.org/jung-analytical-psychology/short-articles-on-analytical-psychology/active-imagination-2/; https://jungutah.org/blog/active-imagination-the-transcendent-function-a-moving-experience-part-1-of-2/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *active imagination* | A waking dialogue between the conscious ego and images that emerge from the unconscious. |
| *transcendent function* | Jung's name for the process by which a new attitude emerges from the tension between conscious and unconscious opposites. |

#### Core Transformation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Connirae Andreas (with Tamara Andreas) |
| Date | 1989 (developed); book Core Transformation, 1994 |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The person chooses an unwanted feeling or reaction, finds the part responsible, and asks what it wants through that behavior, then what it wants through having that, and so on, until the chain of wants reaches a deep positive state the method calls a core state. The chain is then reversed, so that having the core state first changes each earlier want and the original reaction. |
| What it assumes | Every part is ultimately seeking a positive core state, and change comes from letting the part have that state directly rather than through the problem behavior. |
| Mental model | other. A ten-step questioning process developed by Connirae Andreas after an encounter with Milton Erickson's hypnotherapy |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.coretransformation.org/the-core-transformation-story-how-the-process-came-to-be/; https://www.coretransformation.org/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *core state* | The deepest positive state a part is ultimately seeking, reached at the end of the outcome chain. |
| *outcome chain* | The sequence of deeper and deeper wants that a part reveals when repeatedly asked what it wants through the previous one. |
| *reversing the outcome chain* | Starting from the core state and letting it change each earlier want in turn. |

#### Dialogical self methods (Self-Confrontation Method, Personal Position Repertoire)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Hubert Hermans (dialogical self theory with Harry Kempen and Rens van Loon; the Self-Confrontation Method with Els Hermans-Jansen) |
| Date | 1992 (dialogical self theory) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The person lists internal positions (such as 'I as perfectionist') and external positions (such as 'my father') and rates their relations in a matrix, or tells their life stories from different positions. They then look at which positions dominate or are silenced and whether their relations are dialogical or one-sided, and practise taking a meta-position that surveys them all. |
| What it assumes | The self is a multiplicity of positions in dialogue, and change comes from repositioning and from better dialogue among them. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship absent, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialogical_self |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *I-position* | One of the many standpoints from which a person experiences and speaks, each of which can be given a voice. |
| *external position* | A significant other or group that is part of the extended self, such as 'my father'. |
| *meta-position* | An overarching standpoint from which the person can survey and relate to their other positions. |
| *Personal Position Repertoire* | An individual procedure that maps a person's internal and external positions in a matrix. |

#### Ego state therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John G. Watkins and Helen H. Watkins, building on Paul Federn's concept of ego states |
| Date | late 20th century (Ego States: Theory and Therapy, 1997) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist, often using hypnosis though conversation can also serve, contacts distinct ego states within the client and works with them as a family therapist works with family members, helping conflicting states communicate and settle their conflicts. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from resolving conflicts among the ego states within one person, using the methods of group and family therapy. |
| Limits and critiques | It draws heavily on hypnosis and on work with dissociation, and its sources distinguish ordinary facets of personality from the sharply separate ego states found in dissociative identity disorder. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Psychodynamic theory combined with hypnosis and family-therapy techniques |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, trauma healing, conflict |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ego-state_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ego state* | An organized segment of personality with its own typical feelings, thoughts, and behaviors, formed in response to life experiences. |
| *family of self* | The Watkinses' picture of one person as a family of ego states, whose conflicts can be resolved with family-therapy methods. |

#### Ego states in transactional analysis (Parent, Adult, Child)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Eric Berne |
| Date | 1957 (ego-state paper); Transactional Analysis in Psychotherapy, 1961; Games People Play, 1964 |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | People learn to recognize whether they are speaking and acting from their Parent, Adult, or Child ego state and to analyze the exchanges between their states and other people's. Repeated games and a life script laid down in childhood then become visible and open to change. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recognizing which ego state is active and bringing the Adult into charge, so that childhood patterns and scripts can be revised. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics note that Berne never developed an adequate technique for reversing scripts and that the theory depends heavily on content analysis. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Social psychiatry developed out of psychoanalysis |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship present, memory past |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the routing surfaces recounted (speaking being, moreno, the scharmer count).md |
| Sources | https://ericberne.com/transactional-analysis/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transactional_analysis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ego state* | A consistent pattern of feeling and experience tied to a consistent pattern of behavior; Berne named three: Parent, Adult, and Child. |
| *transaction* | The unit of social exchange: a stimulus from one person and a response from another, each coming from some ego state. |
| *game* | A repeating sequence of transactions that leads to an outcome the parties have not consciously agreed to. |
| *script* | A life plan formed in childhood that a person keeps replaying even when it brings pain or defeat. |

#### Empty-chair and two-chair work (Gestalt therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Fritz Perls (Gestalt therapy), drawing partly on J. L. Moreno's psychodrama |
| Date | 1950s–1960s (Gestalt Therapy, 1951; Esalen workshops, 1964–1970) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The person speaks to an imagined figure placed in an empty chair, such as an absent or dead person or a part of themselves, and may switch chairs to answer as that figure. The dialogue brings an unresolved feeling into the present so that it can be expressed and completed. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from bringing an incomplete experience into present contact and expression, so that it can be completed and stop recurring. |
| Limits and critiques | Perls's best-known work was public demonstration without an ongoing therapeutic relationship, and commentators warn that the filmed hot-seat style does not represent Gestalt therapy as a whole. |
| Mental model | Gestalt |
| Structure | holding two poles; a single move |
| Subjects | relationship absent, emotion, conflict, identity self |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://gestaltreview.com/fritz-perls/; https://s3.eu-west-2.amazonaws.com/counselling-tutor/Podcasts/CT+Topdog+and+Underdog+in+Gestalt+Therapy.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unfinished business* | Emotional situations and unexpressed needs from the past that remain incomplete and keep returning to awareness. |
| *topdog / underdog* | Perls's names for a common split: a demanding 'you should' voice and a resisting, apparently powerless voice that tends to win by passive means. |
| *hot seat* | The chair in Perls's workshops where a volunteer did Gestalt work while the group watched. |

#### Feeding Your Demons

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Lama Tsultrim Allione, inspired by Machig Labdrön's Chöd |
| Date | 2008 (book); based on an 11th–12th-century Tibetan lineage |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person locates a fear, craving, or conflict in the body, personifies it as a demon, and asks it aloud what it wants, what it needs, and how it will feel if it gets what it needs, then changes places and answers as the demon. They then imagine dissolving their body into nectar of whatever the demon needs, feed it to full satisfaction, meet the ally it becomes, and finally rest in awareness. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from nurturing rather than fighting what one fears, since fighting a demon strengthens it and feeding it lets it change. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Tibetan Buddhist teaching adapted as a psychological practice |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, conflict, trauma healing, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | enactment role, talk, art image, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://tricycle.org/magazine/feeding-your-demons/; https://www.taramandala.org/feeding-your-demons-online-program/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsultrim_Allione |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *demon* | A fear, craving, illness, or conflict, personified so that it can be met rather than fought. |
| *feeding* | Imaginatively offering the demon, as nectar made from one's own dissolved body, exactly what it needs. |
| *ally* | The helpful figure the satisfied demon turns into. |
| *Chöd* | The Tibetan practice of offering one's body, taught by Machig Labdrön, which inspired the method; Tara Mandala states that the method itself is not a Chöd practice. |

#### Inner child work (reparenting)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Popularized in the recovery movement by Charles L. Whitfield and John Bradshaw; roots in Jung's divine child archetype |
| Date | 1980s–1990s (Whitfield 1987; Bradshaw 1990) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person imagines or addresses the child they once were, acknowledges that child's unmet needs and feelings, and learns to give that child the care and support that may have been missing in childhood. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from an adult giving their own childhood self the recognition and care it did not receive. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics note that the concept is hard to test scientifically and that most studies rely on small samples and self-report. |
| Mental model | other. The recovery movement for adult children of dysfunctional families |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, emotion, trauma healing, identity self |
| Medium | writing, talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_child |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inner child* | The childhood self, with its unresolved experiences and needs, as it continues in the adult. |
| *child within* | Whitfield's name for the inner child in Healing the Child Within (1987). |
| *reparenting* | Learning to acknowledge and care for one's inner child, offering the support that was missing in childhood. |

#### Inner Relationship Focusing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ann Weiser Cornell and Barbara McGavin, developing Eugene Gendlin's Focusing |
| Date | 1990s |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, two people as equals |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Instead of first clearing a space, the person scans the body for what feels alive, acknowledges each inner experience as a part rather than as the whole self, and stays with it in a calm, friendly, curious way, letting it show how things are from its own point of view. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a gentle, accepting relationship between the Self and all its inner parts, including rejected and conflicting ones. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, identity self, attention awareness |
| Medium | breath body, talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Relationship_Focusing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Self-in-Presence* | Gentle listening, with equanimity, to everything that comes up in the Focusing process. |
| *Presence* | The ability of the Self to be with inner aspects in a friendly, gently curious, and nonjudging way. |
| *from its point of view* | The method's emphasis on finding out how a specific part or felt experience feels to itself. |

#### Internal Family Systems (IFS)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Richard C. Schwartz |
| Date | early 1980s |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The client turns attention inward, finds a part (a feeling, voice, or impulse), and asks it to step back until they can relate to it from Self, a state of calm curiosity and compassion. From Self they learn what the protective parts are guarding against and then attend to the vulnerable parts those protectors keep away. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a person relates to their parts from Self, since parts are not bad but have been forced into extreme roles, and they change once it feels safe. |
| Limits and critiques | The vault's evaluation records that its research base is small relative to its influence, with few randomized trials, and that careless parts work can destabilize people with complex trauma. |
| Mental model | systemic and family |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, trauma healing, memory past |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/internal family systems.md` |
| Sources | https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/evolution-internal-family-systems-model-dr-richard-schwartz-ph-d |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Self* | The core of every person, marked by qualities such as calmness, curiosity, clarity, compassion, confidence, creativity, courage, and connectedness (the 'eight Cs'). |
| *managers* | Protective parts that try to keep control of the inner and outer world so that pain does not surface. |
| *firefighters* | Protective parts that act quickly to shut down painful feelings once they have been triggered. |
| *exiles* | Vulnerable parts that carry painful emotions and memories and are kept out of awareness. |

#### Multiple selves exercise (compassion-focused therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Paul Gilbert (compassion-focused therapy); the exercise is also described by Russell Kolts |
| Date | 2000s–2010s (described in Kolts 2016 and Gilbert 2020) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The client takes a recent dispute with someone they care about and explores it from the angry self, the anxious self, and the sad self in turn, often each in its own chair, asking what that self thinks, feels in the body, remembers, and wants. The client then settles into the compassionate self, supported by slow soothing breathing, and from there considers each emotional self and the situation. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from telling apart conflicting threat emotions and relating to all of them from a compassionate motivation rather than from any one of them. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. An integrative model grounded in affective neuroscience, attachment theory, and evolutionary science |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, conflict, identity self |
| Medium | talk, enactment role, breath body |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://contextualconsulting.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/multiple-emotions-multiple-selves-compassion-focused-therapy-chairwork.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *multiple selves* | The exercise's view that threat emotions such as anger, anxiety, and sadness each act like a small self with its own thoughts, focus, bodily feel, and aims. |
| *compassionate self* | A cultivated state and identity, motivated to understand and relieve suffering, from which the other selves are addressed. |
| *soothing-rhythm breathing* | Slowed breathing used to help the client reach the compassionate self. |

#### Parts Party

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Virginia Satir |
| Date | 1970s |
| Scale | one person and their parts, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The host names several of their inner parts, often using the names of public figures or fictional characters, and group members play them as guests at a party. The guests meet, clash as each tries to dominate, then make contact and change, and in a closing ritual each guest tells the host what skill it gives them. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing one's inner parts played out by others, letting their conflicts unfold and change, and then taking back each part's resource. |
| Limits and critiques | The group form needs many participants, about twelve at minimum by one account, so solo and couple adaptations have been developed. |
| Mental model | systemic and family |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, conflict, creativity play |
| Medium | enactment role, talk, movement dance |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Team; https://www.coaching-institutes.net/sources/material/parts-party-from-virginia-satir-english-version; https://dspace.library.uvic.ca/items/39aff5e5-c3f7-4916-a773-9e90678fa5f1 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *host* | The person whose inner parts are being explored, who watches them and finally integrates them. |
| *party guests* | Group members who each play one of the host's parts. |
| *integration ritual* | The closing step in which each part states the skill it gives to the host. |

#### Schema mode work (schema therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jeffrey Young and colleagues |
| Date | 1990s–2003 (mode concept 1996; Schema Therapy: A Practitioner's Guide, 2003) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Therapist and client identify which modes take over the client in difficult moments, for example a vulnerable child, a detached protector, or a punitive parent, and work with them directly through dialogues between modes, imagery of childhood scenes, and a therapeutic relationship that partly meets unmet childhood needs, so that the client's healthy adult mode can take charge. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recognizing which mode is active and strengthening a healthy adult mode that can care for the child modes and limit the critical and avoidant ones. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. An integrative extension of cognitive therapy |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, memory past, trauma healing |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2021.763670/full |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *schema mode* | A moment-to-moment emotional state that temporarily dominates a person's thinking, feeling, and behavior. |
| *child modes* | Modes such as the vulnerable or angry child, working models based on childhood experience. |
| *parent modes* | Internalized critical or demanding parental attitudes, such as the punitive parent. |
| *healthy adult* | The mature, regulated mode that the therapy aims to strengthen so that it can meet the person's needs. |

#### Subpersonality work and disidentification (psychosynthesis)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Roberto Assagioli |
| Date | 1910s–1960s (A New Method of Healing: Psychosynthesis, 1927; Psychosynthesis: A Manual of Principles and Techniques, 1965) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The person identifies the subpersonalities they act from in different settings, such as at work, at home, or in solitude, learns what each wants, and practises disidentifying from them. In the self-identification exercise they affirm, for example, 'I have a body, but I am not my body; I have emotions, but I am not my emotions', to find the 'I' as a center of awareness and will. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recognizing that one has, but is not, one's roles and contents, and from acting from a center of awareness and will that can coordinate them. |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, art image, silence |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychosynthesis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *subpersonality* | A semi-autonomous pattern of behavior, feeling, and thought that a person shifts into in different roles and settings. |
| *disidentification* | Stepping back from a sensation, emotion, thought, or subpersonality so that one observes it instead of being it. |
| *'I' (personal self)* | The center of awareness and will that stays stable while psychological contents change. |
| *Higher Self* | Assagioli's name for a permanent center of the self beyond the conscious 'I'. |

#### Two-chair and empty-chair tasks in emotion-focused therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Leslie S. Greenberg, Laura N. Rice, and Robert Elliott (process-experiential, later emotion-focused, therapy) |
| Date | late 1980s–1990s (Facilitating Emotional Change, 1993) |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | When the client shows a specific marker, the therapist proposes a task: for harsh self-criticism, a dialogue between the critic and the criticized self in two chairs; for lingering unresolved feelings toward a significant other, speech to that person imagined in an empty chair. The work aims to reduce self-criticism, shame, and helplessness and to increase self-compassion and self-protection. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from arousing and reorganizing emotion in the session by bringing two parts of the self, or the self and an imagined other, into live contact. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented. The person-centred experiential tradition (late Rogers and Gendlin), integrating Gestalt methods |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self, relationship absent, conflict |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://strathprints.strath.ac.uk/61216/1/Elliott_Greenberg_McGraw_Hill_OUP_2017_Emotion_focused_therapy.pdf; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9564898/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *marker* | A recognizable in-session statement or behavior that signals which therapeutic task fits at that moment. |
| *self-critical split* | A conflict between a critical voice and the part of the self it criticizes, worked with in two-chair dialogue. |
| *unfinished business* | Lingering unresolved feelings toward a significant other, worked with in empty-chair dialogue. |
| *self-interruption* | A split in which one part of the self blocks or shuts down another part's feeling. |

#### Voice Dialogue

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Hal Stone and Sidra Stone |
| Date | early 1970s |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The facilitator asks to speak directly with one of the person's selves, such as the inner critic or the responsible self, and the person lets that self speak in its own voice. Afterward the person, speaking again as themselves, notices the difference between being identified with that self and being aware of it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from no longer being controlled by a few dominant selves and from developing an aware ego that can keep opposite selves in awareness and choose among them. |
| Mental model | other. The Stones' Psychology of Selves: the personality as many selves, with an aware ego developing among them |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.voicedialogue.com/what-is-voice-dialogue/; https://www.voicedialogueinternational.com/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *primary selves* | The selves a person identifies with, which hold their rules, values, and personal history and together feel like 'me'. |
| *disowned selves* | Selves pushed into the shadow, which the person judges or barely knows. |
| *Aware Ego* | The capacity, developed through the work, to recognize identification with a self and unhook from it while staying in contact with other selves, which gives real choice. |

### A text or tool with no one present

This scale is the main one for 13 techniques, and the vault has met 4 of them.

#### Bibliotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Term coined by Samuel McChord Crothers; practised by librarians, clinicians, and teachers |
| Date | 1916 (the term); ancient precedents |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A reader works through books chosen to meet a need: self-help books based on a therapy, fiction and poetry in which they can recognize their own situation, or titles recommended by a librarian, teacher, or clinician. In structured forms a professional selects the material and discusses it with the reader. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from what a well-chosen text gives a reader: information, a character or model to identify with, or words for their own situation. |
| Limits and critiques | Evidence is strongest for cognitive self-help books for mild to moderate depression; non-clinical uses are less studied, and some readers are not ready to face what a book brings up. |
| Mental model | other. Several models: cognitive self-help books, fiction for emotional identification, and preventive developmental reading |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, meaning purpose, learning skill |
| Medium | reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliotherapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bibliotherapy* | The use of reading to help with emotional or psychological difficulties, a word Crothers introduced in 1916. |
| *clinical bibliotherapy* | Bibliotherapy delivered by qualified professionals as part of treatment. |
| *developmental bibliotherapy* | Preventive use of reading, often by teachers or parents, before problems arise. |
| *psychēs iatreion* | 'Healing place for the soul': the inscription Diodorus Siculus reports over the library of Ramses II. |

#### Cognitive bibliotherapy (therapy-based self-help books)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Cognitive therapy (Aaron T. Beck); best-known text by David D. Burns, Feeling Good |
| Date | 1980 (Feeling Good) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The reader works through a book that teaches cognitive therapy step by step, learning to identify common distortions in their thinking and to correct them through the book's written exercises, in place of sessions with a therapist. |
| What it assumes | Moods follow from thoughts, so change comes from learning to identify and correct distorted thinking, which a book can teach. |
| Limits and critiques | Studies summarized in the encyclopedia article on cognitive behavioral therapy found reading-based self-help significant only when it was guided by a professional, and one study found a negative effect in people prone to rumination. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, learning skill |
| Medium | reading text, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeling_Good:_The_New_Mood_Therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_behavioral_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliotherapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *cognitive distortions* | Habitual errors in thinking that the reader learns to spot and correct. |
| *guided self-help* | Self-help material used with brief support from a professional. |

#### Computerized and internet-delivered CBT programs

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Cognitive behavioural therapy researchers and health services; programs such as Beating the Blues and MoodGYM |
| Date | 2000s (recommended by NICE for England and Wales in 2006) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person works through a series of online modules that teach cognitive behavioural techniques, completing exercises and homework on a computer or phone, either entirely alone or with brief messages from a therapist. |
| What it assumes | The active ingredients of cognitive behavioural therapy can be taught by a program, so that a person learns the skills without a live therapist. |
| Limits and critiques | NICE's 2009 guideline withdrew its endorsement of specific products, and one study found MoodGYM no better than informational websites. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, learning skill |
| Medium | technology, reading text, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the alliance holds its size when the exchange is mediated and written.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_behavioral_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *CCBT* | Computerised cognitive behavioural therapy: CBT delivered through a program rather than by a therapist. |
| *guided versus unguided* | Whether a professional supports the program with brief contact, or the person uses it entirely alone. |

#### Consulting the I Ching (Yijing)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Zhou dynasty China (the Zhou yi), with later commentaries (the Ten Wings) |
| Date | core text compiled in the late 9th century BCE (Edward Shaughnessy's dating) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person keeps a question in mind, produces a six-line figure (a hexagram) by a chance procedure, traditionally by dividing fifty yarrow stalks and later by tossing coins, and reads the text attached to that hexagram as a response to consider. |
| What it assumes | Insight comes from reading one's situation through a text chosen by a chance procedure rather than by deliberate thought. |
| Mental model | other. Divination; in modern Western use often treated as a prompt for reflection |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, future vision, meaning purpose |
| Medium | reading text, ritual object |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Ching |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hexagram (guà)* | A figure of six stacked lines, each whole or broken; the book has a text for each of the 64 possible figures. |
| *Ten Wings* | A set of ten commentaries, much later than the core text, attached to it. |
| *yarrow stalks* | The traditional means of producing a hexagram, by numerical operations on fifty stalks; coins came into wide use in the Tang dynasty. |

#### Conversational programs for reflection (from ELIZA to CBT chatbots)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Joseph Weizenbaum (ELIZA, MIT); later CBT-based chatbots such as Woebot (Alison Darcy and colleagues) |
| Date | 1964–1967 (ELIZA); 2017 (first Woebot trial) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The person types messages to a program that answers in conversation. ELIZA's DOCTOR script reflected the user's own words back as questions in the manner of a person-centred therapist, while recent programs such as Woebot hold brief daily conversations, track mood, and teach cognitive behavioural techniques. |
| What it assumes | Some of what helps in reflective conversation, such as having one's words taken up and questioned or being led through structured exercises, can be supplied by a program. |
| Limits and critiques | Weizenbaum warned in Computer Power and Human Reason (1976) against treating computers as capable of human judgment, and the first Woebot trial was small (70 students, two weeks, no follow-up). |
| Mental model | other. ELIZA imitated a person-centred therapist's reflections; recent programs deliver cognitive behavioural exercises |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, thought belief |
| Medium | technology, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ELIZA; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5478797/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *DOCTOR script* | ELIZA's best-known script, which imitated a Rogerian psychotherapist by reflecting the patient's words back. |
| *ELIZA effect* | The tendency of users to attribute human understanding and feeling to a program that has none. |
| *conversational agent* | A program that interacts with a person through dialogue, used here to deliver therapy exercises. |

#### Fourth-step moral inventory (Alcoholics Anonymous)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Alcoholics Anonymous (Bill Wilson and early members) |
| Date | 1939 (the Big Book) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone, two people as equals |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Following the Big Book's instructions, the person lists the people, institutions, and principles they resent, writes beside each name the cause and what it threatened (self-esteem, security, ambitions, personal or sexual relations), and then reviews their fears and sexual conduct. In the fifth step they admit what they found to God, to themselves, and to another person, usually their sponsor. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing one's own part in resentments and fears in writing and then admitting it to God, oneself, and another person. |
| Mental model | other. Twelve-step spirituality of surrender, moral inventory, and admission to another person |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, relationship absent, emotion, conflict |
| Medium | writing, reading text, talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.aa.org/sites/default/files/2021-11/en_bigbook_chapt5.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-step_program |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *searching and fearless moral inventory* | The fourth step's own wording for a written review of one's resentments, fears, and conduct. |
| *grudge list* | The Big Book's name for the list of those one resents, with each injury written opposite the name. |
| *sponsor* | An experienced member who usually hears the inventory in the fifth step. |

#### Guided meditation recordings

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Many meditation teachers; early documented uses include Mahasi Sayadaw's tape-recorded introductory talk and the guided audio used for home practice in Jon Kabat-Zinn's Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction |
| Date | 1951 (Mahasi's recorded talk); 1979 onward (MBSR) |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The listener follows a recorded voice that leads a practice step by step, for example a body scan that moves attention from the toes to the top of the head. In MBSR, participants use such recordings for 45 to 60 minutes of home practice six days a week between group sessions. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from repeated practice, which a recording makes possible without a teacher present by giving the instructions in real time. |
| Limits and critiques | A recording cannot see or respond to the listener, so it cannot adjust to difficulties that arise; at Mahasi's centre and in MBSR it supplemented live teaching rather than replacing it. |
| Mental model | other. A delivery format used across contemplative and clinical traditions; its best-studied clinical use is secular mindfulness |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, body, emotion |
| Medium | technology, voice music, breath body, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the completion family (hudson's art of accomplishment, mclaren's language of emotions).md |
| Sources | https://mahajana.net/en/texts/mahasit1.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindfulness-based_stress_reduction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *body scan* | A guided practice of moving attention systematically through the body, region by region. |
| *home practice* | Daily practice done alone between group sessions, supported by recordings. |

#### Letters to a Young Poet (Rilke)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Rainer Maria Rilke, writing to Franz Xaver Kappus |
| Date | letters 1903–1908 (Rilke's replies); published 1929 |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Asked by a young military cadet to judge his poems, Rilke declines to criticize them and instead tells him that nobody can advise or help him and that he should go into himself, value solitude, and live with his questions. Later readers take the ten letters as counsel addressed to their own uncertainties. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the reader's own inward work and patience, which a letter can call for but cannot do for them. |
| Mental model | existential and phenomenological. Literary counsel rather than a school of practice |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, identity self, meaning purpose |
| Medium | writing, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/rainer maria rilke.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_to_a_Young_Poet |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Go into yourself* | Rilke's answer, in the first letter, to the request for advice. |
| *live the questions* | The counsel, in the fourth letter, to live with unresolved questions instead of forcing answers. |

#### Moral letters (Seneca's Epistulae Morales ad Lucilium)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Seneca the Younger, in the tradition of philosophical letters of guidance such as the Epicureans' |
| Date | c. 65 CE |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Seneca writes letters to one friend, Lucilius, advising him how to become a more committed Stoic, and the early letters end with a maxim, often from Epicurus, for him to meditate on. Written as open letters for a wider readership, they are read by later readers as a course of guidance addressed as if to them. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from steady, personal counsel repeated over time, which a letter can carry to a reader who is far away. |
| Mental model | other. Stoic philosophy practised as a way of life |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, death finitude, thought belief |
| Medium | writing, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the book's native genre is the letter from a friend read alone.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistulae_Morales_ad_Lucilium; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_direction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Epistulae Morales ad Lucilium* | 'Moral Letters to Lucilius': 124 surviving letters, addressed to one person but written for many. |
| *maxim* | A short saying, often taken from Epicurus, placed at the end of the early letters for the reader to meditate on. |

#### Programmed instruction

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Sidney Pressey (self-scoring teaching machine); B. F. Skinner (linear programs); Norman Crowder (branching programs) |
| Date | 1926 (Pressey's machine); Skinner's and Crowder's programs in the mid-20th century |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The learner works alone through material divided into small steps, answering a question at each step and learning at once whether the answer was right before going on. In Skinner's linear programs every learner follows the same sequence; in Crowder's branching programs each multiple-choice answer leads to its own feedback. |
| What it assumes | Learning is shaped by active responding and immediate feedback, which a well-sequenced program can supply without a teacher. |
| Limits and critiques | Educators doubt how far a list of observable behaviours captures what they are trying to teach, which limits the method outside well-defined skills. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill |
| Medium | reading text, technology |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programmed_learning |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *linear programming* | Skinner's form, in which every learner goes through the same small steps in the same order. |
| *branching programming* | Crowder's form, in which each multiple-choice answer leads to its own feedback. |
| *knowledge of results* | Immediate confirmation of whether each answer was right. |

#### Sayings of the Desert Fathers (Apophthegmata Patrum)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Desert Fathers and Mothers of Egypt; collected by later monks |
| Date | 4th–5th centuries CE |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A disciple asks an elder, 'Give me a word, abba', and receives a short saying or brief story to live by rather than a general teaching. The recorded exchanges were gathered into collections, arranged by the elder's name or by theme, which later monks read as guidance. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a single saying given for one person's need and then lived out over time, rather than from general instruction. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, learning skill, identity self |
| Medium | reading text, talk |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayings_of_the_Desert_Fathers |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *abba* | Father: the title given to a desert elder whose counsel is sought. |
| *give me a word* | The standard request a disciple makes of an elder, asking for a short saying to live by. |
| *apophthegm* | A short saying; the Greek collections are called apophthegmata. |

Letters attributed to Jean-Pierre de Caussade, written for Visitation nuns at Nancy, were compiled into the book published in 1861 as Abandonment to Divine Providence. |

#### The Artist's Way (a course in a book)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Julia Cameron |
| Date | 1992 |
| Scale | a text or tool, no one present, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | The reader follows a 12-week program of weekly readings, creative exercises, and daily assignments, with two standing practices: daily Morning Pages and a weekly solitary Artist Date. The book encourages readers to work through it in a group, and many self-run groups have formed. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from sustained daily and weekly creative practices that increase a person's confidence in their own creative ability. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts |
