### Two people, one helping

This scale is the main one for 47 techniques, and the vault has met 19 of them.

#### Accelerated Experiential Dynamic Psychotherapy (AEDP)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Diana Fosha |
| Date | 2000 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist actively offers safety and emotional engagement so that the client does not face overwhelming feelings alone, and helps the client keep attending to core emotions until they are processed. After a change occurs, the therapist asks the client to reflect on the experience of changing itself. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from processing emotion to completion within a secure relationship and then reflecting on the change itself. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. An experiential, attachment-based form of dynamic psychotherapy |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, trauma healing |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://aedpinstitute.org/about-aedp/; https://aedpinstitute.org/journal/transformance-and-the-phenomenology-of-transformation/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *undoing aloneness* | The therapist's active, engaged presence, which ends the client's experience of facing overwhelming emotion alone. |
| *transformance* | Fosha's name for the motivational force toward healing and positive change present in every person. |
| *metaprocessing* | Reflecting on the experience of transformation itself, which the model treats as the third state. |
| *core state* | The fourth state of the model, marked by clarity, ease, vitality, and a wider perspective. |

#### Acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Steven C. Hayes, with Kirk Strosahl and Kelly Wilson |
| Date | 1980s–1999 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist uses exercises and metaphors to help the client notice thoughts as thoughts rather than facts, make room for painful feelings instead of fighting them, and clarify what they value. The client then commits to specific actions guided by those values. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from accepting inner experience and acting on chosen values, rather than from reducing or controlling symptoms. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics have argued that the promotion of ACT ran ahead of its evidence and that its distinctive components have not been shown to be the active ingredients of its effects. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, meaning purpose, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://contextualscience.org/the_six_core_processes_of_act; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acceptance_and_commitment_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *psychological flexibility* | The ability to be in contact with the present moment and to change or keep up a behaviour when doing so serves chosen values. |
| *cognitive defusion* | Changing how one relates to a thought so that it has less control, without trying to change what the thought says. |
| *experiential avoidance* | Attempts to avoid unwanted thoughts, feelings, or memories, which ACT treats as a main source of suffering. |
| *self as context* | The observing sense of self that notices experiences without being defined by them. |
| *committed action* | Setting goals according to one's values and carrying them out. |

#### Befriending (Samaritans emotional support)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Chad Varah (Samaritans) |
| Date | 1953 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | A trained volunteer listens to a person in distress, often by telephone, without judging them or giving advice, and keeps what is said confidential. The volunteer lets the caller explore their feelings, including thoughts of suicide, at their own pace. |
| What it assumes | A person in despair is helped by being listened to, without judgment, by another human being. |
| Limits and critiques | The founder later objected that the organization had shifted from an emergency service for suicidal people toward general emotional support. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. Lay emotional support by trained volunteers rather than therapy |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, death finitude |
| Medium | talk, technology |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans_(charity) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *befriending* | Samaritans' name for offering emotional support by listening to anyone in distress. |
| *confidentiality* | The rule that what callers say stays private except in exceptional circumstances involving safety. |
| *emotional support* | Listening support for a person's feelings, as distinct from advice or treatment. |

#### Clean Language and Symbolic Modelling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Grove; developed further by Penny Tompkins and James Lawley |
| Date | 1980s (Symbolic Modelling, 2000) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | The facilitator repeats the client's exact words and asks a small set of fixed questions about them, such as what kind of thing a word refers to, where it is, or whether there is anything else about it. The facilitator adds no metaphors, interpretations, or suggestions of their own, so the client develops and explores their own metaphors. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's own metaphors developing and reorganizing when they are explored without outside content. |
| Mental model | other. Metaphor-centred questioning; Symbolic Modelling adds a systems view of the client's metaphors |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, identity self, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/how much structure makes recognition possible without determining what is recognized.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_language; https://cleanlanguage.com/what-is-clean-language/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clean question* | One of a small set of fixed questions built only from the client's own words, for example asking what kind of X it is or whether there is anything else about X. |
| *clean* | Not adding the facilitator's own words, assumptions, advice, or judgements to what the client says. |
| *metaphorical landscape* | The set of symbols and relationships that a client's metaphors form. |
| *Symbolic Modelling* | Lawley and Tompkins's method of using clean questions to help a client model their own metaphors and patterns. |

#### Clinical supervision (with the seven-eyed model)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | A professional practice shared across therapeutic schools; the seven-eyed model is by Peter Hawkins and Robin Shohet |
| Date | 20th century; seven-eyed model 1989 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A practitioner meets regularly with another, usually more experienced, professional to reflect on their casework, their reactions to clients, and professional issues. The seven-eyed model directs attention in turn to the client, the practitioner's interventions, the practitioner-client relationship, the practitioner's own process, the supervisory relationship, the supervisor's own process, and the wider context. |
| What it assumes | Practice improves, and clients are protected, when practitioners reflect regularly on their work with another professional. |
| Mental model | other. A professional practice used across therapeutic schools |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, relationship present, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_supervision |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *seven-eyed model* | Hawkins and Shohet's map of seven areas a supervisor can attend to, from the client to the wider context. |
| *parallel process* | The appearance, within the supervision relationship, of dynamics from the practitioner's relationship with the client. |
| *supervisee* | The practitioner who brings their work to supervision. |

#### Co-Active coaching

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Laura Whitworth, Henry Kimsey-House, Karen Kimsey-House, and Phillip Sandahl (Coaches Training Institute, now the Co-Active Training Institute) |
| Date | 1990s (institute founded 1992) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Coach and client first design their working relationship together, and the client sets the agenda. The coach listens closely, asks open questions, and offers intuitions or challenges that the client may take or leave, balancing forward action with deeper learning. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's own resourcefulness, drawn out within a relationship the client helps design and in which the client sets the agenda. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, decision choice, future vision, identity self |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.crowe-associates.co.uk/coaching-and-mentoring-skills/the-co-active-coaching-model/; https://coactive.com/about/; https://www.competence.org/inspiration/what-is-co-active-coaching/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *naturally creative, resourceful and whole* | The first cornerstone: the belief that the client has, or can find, their own answers. |
| *designed alliance* | The working agreement coach and client create together, which the model's authors describe as the container in which the coaching takes place. |
| *dance in the moment* | Responding to whatever arises in the conversation instead of following a set plan. |
| *forward the action and deepen the learning* | The coach's two aims: helping the client act, and helping the client learn from acting. |
| *self-management* | The coach's handling of their own opinions and reactions so that they stay with the client's agenda. |

#### Cognitive therapy (cognitive behavioural therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Aaron T. Beck |
| Date | 1960s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Therapist and client identify the automatic thoughts that come before a painful feeling and then examine the evidence for and against them together, using questions and small behavioural experiments. The client practises these skills between sessions, with the aim of becoming their own therapist. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from identifying and revising the thoughts and beliefs that shape how a person feels and acts. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion, learning skill |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_therapy; https://beckinstitute.org/about/understanding-cbt/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *automatic thoughts* | Quick, often negative thoughts that arise in a situation and shape how the person feels. |
| *collaborative empiricism* | Therapist and client act as joint investigators who test the client's beliefs against evidence. |
| *cognitive distortions* | Habitual errors in thinking, such as overgeneralizing or magnifying, that make distress worse. |
| *Socratic questioning* | A series of questions the therapist asks to help the client examine the meaning and accuracy of a thought. |
| *core beliefs* | Deep, general beliefs about the self, the world, and the future that lie beneath automatic thoughts. |

#### Compassionate Inquiry

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Gabor Maté, with Sat Dharam Kaur |
| Date | 2010s (first training workshop 2016) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The practitioner asks questions while noticing the client's body states, tone, and words, to bring out the unconscious story and beliefs beneath the client's present difficulty. The practitioner helps the client see where those beliefs came from in childhood experience and consider letting them go. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing the unconscious beliefs formed in response to early trauma and feeling the emotions that were suppressed. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Trauma-informed; attends to body states and implicit memory |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | trauma healing, thought belief, memory past, body |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://compassionateinquiry.com/; https://compassionateinquiry.com/the-approach/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *core stories* | The unconscious stories a person tells themselves about who they are and how the world is. |
| *compassion and curiosity* | The attitude the client is helped to take toward their own history and ways of coping. |
| *implicit memories* | Memories held without conscious recall, often in body states, that shape present reactions. |
| *coping mechanisms* | Patterns such as addictions and anxieties, which the approach treats as adaptations to early pain rather than as the person's identity. |

#### Dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT), individual therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Marsha M. Linehan |
| Date | late 1970s–1993 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist balances two kinds of response: validating the client's feelings and behaviour as understandable, and pushing for change by analysing the chain of events that led to a problem behaviour and teaching new skills. The client records emotions and behaviours on a diary card, which sets what each session addresses. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a balance of acceptance and change strategies, within which the client learns skills to regulate emotion and tolerate distress. |
| Limits and critiques | Full DBT needs a large time commitment, and some clinicians argue that it leaves core trauma symptoms such as shame, guilt, and negative beliefs about the self unaddressed. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, learning skill, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialectical_behavior_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dialectics* | Holding opposites together, above all accepting the person as they are while pushing for change. |
| *validation* | The therapist's communication that the client's feelings and behaviour make sense in their context. |
| *chain analysis* | A step-by-step review of the events, thoughts, and feelings that led to a problem behaviour. |
| *diary card* | A daily record of emotions, urges, and behaviours that the client fills in and brings to sessions. |
| *distress tolerance* | Skills for bearing intense emotional pain without acting in harmful ways. |

#### Disclosure of thoughts to an elder (Orthodox eldership)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Egyptian desert monasticism (the Desert Fathers) and Orthodox Christian eldership |
| Date | 4th century onward |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The disciple brings their thoughts, including temptations, to an experienced elder and asks about them instead of struggling with them alone. The elder responds with insight into the disciple's situation and guides by companionship and example as well as by words. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from bringing one's inner life into the open before a spiritual father who can discern it, and from obedience to his guidance. |
| Limits and critiques | The practice asks the disciple to surrender their will to the elder, so its safety depends entirely on the elder's integrity. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.pravmir.com/the-spiritual-guide-in-orthodox-christianity-part-i/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *logismoi* | Thoughts, especially the recurring thoughts and temptations that a monk struggles with. |
| *starets* | The Russian word for an elder or spiritual father; the Greek word is geron, and the desert monks said abba. |
| *obedience* | The disciple's entrusting of their will to the spiritual father, which the tradition treats as freeing. |

#### Dokusan (sanzen), the private interview with a Zen teacher

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Japanese Zen (Rinzai and Soto schools) |
| Date | traditional, undated |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The student enters the teacher's room with prescribed bows and, in Rinzai practice, presents their understanding of an assigned koan. The teacher tests the response, for example with a checking question, and either accepts it or rings a bell to dismiss the student, which means the work on the koan must continue. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the student's own direct seeing, which the teacher tests and confirms in person rather than explains. |
| Limits and critiques | Because one teacher confirms or rejects the student's insight, the practice depends heavily on that teacher's integrity and skill. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, ritual object, silence |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | `notes/zen's makyo instruction refuses the self-certifying experience.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanzen; https://redcedarzen.org/dokusan |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dokusan* | Literally 'going alone': the student's one-to-one meeting with the teacher. |
| *sanzen* | Going to a Zen master for instruction; in the Rinzai school it means the same as dokusan. |
| *koan* | A teaching question or story that the student works on and presents in the interview. |
| *checking question* | A follow-up question with which the teacher probes how deep a student's insight goes. |

#### Emotion-focused therapy (process-experiential therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Leslie Greenberg, Laura Rice, and Robert Elliott |
| Date | late 1980s–1990s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person and their parts |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist attends closely to the client's emotional experience moment by moment and watches for markers, statements in the session that show the client is ready to work on a particular emotional task. When a marker appears, the therapist offers a task, such as a dialogue between a critical part and a criticized part of the self in two chairs, or a conversation with an imagined significant other in an empty chair. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from accessing, experiencing, and transforming painful emotional experience within an empathic relationship. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship absent, identity self |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://strathprints.strath.ac.uk/61216/1/Elliott_Greenberg_McGraw_Hill_OUP_2017_Emotion_focused_therapy.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *marker* | A client statement or action in the session that shows readiness to work on a particular emotional issue. |
| *task* | A structured piece of therapeutic work the therapist offers in response to a marker. |
| *two-chair work* | An enacted conversation between two parts of the self, such as an inner critic and the part that feels criticized. |
| *emotion schemes* | Organized networks of memories, bodily sensations, meanings, and action tendencies that produce emotional experience. |
| *unfinished business* | Lingering unresolved feelings toward a significant other, worked with through empty-chair dialogue. |

#### Ericksonian hypnotherapy (the utilization approach)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Milton H. Erickson |
| Date | 1950s–1970s |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist uses the client's own language, interests, and even resistance as the means of guiding them into trance, and gives suggestions indirectly through stories, metaphors, and deliberately open wording. The client's unconscious is expected to take from the suggestions whatever fits. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's unconscious resources, which the therapist evokes and directs through trance and indirect suggestion. |
| Limits and critiques | Colleagues questioned the reliability of Erickson's case reports, and indirect suggestion is designed to work partly outside the client's conscious evaluation. |
| Mental model | other. Clinical hypnosis; it later shaped strategic, brief, and solution-focused therapies and neuro-linguistic programming |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, thought belief |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_H._Erickson |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *utilization* | Using whatever the client brings, including their behaviour, beliefs, and resistance, as the vehicle for change. |
| *indirect suggestion* | Suggestion given through implication, story, or open wording rather than direct command. |
| *confusion technique* | Deliberately complex or ambiguous language that overloads conscious attention and makes the listener more receptive to suggestion. |
| *handshake induction* | A trance induction that interrupts an ordinary handshake to capture attention. |

#### Existential psychotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ludwig Binswanger and Medard Boss (Daseinsanalysis); Rollo May; James Bugental; Irvin Yalom; Emmy van Deurzen |
| Date | 1940s–1980s |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Therapist and client explore the client's lived experience together, with attention to the relationship between them in the session itself. The therapist helps the client face basic conditions of existence, such as death and freedom, instead of relying on techniques or diagnoses. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a person faces the given conditions of existence directly and takes responsibility for how they live within them. |
| Mental model | existential and phenomenological |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | death finitude, meaning purpose, decision choice, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existential_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ultimate concerns* | Yalom's four basic conditions of human existence: death, freedom and responsibility, isolation, and meaninglessness. |
| *existential isolation* | Yalom's name for the gap between oneself and any other being, which no relationship removes. |
| *presence* | The therapist's full and authentic attention to the client in the session. |
| *Daseinsanalysis* | The Swiss form of existential analysis that Binswanger and Boss built on Heidegger's philosophy. |

#### Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing (EMDR)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Francine Shapiro |
| Date | 1987–1989 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | After history-taking and preparation, the client keeps a target memory in mind while following the therapist's hand with their eyes, or receiving taps or tones, in short sets. After each set the therapist asks what the client notices and follows the new material until the memory is no longer distressing. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from reprocessing stored traumatic memories until they are integrated and no longer distressing. |
| Limits and critiques | The founding institute stresses that the therapist must first make sure the client can handle emotional distress, because processing can bring up intense material. |
| Mental model | other. Shapiro's adaptive information processing model of memory |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | trauma healing, memory past, emotion |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.emdr.com/what-is-emdr/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bilateral stimulation* | Alternating left and right stimulation, usually eye movements directed by the therapist, sometimes hand taps or tones. |
| *adaptive information processing* | Shapiro's model that distressing memories are stored unprocessed and can be brought to an adaptive resolution. |
| *eight phases* | The structured protocol, running from history-taking and preparation through processing, closure, and re-evaluation. |
| *target* | The specific memory or situation chosen for processing. |

#### Focusing-oriented psychotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Eugene Gendlin |
| Date | 1960s–1996 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The therapist listens and reflects, and helps the client turn attention to the unclear bodily sense of a situation that lies beneath their words. The client checks possible words or images against that sense until something fits and the sense shifts. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's direct attention to the felt sense, which moves forward when it is accurately symbolized. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, silence, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/eugene gendlin.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focusing_(psychotherapy) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *felt sense* | An unclear, bodily sensed knowing about a situation, which is always more than any words for it. |
| *felt shift* | The bodily release and fresh understanding that comes when the felt sense is accurately put into words. |
| *handle* | A word, phrase, or image that fits the felt sense. |
| *resonating* | Checking a handle back against the felt sense to see whether it fits. |

#### Free association and interpretation (psychoanalysis)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Sigmund Freud |
| Date | 1890s–1912 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The patient says whatever comes to mind without selecting or censoring it, traditionally lying on a couch, while the analyst listens with evenly suspended attention. The analyst then offers interpretations that connect what was said to hidden wishes, memories, and feelings about the analyst. |
| What it assumes | Change happens when unconscious material reaches awareness through the patient's unselected speech and the analyst's interpretation of it. |
| Limits and critiques | Rogers argued in 1957 that free association and interpretation have no essential value in themselves and help only as channels for the relationship conditions he named. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, emotion, identity self |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | archive/reading — rogers, the necessary and sufficient conditions (1957).md |
| Sources | https://www.encyclopedia.com/psychology/dictionaries-thesauruses-pictures-and-press-releases/fundamental-rule |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *fundamental rule* | The instruction that the patient say whatever comes into their head, even if it seems unimportant, nonsensical, or embarrassing. |
| *free association* | The patient's unselected flow of speech, used to reach meanings the patient is not aware of. |
| *evenly suspended attention* | The analyst's way of listening, which gives equal attention to everything said instead of selecting in advance. |
| *interpretation* | The analyst's statement of a hidden meaning in what the patient has said or done. |

#### Gestalt therapy (in session)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Fritz Perls, Laura Perls, and Paul Goodman, with Ralph Hefferline |
| Date | 1940s–1951 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist draws the client's attention to what they are doing and feeling right now, including gesture, voice, and breathing, and asks how rather than why. The therapist proposes small experiments, such as speaking to an empty chair or exaggerating a gesture, so that the client experiences directly what they have been avoiding. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from full awareness and acceptance of present experience, not from effort to become different. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics inside the field, including Isadore From, objected that some of Perls's brief public demonstrations provoked strong experiences without follow-through. |
| Mental model | Gestalt |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, emotion, body, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, enactment role, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/tony robbins.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestalt_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *here and now* | The focus on present experience in the session rather than on stories about the past or the future. |
| *contact* | The meeting between a person and their surroundings, including the therapist, which Gestalt therapy treats as the place where growth happens. |
| *experiment* | An action the therapist proposes for the client to try in the session in order to heighten awareness. |
| *unfinished business* | Feelings toward people in the client's life that were never expressed and still affect the client in the present. |
| *paradoxical theory of change* | Arnold Beisser's statement that change comes from fully accepting what one is rather than from trying to be different. |

#### GROW model (Goal, Reality, Options, Way forward)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Graham Alexander, Alan Fine, and Sir John Whitmore |
| Date | late 1980s (first published by Whitmore in 1992) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The coach asks questions in four stages: what the client wants, what is happening now, what the client could do, and what the client will do. The coach relies on questions rather than instructions, so the client works out their own route. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's own clarity about goal, situation, and options, reached through the coach's questions. |
| Limits and critiques | Passmore and Cantore argued that its focus on goals leaves little room to explore the philosophical side of life, so it suits performance settings better than life coaching. |
| Mental model | organizational. Performance coaching from sport and business, influenced by Gallwey's Inner Game |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, decision choice, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GROW_model; https://www.performanceconsultants.com/grow-model |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Goal* | The outcome the client wants from the topic or the session. |
| *Reality* | The current situation, described as accurately as possible. |
| *Options* | The possible courses of action, and in some versions the obstacles. |
| *Will or Way forward* | The specific actions the client commits to taking. |

#### Guru–shishya relationship (paramparā)

|---|---|

The teacher takes responsibility for the disciple's development and passes on what the lineage has transmitted. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from knowledge and realization transmitted through a living relationship with a teacher who stands in an unbroken lineage. |
| Limits and critiques | The large difference in power can foster unhealthy dependence on the teacher and projection onto them. |

| *paramparā* | The lineage: an unbroken succession of teachers and disciples. |
| *dīkṣā* | Initiation, in which the guru formally accepts the disciple. |
| *sevā* | Selfless service to the guru, a valued part of the relationship. |

#### Hakomi (mindfulness-centred somatic psychotherapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ron Kurtz, with a core group of colleagues |
| Date | 1970s (Hakomi Institute established in the early 1980s) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist helps the client enter a mindful state and then offers a small experiment, such as a gentle statement called a probe, so that the client can observe how they spontaneously react to it. The reactions reveal core beliefs, often expressed in posture and tension, and the therapist then offers the missing experience that the old belief treated as impossible. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from studying one's own automatic reactions in mindfulness and then receiving an experience that an old core belief ruled out. |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | body, thought belief, memory past, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://hakomiinstitute.com/about/hakomi-principles/; https://hakomiinstitute.com/about/the-hakomi-method; https://sensorimotorpsychotherapy.org/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *loving presence* | The therapist's open-hearted, well-intentioned state of attention toward the client, which Kurtz emphasized. |
| *core material* | The deep beliefs and memories that organize a person's experience, often held in posture, gesture, and tension. |
| *probe* | A small experimental statement or gesture offered while the client is mindful, so the client can observe their own spontaneous reaction; it is not advice. |
| *missing experience* | An experience the person's old beliefs taught them not to expect, which the therapy provides in order to change the pattern. |
| *nonviolence* | One of the method's five principles: gently supporting the person's own process instead of imposing on it or confronting its resistance. |

#### Ignatian spiritual direction (giving the Spiritual Exercises)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ignatius of Loyola |
| Date | 1520s–1548 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The director gives the retreatant prayer exercises suited to where they are and listens to their account of the inner movements that arose, especially consolation and desolation. The director helps the retreatant notice and discern these movements while taking care not to push them toward any choice, so that God can deal directly with the person. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from God's direct communication with the person, which the director protects by listening and by helping the person discern their inner movements. |
| Limits and critiques | The method assumes a trained director and a person able to undertake extended prayer; it was not designed for solitary use. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, decision choice, emotion |
| Medium | talk, silence, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/ignatius of loyola.md` |
| Sources | https://www.ccel.org/ccel/ignatius/exercises.ix.html; https://www.ignatianspirituality.com/making-good-decisions/spiritual-direction/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *consolation* | An inner movement toward faith, hope, love, and peace, noticed in prayer. |
| *desolation* | An inner movement of darkness, disturbance, or pull away from faith, hope, and love. |
| *discernment of spirits* | Telling apart inner movements that lead toward God from those that lead away, following Ignatius's rules. |
| *Annotation 15* | Ignatius's instruction that the one giving the Exercises should not incline the retreatant either way but, 'standing in the centre like a balance, leave the Creator to act immediately with the creature'. |

#### Jungian analysis (analytical psychology), with amplification of dream images

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Gustav Jung |
| Date | 1908–1930s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person and their parts |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The analysand brings dreams and images, and analyst and analysand enlarge a puzzling image by setting it beside similar images from myth, religion, alchemy, and art. The work stays with the image itself instead of following a chain of associations away from it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the conscious ego entering into relation with images from the unconscious, which carry meaning for the person's development. |
| Limits and critiques | Because the analyst supplies many of the parallels, the analyst's own knowledge and psyche strongly shape what an image is taken to mean. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. Jung's analytical psychology, a depth psychology with transpersonal elements |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, identity self, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | talk, art image |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/a sealed edge lets intensity concentrate to the point of transformation rather than dissipate.md` |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *amplification* | Enlarging an obscure dream image by placing it among parallel images from myth, religion, alchemy, and the arts. |
| *individuation* | Jung's term for the lifelong process of becoming a whole and distinct person. |
| *active imagination* | A method in which a person calls up an inner image while awake and then engages it in conversation. |
| *analysand* | The person being analysed. |

#### Logotherapy (existential analysis)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Viktor Frankl |
| Date | 1930s (first published 1938) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist uses questions to help the client find a meaning they can fulfil in their own situation, including in suffering that cannot be changed. For anxiety and obsessions, the therapist may ask the client to wish for or exaggerate the feared thing with humour, or to turn attention away from the symptom toward tasks and other people. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a person discovering a meaning to live for, which gives them freedom in relation to their symptoms and circumstances. |
| Mental model | existential and phenomenological |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, thought belief, death finitude |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the transformation field divides into five theories of where suffering lives.md` |
| Sources | https://www.viktorfrankl.org/logotherapy.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *will to meaning* | Frankl's name for the basic human drive to find and fulfil meaning. |
| *paradoxical intention* | A technique in which the client deliberately wishes for or exaggerates the feared symptom, with humour, to gain distance from it. |
| *dereflection* | A technique that draws the client's attention away from the symptom and toward meaningful activity or other people. |
| *Socratic dialogue* | Questions that help the client become aware of the possibility of meaning and of their freedom to fulfil it. |

#### Mentalization-based treatment (MBT)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Anthony Bateman and Peter Fonagy |
| Date | 1990s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The therapist asks the client, with genuine curiosity, about their own and other people's states of mind in specific interactions, instead of telling the client what they feel or mean. When emotion rises and the client stops being able to think about minds, the therapist slows the conversation to restore reflection. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recovering the capacity to think about minds, one's own and other people's, especially under emotional stress. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, thought belief |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mentalization-based_treatment; https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/1489633/3/Fonagy_MBT%20chapter%20for%20Kaplan%20and%20Sadock%20complete%20071015.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mentalizing* | Understanding one's own and other people's behaviour in terms of mental states such as feelings, wishes, and beliefs. |
| *not-knowing stance* | The therapist's respect for the fact that minds cannot be fully known, so the therapist asks rather than assumes what the client thinks or feels. |
| *inquisitive stance* | A wish to inquire and a willingness to be surprised by the client's response. |
| *epistemic trust* | Trust in another person as a source of knowledge relevant to oneself, which the treatment aims to restore. |
| *pretend mode* | A state in which talk about mental states is cut off from real feeling. |

#### Mentoring

|---|---|

| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A more experienced person meets a less experienced one over time and offers career help, such as advice, sponsorship, and visibility, together with personal support, such as acceptance and friendship. The relationship may arise informally or be arranged through an organization's formal programme. |
| What it assumes | Growth comes from a relationship with a more experienced person who models, advises, and supports over time. |
| Limits and critiques | Researchers have questioned whether the classic model of a single senior white male mentor serves under-represented groups. |

| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, identity self, task work |

| *reverse mentoring* | An arrangement in which a younger person mentors a more senior one, for example in new technology. |
| *mosaic mentoring* | Mentoring by several people at once, proposed as an alternative to the single senior mentor. |

#### Morita therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Shoma Morita |
| Date | 1919 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | In the classical inpatient form, the patient spends about a week in isolated bed rest and then moves through light work, heavier work, and preparation for daily life. The patient writes a daily diary of what they did, and the therapist writes comments in it that turn the patient's attention from symptoms toward purposeful action. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from accepting feelings as they are and turning attention to purposeful activity, rather than from trying to remove symptoms. |
| Limits and critiques | The classical inpatient form takes weeks and modern inpatient programmes about three months, which limits who can use it. |
| Mental model | other. A Japanese psychotherapy of accepting feelings as they are while acting constructively |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | emotion, task work, attention awareness |
| Medium | writing, silence, nature place |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *arugamama* | Accepting things as they are, including one's feelings and symptoms. |
| *shinkeishitsu* | Morita's name for the anxious, self-focused condition the therapy was designed to treat. |
| *isolation bed rest* | The first stage, in which the patient stays in bed alone except for eating, washing, and toileting, and faces their condition without distraction. |
| *diary guidance* | The practice in which the patient keeps a daily diary and the therapist responds with written comments. |

#### Motivational interviewing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | William R. Miller and Stephen Rollnick |
| Date | 1983–1991 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The practitioner asks open questions, affirms, reflects, and summarizes, and listens for the client's own statements in favour of change. The practitioner reflects those statements back and asks for more of them, while holding back the urge to argue for change or to fix the problem. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from resolving ambivalence through the client's own spoken reasons for change, which the helper draws out rather than supplies. |
| Limits and critiques | A 2016 Cochrane review found no substantive benefit of motivational interviewing for preventing alcohol misuse in young adults, and it appears less effective in groups than one to one. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *change talk* | The client's own statements of desire, ability, reasons, need, or commitment to change. |
| *sustain talk* | The client's statements in favour of keeping things as they are. |
| *righting reflex* | The helper's urge to correct the client or supply the solution; the fourth edition renames it the fixing reflex. |
| *OARS* | The four core skills: open questions, affirmations, reflections, and summaries. |
| *MI spirit* | The attitude of partnership, acceptance, compassion, and empowerment (earlier called evocation) that defines the approach. |

#### Narrative therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Michael White and David Epston |
| Date | 1980s (Narrative Means to Therapeutic Ends, 1990) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The therapist asks questions that name the problem as something separate from the person and trace its effects on the person's life. The therapist then asks about moments that contradict the problem's story and helps the person build a new account of their life from them, sometimes with invited listeners who respond to that account. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from separating a person's identity from the problem and developing an alternative story of their life from events the problem's story left out. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics point to a limited base of quantitative outcome research. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, memory past, justice power |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *externalizing conversation* | Talk that treats the problem as separate from the person, summarized as 'the person is not the problem, the problem is the problem'. |
| *unique outcomes* | Moments that contradict the person's problem-saturated story; the term is borrowed from Erving Goffman. |
| *re-authoring conversations* | Conversations that develop an alternative story of the person's life from overlooked events. |
| *outsider witness practice* | A structure in which invited listeners hear a person's preferred account of their life and respond by saying what resonated with their own lives. |

#### Ontological coaching

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Fernando Flores (foundations); Rafael Echeverría and Julio Olalla (Newfield Network) |
| Date | 1980s–1990s (Newfield founded around 1990) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, an organization |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The coach observes how the client uses language, moods, and the body to produce or block the results they want, and helps the client see the interpretations that make up their way of observing. The client then practises new ways of speaking, such as clear requests and promises, along with new moods and bodily postures. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a shift in the kind of observer a person is, which changes which actions become possible for them. |
| Mental model | existential and phenomenological. Draws on Heidegger, the speech-act theory of Austin and Searle, and Maturana's biology of cognition |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, body, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://instituteofcoaching.org/blogs/grounding-our-coaching-how-linguistic-coaching-came-be |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *observer* | The client's structure of interpretations, which shapes which actions seem possible to them. |
| *language, moods, and body* | The three domains the approach treats as intertwined in every human action. |
| *speech acts* | Utterances that perform actions, such as declarations, requests, and promises, which the coach helps the client use deliberately. |
| *moods* | Background emotional dispositions that shape what a person sees as possible. |

#### Person-centred therapy (client-centred therapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Rogers |
| Date | 1940s–1950s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | The therapist listens for the client's own frame of reference and says back what the client seems to mean and feel, without directing, diagnosing, or advising. The therapist tries to stay genuine and accepting throughout, so that the client leads the conversation. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the client's own tendency to grow, which is released when the client experiences a relationship with a genuine, accepting, and understanding other person. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics say it gives too little structure for clients with severe or complex conditions, and Rogers himself reported a study finding that clients who place their problem outside themselves and feel little responsibility for it were more likely to fail. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, identity self, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/carl rogers.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person-centered_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unconditional positive regard* | The therapist's acceptance of the client, which does not depend on what the client says or does. |
| *congruence* | The therapist is genuine in the relationship, so that what the therapist shows matches what the therapist actually experiences. |
| *empathic understanding* | The therapist senses the client's inner frame of reference and communicates that understanding back to the client. |
| *actualizing tendency* | Rogers's name for an inborn tendency of a person to grow and develop when conditions allow. |
| *non-directive* | The early name for the approach, meaning that the therapist does not steer the content or the goals. |

#### Pointing-out instruction (ngo sprod)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Tibetan Buddhist Mahāmudrā and Dzogchen lineages; popularized in the Kagyu school by Gampopa |
| Date | medieval Tibet (Gampopa, 12th century) onward |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | A lama introduces the student directly to the nature of their own mind, through words, gestures, or questions, in a way that allows the student to recognize it. The student either recognizes it or does not, and later practice aims to return to and stabilize that recognition. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from recognizing a nature of mind that is already present, which a qualified teacher can point out but cannot produce. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a single move |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, attention awareness, identity self |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | `notes/learning draws out what the learner already holds so the truest teaching midwifes rather than fills.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointing-out_instruction |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ngo sprod* | The Tibetan term for pointing out or introduction: the teacher's direct introduction to the nature of mind. |
| *rigpa* | In Dzogchen, the pure awareness that is recognized. |
| *ordinary mind* | In Mahāmudrā, the unfabricated mind that is pointed out. |
| *recognition* | The student's own seeing of what was pointed out, which the pointing out does not guarantee. |

#### Process Work (process-oriented psychology)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Arnold Mindell |
| Date | late 1970s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The practitioner notices signals the client does not identify with, such as a body symptom, a gesture, or a figure in a dream, and helps the client amplify and follow them through other channels such as movement, sound, or image. The practitioner pays close attention to the edge, the point where the client stops identifying with the experience. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from following and unfolding experiences a person does not yet identify with, so that they become part of awareness. |
| Mental model | process-oriented |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | body, identity self, attention awareness, conflict |
| Medium | talk, movement dance, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://iapop.com/history/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *primary process* | The parts of experience a person identifies with and intends. |
| *secondary process* | Less familiar experience that the person does not identify with, which carries information for growth. |
| *edge* | The boundary between primary and secondary process, where a person's sense of identity limits their awareness. |
| *dreambody* | Mindell's term for the idea that body symptoms and dreams express the same underlying process. |
| *amplification* | Intensifying a signal, such as a symptom or a gesture, and following it through different channels until its meaning becomes clear. |

#### Rational emotive behaviour therapy (REBT)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Albert Ellis |
| Date | 1955 |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The therapist teaches the client that upset comes mainly from rigid beliefs about an event rather than from the event itself, and then actively disputes those beliefs with the client. The client learns to replace absolute demands, the musts and shoulds, with flexible preferences and practises this between sessions. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from replacing rigid, absolute demands on oneself, on others, and on life with flexible preferences. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, emotion |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://albertellis.org/rebt-cbt-therapy/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rational_emotive_behavior_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ABC model* | An adversity (A) meets a person's beliefs (B), and the beliefs produce the emotional and behavioural consequences (C). |
| *disputing* | Questioning and challenging the evidence and logic behind an irrational belief; the D step. |
| *effective new belief* | The flexible, rational belief that replaces the irrational one; the E step. |
| *unconditional self-acceptance* | Accepting oneself as a person regardless of one's performance or of other people's approval. |

#### Sacramental confession (the Sacrament of Penance and Reconciliation)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Catholic Church; private confession spread from Irish and Celtic monastic practice |
| Date | 6th century onward; required at least yearly from 1215 |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a society's customs |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | After examining their conscience, the penitent tells their sins aloud to a priest, expresses sorrow, and receives a penance to perform. The priest then pronounces absolution, and everything confessed is protected by the seal of confession. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from repentance spoken aloud and from forgiveness pronounced by the Church's minister. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic. Sacramental Catholic theology rather than a contemplative method |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, memory past, identity self |
| Medium | talk, ritual object |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/ignatius of loyola.md` |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *contrition* | Sorrow for sin together with the resolve not to sin again. |
| *confession* | Telling one's sins aloud to a priest. |
| *satisfaction* | The act of repair, or penance, that the confessor assigns to the penitent. |
| *absolution* | The priest's pronouncement of forgiveness in the name of God. |
| *seal of confession* | The rule that the confessor may never reveal the penitent or the sins confessed. |

#### Scaffolding (tutoring in the zone of proximal development)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Wood, Jerome Bruner, and Gail Ross, building on Lev Vygotsky's zone of proximal development |
| Date | 1976 (Vygotsky's concept is earlier) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The tutor engages the learner in a task a little beyond what they can do alone, simplifies it, points out its important features, keeps frustration in check, and demonstrates when needed. The tutor withdraws this support step by step as the learner becomes able to do the task independently. |
| What it assumes | Learning happens when a more capable person supports what the learner cannot yet do alone and then hands over responsibility. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `ops/reading queue.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instructional_scaffolding |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *scaffolding* | Temporary support a tutor gives so that a learner can complete a task they cannot yet do alone. |
| *zone of proximal development* | The range between what a learner can do alone and what they can do with help from a more knowledgeable person. |
| *fading* | The gradual removal of support as the learner becomes more capable. |

#### Sensorimotor Psychotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Pat Ogden |
| Date | 1980s (institute founded 1981; Trauma and the Body, 2006) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist helps the client notice body sensations, posture, and movement mindfully while talking about difficult experience, keeping arousal within a range the client can bear. Therapist and client then try out physical actions, such as a change of posture or a movement, that help the client reorganize habitual trauma responses. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from working directly with the body's automatic responses to trauma, not only through understanding or talk. |
| Limits and critiques | The approach depends on the client's capacity for body awareness, and attention to inner states can suddenly bring overwhelming feeling, so interventions need careful pacing. |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, trauma healing, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the apparatus descends from the settlement the book positions itself against.md` |
| Sources | https://sensorimotorpsychotherapy.org/about/; https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/advances-in-psychiatric-treatment/article/sensorimotor-approaches-to-trauma-treatment/D89DECA6078BBCF28669E8F76090197C |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bottom-up processing* | Working from body sensation and movement toward emotion and thought, rather than starting from thought. |
| *window of tolerance* | The range of arousal in which a person can process experience without becoming overwhelmed or shutting down. |
| *tracking* | The therapist's close observation of the client's moment-to-moment bodily responses. |
| *dual awareness* | Noticing present sensations and surroundings while remembering a past event. |
| *somatic resources* | Physical actions such as posture, hand placement, or movement that help a person stay regulated. |

#### Six Category Intervention Analysis

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John Heron (Human Potential Research Project, University of Surrey) |
| Date | 1970s |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The helper learns to recognize and choose among six kinds of intervention: three authoritative ones (prescriptive, informative, confronting), in which the helper takes the lead, and three facilitative ones (cathartic, catalytic, supportive), which draw on the client's own capacities. The helper uses the scheme to notice which kind they are using and whether it suits the moment. |
| What it assumes | Effective help depends on the helper's awareness of which kind of intervention they are making and on a suitable balance between directing and facilitating. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | relationship present, learning skill |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | general knowledge, unverified; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Heron_(social_scientist) |
| Verified at those sources | no |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *authoritative interventions* | Prescriptive, informative, and confronting interventions, in which the helper directs, informs, or challenges. |
| *facilitative interventions* | Cathartic, catalytic, and supportive interventions, which help the client release emotion, find things out for themselves, and feel valued. |
| *catalytic* | An intervention that draws out the client's own self-discovery and problem-solving. |
| *cathartic* | An intervention that helps the client release painful emotion such as grief, fear, or anger. |

#### Socratic questioning (elenchus and maieutics)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Socrates, as portrayed in Plato's dialogues |
| Date | 5th–4th century BCE |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The questioner asks the other person to state what they believe and then, through a series of questions and agreed premises, shows that the belief conflicts with other things they hold. The questioner claims no knowledge of their own and describes the work as helping others bring forth and test their own ideas. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from discovering contradictions in one's own beliefs, which leaves a person ready to inquire into what they actually know. |
| Limits and critiques | Educators warn that the method can shame or humiliate the person questioned if it is misused, and scholars note that refuting a belief does not establish which alternative is true. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, learning skill |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | archive/reading — plato, meno and theaetetus on drawing-out and wonder (c. 380 bce).md |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socratic_method |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *elenchus* | Cross-examination that tests a stated belief, usually ending in its refutation. |
| *aporia* | The state of puzzlement in which the inquiry often ends. |
| *maieutics* | Midwifery: Socrates's image, in the Theaetetus, for helping others bring forth and test their own understanding. |
| *Socratic irony* | The questioner's profession of ignorance. |

#### Solution-focused brief therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Steve de Shazer, Insoo Kim Berg, and colleagues at the Brief Family Therapy Center, Milwaukee |
| Date | late 1970s–1980s |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The therapist asks the client to describe the future they want and the times when the problem is already absent or smaller, instead of analysing the problem. The therapist uses the miracle question and scaling questions, and compliments the client on what they already do that works. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from building on what the client already does that works and on the client's description of a preferred future, not from understanding the causes of the problem. |
| Mental model | solution-focused |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solution-focused_brief_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *miracle question* | A question that asks the client to imagine the problem solved overnight and to describe the first signs they would notice. |
| *exception questions* | Questions about times when the problem is absent, less severe, or better managed. |
| *scaling questions* | Questions that ask the client to rate progress or confidence on a numbered scale and to describe the next step up. |
| *not-knowing stance* | The therapist's curiosity and refusal to act as the expert on the client's life. |

#### Somatic Experiencing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Peter A. Levine |
| Date | 1970s (trademarked 1989) |
| Scale | two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The practitioner guides the client's attention to bodily sensations, briefly attending to a small amount of traumatic activation and then returning to sensations of safety or support. Working in these small steps, the practitioner watches for signs of release, such as trembling, warmth, tears, or deeper breathing, as the nervous system settles. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from letting the body complete and discharge survival responses that were interrupted during a traumatic event. |
| Limits and critiques | Reviews describe its evidence as promising but weak, and the polyvagal theory it often draws on is contested. |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, trauma healing |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the apparatus descends from the settlement the book positions itself against.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somatic_experiencing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *titration* | Working with a small, manageable amount of traumatic material at a time. |
| *pendulation* | Moving attention back and forth between activation and ease, which Levine describes as the organism's natural rhythm of contraction and expansion. |
| *SIBAM* | A map of experience as Sensation, Image, Behaviour, Affect, and Meaning. |
| *discharge* | The release of held activation, seen as tears, warmth, trembling, or changes in breathing. |
| *resourcing* | Finding and strengthening sensations, memories, or supports that help the nervous system settle. |

#### Sponsorship (Twelve Step programmes)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Alcoholics Anonymous and the later Twelve Step fellowships |
| Date | 1930s onward (Alcoholics Anonymous founded 1935) |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A member with longer recovery guides a newer member through the twelve steps, sharing their own experience and usually hearing the newer member's written moral inventory in the Fifth Step. The sponsor is not a professional, and sponsoring others is part of the sponsor's own recovery under the Twelfth Step. |
| What it assumes | Recovery comes from working the steps with someone who has done so, and helping others sustains one's own recovery. |
| Limits and critiques | Research has suggested few short-term benefits of sponsorship for a sponsee's one-year abstinence rate. |
| Mental model | other. Peer mutual aid within the Twelve Steps' spiritual programme of recovery |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, memory past, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-step_program |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sponsor* | A more experienced member in recovery who guides a newer member through the steps. |
| *sponsee* | The newer member being guided. |
| *experience, strength, and hope* | What a sponsor shares: their own story of recovery rather than professional advice. |
| *Fifth Step* | Admitting the exact nature of one's wrongs to another person, usually the sponsor, after writing a moral inventory. |
| *Twelfth Step* | Carrying the programme's message to others who still suffer, which includes sponsoring them. |

#### The Bonny Method of Guided Imagery and Music (GIM)

|---|---|

| Date | 1970s |

| What happens | After a conversation to set a focus and a guided relaxation, the client listens with eyes closed to a programme of classical music chosen by the therapist and describes aloud the images, feelings, and memories that arise. The therapist responds to support the imagery and, when the music ends, helps the client return and reflect on the experience. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from inner imagery evoked by music in an expanded state of consciousness, which brings unconscious material into awareness. |

| Medium | voice music, art image, talk |

| *relaxation induction* | A guided relaxation that brings the client into a receptive, expanded state before the music begins. |
| *music-imaging* | The central phase, in which the client describes imagery evoked by the music while the therapist talks with them. |
| *postlude* | The closing conversation that helps the client integrate the imagery. |

#### The Inner Game (Inner Game coaching)

| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Instead of giving instructions about technique, the coach directs the learner's attention to accurate, non-judgmental observation of what is happening, such as the ball's path or the feel of the racket. The learner lets the body adjust by itself while the judging, instructing voice in the mind quiets. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from removing mental interference so that the learner's natural capacity to learn can work. |

| *non-judgmental observation* | Noticing accurately what is happening without labelling it good or bad. |
| *interference* | Whatever keeps a person from expressing their potential, chiefly the doubt and over-control of Self 1. |

| What happens | The helper uses attentive listening and empathic responses to help the client tell their story, then challenges the client's blind spots and helps them picture a better future and set goals. Finally the helper supports the client in choosing strategies and planning action. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from helping clients manage their problems and use unused opportunities through a staged process that moves from understanding to action. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. An integrative problem-management model, which Egan calls developmental eclecticism, built on core listening skills |

When the Thinker reaches a limiting assumption, the partner helps them form an Incisive Question from the Thinker's own words that sets the untrue assumption aside so their thinking can continue. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the quality of attention the Thinker receives, which lets them think further for themselves, and from removing untrue limiting assumptions. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |

| Time | session |

| *Attention* | Listening without interruption and with interest in where the person will go next in their thinking. |
| *Incisive Questions* | Questions that free the mind of untrue assumptions that a person lives as true. |
| *Ease* | Discarding internal urgency. |
| *Ten Components* | The ten conditions of a Thinking Environment: Attention, Equality, Ease, Appreciation, Encouragement, Feelings, Information, Difference, Incisive Questions, and Place. |

The tradition holds that the master's presence and guidance shape the disciple's thought and character, and periods of companionship alternate with periods of seclusion. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the influence of a realized master's presence, received through disciplined companionship. |
| Limits and critiques | The relationship asks for obedience to the master, which gives the master great influence over the disciple. |

| Structure | tending conditions; a method |

| *shaykh* | The spiritual master who guides disciples. |
