### Two people as equals

This scale is the main one for 17 techniques, and the vault has met 5 of them.

#### 36 Questions for Increasing Closeness

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Arthur Aron and colleagues |
| Date | 1997 |
| Scale | two people as equals |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Two people take turns answering three sets of questions that become more personal from set to set, spending about fifteen minutes on each set. Both partners answer every question, so the disclosure stays mutual and deepens gradually. |
| What it assumes | Closeness grows from sustained, gradually deepening, reciprocal self-disclosure. |
| Limits and critiques | The practice may not benefit people with clinically low social motivation, and some questions may distress trauma survivors who lack support. |
| Mental model | other. A social-psychology procedure for producing interpersonal closeness |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, identity self |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://ggia.berkeley.edu/practice/36_questions_for_increasing_closeness |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *36 Questions* | The question list, in three sets of twelve that move from light topics to deeply personal ones. |
| *closeness* | The feeling of connection the study measured, which rose more than it did for pairs asking small-talk questions. |
| *self-disclosure* | Revealing personal information, which the procedure makes reciprocal and increases step by step. |

The Martyrology of Óengus records Brigid of Kildare's counsel that 'anyone without a soul friend is like a body without a head'. |

#### Appreciative interview (the Discovery phase of Appreciative Inquiry)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Cooperrider and Suresh Srivastva (Case Western Reserve University) |
| Date | 1987 |
| Scale | two people as equals, a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants pair up and interview each other with prepared questions about times when they, their work, or their organization were at their best, and about what made those times possible. The stories are then shared in groups to identify the organization's positive core, which the later Dream, Design, and Destiny phases use as their starting material. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from building on what already works and on what people care about, found through stories of the organization at its best. |
| Limits and critiques | Gervase Bushe argues that practitioners often overemphasize positive feeling and that transformation needs new generative ideas. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist. Social-constructionist organization development |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, meaning purpose, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *4-D cycle* | The four phases of Appreciative Inquiry: Discovery, Dream, Design, and Destiny. |
| *Discovery* | The first phase, in which people share stories of the organization or of their lives at their best. |
| *positive core* | What works well and what people care about most, identified from the stories. |

#### Authentic Movement (mover and witness)

|---|---|
| Originators | Mary Starks Whitehouse; developed by Janet Adler (the Discipline of Authentic Movement) and Joan Chodorow |
| Date | mid-20th century (1950s–1970s) |
| Scale | two people as equals, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | The mover closes their eyes and follows inner impulses to move or to be still, while a witness watches with benevolent, non-judging presence. Afterwards they may speak about the experience, and the witness avoids judging or interpreting the mover. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from moving from inner impulse while being seen without judgment, which strengthens a person's own inner witness. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. A dance and movement practice with Jungian roots; Adler's discipline adds contemplative elements |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | body, attention awareness, emotion |

| Time | session |

|---|---|

| *witness* | The person who watches the mover with benevolent, non-judging presence. |
| *inner witness* | The capacity to observe one's own experience, which develops through the practice. |
| *movement-in-depth* | Whitehouse's name for her Jungian-influenced approach to movement. |

#### Co-counselling (Re-evaluation Counseling and Co-Counselling International)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Harvey Jackins (Re-evaluation Counseling); John Heron with Tom and Dency Sargent (Co-Counselling International) |
| Date | early 1950s; Co-Counselling International 1974 |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Two trained peers divide the time exactly equally: one is the client and works on whatever they choose, while the other gives full, friendly attention without advising or interrupting, and then they swap. The client is encouraged to let feeling out through crying, laughing, trembling, or yawning. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from releasing stored emotional hurt in the presence of an attentive peer, which frees the person to think freshly about their experience. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics, including supporters of Co-Counselling International, have described Re-evaluation Counseling as authoritarian and cult-like and have cited allegations of sexual abuse of clients; Co-Counselling International split from it in 1974. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. A cathartic theory of peer counselling built on emotional discharge and re-evaluation |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, memory past, trauma healing |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-counselling; https://www.co-counselling.org.uk/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Heron_(social_scientist) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *client and counsellor* | The two roles the partners take in turn. |
| *discharge* | Emotional release such as crying, laughing, trembling, warm perspiration, or yawning, which the theory holds frees a person from past distress. |
| *distress patterns* | Rigid, repetitive behaviours rooted in unprocessed painful experience. |
| *re-evaluation* | The fresh thinking about an experience that the theory expects to follow discharge. |

#### Empathy exchange in Nonviolent Communication (empathy buddies)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Marshall Rosenberg and the Nonviolent Communication community |
| Date | 1960s–1970s onward |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Practice partners take turns: one speaks about something that matters to them, while the other listens for the feelings and needs underneath and offers guesses about them for the speaker to confirm or correct. The listener holds back advice, analysis, and reassurance. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from connecting with the feelings and needs beneath what a person says, which makes a compassionate response possible. |
| Limits and critiques | Researchers note a small evidence base, and critics say its emphasis on explicit emotional disclosure can feel intrusive in some cultures and does not address structural imbalances of power. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, conflict, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/interlocutors.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolent_Communication |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *observations, feelings, needs, requests* | The four components of the Nonviolent Communication model. |
| *empathy* | Receiving another person's experience with full attention to their feelings and needs, rather than understanding it only intellectually. |
| *self-empathy* | Compassionately connecting with one's own feelings and needs. |
| *needs* | Universal human needs, which the model treats as the source of feelings. |

#### Focusing partnership (Changes groups)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Eugene Gendlin and his graduate students at the University of Chicago |
| Date | 1970 |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Two people divide their time equally; in each turn one person focuses on their felt sense while the other listens and says back what they hear. The focuser decides what happens in their own turn, and the listener does not advise or interpret. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from attending to one's own felt sense while another person listens, which helps the felt sense move and shift. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | body, emotion, attention awareness |

| *focuser* | The person attending inwardly to their felt sense during their turn. |
| *listener* | The partner who gives attention and reflects back the focuser's words. |
| *Changes* | The name of the first peer Listening and Focusing community in Chicago, and of the groups modelled on it. |

#### Holotropic Breathwork (breather and sitter pairs)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Stanislav Grof and Christina Grof |
| Date | 1970s–1980s (practised at Esalen in the 1980s; Grof Transpersonal Training from 1989) |
| Scale | two people as equals, a large group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Within a workshop, participants work in pairs and take turns: the breather lies down with eyes closed and breathes faster and deeper to evocative music, while the sitter stays present and available without interfering, interrupting, or trying to guide. Trained facilitators help when needed, and the experience is integrated through practices such as mandala drawing. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a non-ordinary state of consciousness in which the person's own inner healing intelligence brings material forward and resolves it. |
| Limits and critiques | Rapid breathing can cause tingling, cramping, and distressing states; the method is contraindicated for conditions such as cardiovascular disease, glaucoma, severe asthma, seizure disorders, and mental illness; and research on it is limited. |
| Mental model | transpersonal |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body, memory past, spirit ultimate, trauma healing |
| Medium | breath body, voice music, art image |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.holotropic.com/holotropic-breathwork/about-holotropic-breathwork/; https://www.holotropic.com/grof-transpersonal-training/gtt-history-and-founders/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holotropic_Breathwork |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *breather* | The participant who breathes and goes through the experience. |
| *sitter* | The partner who stays present and available to assist the breather without interfering or guiding. |
| *inner healing intelligence* | The idea that the person's own inner capacity guides the process, so its content is unique to each person. |
| *non-ordinary state of consciousness* | The altered state that the faster breathing and the music are meant to bring about. |

#### Insight Dialogue (interpersonal meditation)

A short set of meditation guidelines, beginning with pausing and relaxing, shapes how they speak and listen. |
| What it assumes | Insight arises when meditative awareness is carried into the moment of speaking and listening with another person. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. Rooted in Buddhist insight (vipassana) meditation |

#### Intentional Peer Support (IPS)

|---|---|
| Originators | Shery Mead |

| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A peer supporter with lived experience of psychiatric services builds a mutual relationship with another person in which both explore how they each came to see the world as they do. Instead of helping or fixing, the two learn together and move toward what the person wants rather than away from what they fear. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from mutual relationships in which both people learn, rather than from one person helping or treating another. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory. Peer support grounded in lived experience of psychiatric services |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |

| *mutuality* | The third task: a relationship in which both people learn and both have a say. |
| *moving towards* | The fourth task: moving toward what one wants rather than away from what one does not want. |

The person circling reflects the other's words and experience and checks whether they have understood correctly, instead of steering toward a goal. |

#### Peer coaching (teachers' peer coaching study teams)

|---|---|
| Originators | Beverly Showers and Bruce Joyce |
| Date | 1980s |
| Scale | two people as equals, an organization |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Teachers work in pairs or small teams to plan lessons, develop materials, and watch each other teach, in order to put new teaching methods into practice. In Showers and Joyce's later form the observer learns by watching and gives no verbal feedback, and the one teaching is called the coach. |
| What it assumes | Teachers put new practices into use when they plan and observe together regularly, rather than through training alone. |
| Limits and critiques | Showers and Joyce found that verbal feedback between peers tended to become evaluative, so they dropped it from the process. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |

| *coached* | The observing teacher, who learns by watching a peer teach. |

#### The Enlightenment Intensive dyad

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Charles Berner (later known as Yogeshwar Muni) |
| Date | 1968 |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Two partners sit facing each other; one asks the other to tell them who they are, and the other contemplates and then communicates whatever arises, while the listener only receives it and thanks them. The partners swap roles every few minutes through a dyad of about forty minutes, repeated many times during a retreat that usually lasts three days. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from repeated direct contemplation of a fundamental question, joined with full communication to a partner who receives without comment. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, spirit ultimate, attention awareness |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | general knowledge, unverified |
| Verified at those sources | no |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dyad* | The paired exercise in which partners alternate between contemplating and communicating, and receiving. |
| *contemplation* | Attending directly to the question, such as who am I, and noticing what one actually experiences. |
| *communication* | Telling one's partner what arose in contemplation, which the partner receives without comment. |
| *direct experience* | Knowing the truth about oneself directly rather than through ideas, which the retreat aims at. |

| What it assumes | People think best for themselves when they receive uninterrupted, interested attention from an equal. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |

### Three people

This scale is the main one for 18 techniques, and the vault has met 1 of them.

#### Discernment Counseling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | William J. Doherty (University of Minnesota; Doherty Relationship Institute) |
| Date | 2008 onward |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | With a couple in which one partner is leaning out of the marriage and the other is leaning in, the counselor meets them together and then mostly separately, helping each look at what happened and their own part in it, so that they can choose one of three paths: keep things as they are for now, move toward divorce, or commit to six months of couples therapy with divorce off the table. |
| What it assumes | Couples close to divorce first need clarity and confidence about their direction, reached by each partner understanding the problems and their own contribution, before therapy can help. |
| Limits and critiques | It is deliberately short, usually one to five sessions, and does not try to repair the relationship; it only clarifies whether the couple will try. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. A brief, decision-focused protocol from a family therapist, not a form of couples therapy |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, relationship present, conflict |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.family-institute.org/sites/default/files/pdfs/csi_hardy_discernment_counseling.pdf; https://discernmentcounseling.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *mixed-agenda couple* | a couple in which one partner wants to save the marriage and the other is considering ending it |
| *leaning out and leaning in* | the positions of the partner inclined to end the marriage and of the partner who wants to keep it |
| *three paths* | the options the process clarifies: keep the status quo for now, divorce, or a six-month trial of couples therapy |

#### Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Sue Johnson and Leslie Greenberg |
| Date | 1985 |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist helps the couple identify the negative interaction cycle they are in and the attachment emotions and needs beneath each partner's moves, and then guides each partner to express those deeper emotions and needs directly to the other so that the bond between them changes. |
| What it assumes | Couple distress is kept going by an interaction cycle that blocks secure attachment, and change comes when the partners reach and express the emotions and needs beneath it. |
| Limits and critiques | James C. Coyne criticized some of its research as underpowered and at high risk of bias, while noting that such problems are common in psychotherapy research. |
| Mental model | experiential and Focusing-oriented. Johnson's version integrates attachment theory with experiential (humanistic) and systemic approaches |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, conflict |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotionally_focused_therapy; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35191060/; https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1752-0606.1985.tb00624.x |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *negative interaction cycle* | the repeating pattern of moves between the partners, such as one pursuing and one withdrawing, which the couple learns to treat as the shared problem |
| *de-escalation* | the first stage, in which the partners stop seeing themselves as victims of each other and become allies against the cycle |
| *withdrawer re-engagement* | a change event in which the more withdrawn partner becomes more present and assertive |
| *blamer softening* | a change event in which the pursuing partner expresses softer and more vulnerable longings instead of blame |

#### Facilitative mediation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Community mediation practice in volunteer dispute resolution centers; named as a style in Leonard Riskin's grid of mediator orientations and by Zena D. Zumeta |
| Date | late 20th century; Riskin's grid 1994 and 1996 |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The mediator structures the meeting, asks questions, acknowledges and normalizes each party's point of view, looks for the interests beneath the positions each side has taken, and helps the parties find and weigh options, without recommending an outcome or predicting what a court would do. |
| What it assumes | The parties can reach a better agreement themselves once their underlying interests are made explicit in a well-structured conversation. |
| Limits and critiques | The facilitative mediator declines to give opinions on the merits of the case, which parties who want an expert assessment may find unhelpful. |
| Mental model | other. Interest-based problem solving: the parties reach agreement by identifying the interests beneath their positions |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://imimediation.org/resources/background/styles-of-mediation; https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=1506684 |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *positions* | what each party says it wants |
| *interests* | the needs and concerns beneath a position, which can often be met in more than one way |
| *facilitative and evaluative* | the distinction between a mediator who only structures the process and one who assesses the weaknesses of each side's case |

#### Family mediation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Present in various forms in many cultures; the current professional form is offered, for example, under the Family Mediation Council in England and Wales |
| Date | traditional, undated; current professional practice |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | After an assessment meeting, an impartial mediator, or two co-mediators, helps separating family members make their own decisions about children, money, and property, meeting them jointly or separately and keeping the discussion on the future and on the children. |
| What it assumes | Separating families make arrangements they are more likely to keep when they decide for themselves with the help of an impartial third person. |
| Limits and critiques | In England and Wales the requirement to attend a first meeting does not apply in some cases, mainly those involving domestic abuse, and the mediator decides whether a case is suitable. |
| Mental model | other. Future-focused problem solving that keeps children's needs at the center |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, decision choice, future vision |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.familymediationcouncil.org.uk/family-mediation/assessment-meeting-miam/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_mediation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divorce_mediation |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *MIAM* | Mediation Information and Assessment Meeting, the first meeting, at which the mediator hears the situation and says whether the case is suitable for mediation |
| *co-mediation* | two mediators working together with the family |
| *child-centred* | keeping the children's needs at the center, including, in England and Wales, discussing whether children over ten can have their views heard |

#### Gottman Method Couples Therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John Gottman and Julie Schwartz Gottman |
| Date | 1990s onward (Gottman Institute founded 1996) |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist assesses the relationship through a joint session, individual interviews with each partner, and questionnaires, gives the couple detailed feedback, and then works on friendship, conflict management, and shared meaning, for example by helping the partners manage recurring conflicts in dialogue rather than trying to solve them. |
| What it assumes | A relationship improves when partners strengthen their friendship, handle conflict with behaviors that research has linked to lasting relationships, and build shared meaning. |
| Limits and critiques | The claim that Gottman's research predicts divorce with high accuracy has been criticized, by Richard Heyman among others, because the prediction formulas were fitted to outcomes that were already known. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. Derived from observational research on how couples interact; it teaches specific behaviors and skills |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, conflict, emotion, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.gottman.com/about/the-gottman-method/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Gottman |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Sound Relationship House* | the Gottmans' model of the components of a healthy relationship, arranged as levels that rest on one another, from building love maps of each other's worlds to creating shared meaning |
| *Four Horsemen* | criticism, contempt, defensiveness, and stonewalling, the four communication patterns that Gottman's research associated with relationships ending |
| *perpetual problems* | recurring conflicts rooted in lasting differences between partners, which are managed rather than solved |
| *bids* | small attempts by one partner to get the other's attention, affection, or connection, to which the other either responds or does not |

#### Imago Dialogue (Couples Dialogue)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Harville Hendrix and Helen LaKelly Hunt |
| Date | 1980s (couples workshop from 1979; book 1988) |
| Scale | three people, two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | One partner, the sender, speaks in 'I' statements while the other, the receiver, mirrors back what was said and asks 'Is there more?', then says that it makes sense from the sender's point of view, then names the feelings the sender may be having; the partners then switch roles, often with a therapist guiding the steps. |
| What it assumes | Partners change how they relate when each one feels accurately heard and understood by the other in a safe, structured exchange. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, conflict, emotion |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harville_Hendrix; https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sender* | the partner who speaks about their experience in 'I' statements, avoiding shaming and blaming |
| *receiver* | the partner who listens and responds through the three steps |
| *mirroring* | repeating or paraphrasing the sender's words and then asking 'Is there more?' |
| *validation* | saying that what the sender says makes sense from their point of view, without having to agree |
| *empathy* | imagining and naming the feelings the sender may be having |

#### Integrative Behavioral Couple Therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Andrew Christensen and Neil S. Jacobson |
| Date | 1990s (book 1996) |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | After a joint session and separate sessions with each partner, the therapist gives the couple a formulation of their differences, emotional sensitivities, outside stresses, and pattern of interaction, and then combines acceptance strategies, such as describing one's pain without accusing or describing the shared pattern together from a distance, with traditional behavior-change skills. |
| What it assumes | Couples need both to accept what will not change and to change what can, and acceptance often makes change easier. |
| Mental model | cognitive and behavioral. A behavioral therapy that adds acceptance strategies to traditional behavior change |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, conflict, emotion |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5096782/; https://www.apa.org/pubs/videos/4310904 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *DEEP analysis* | the formulation of a couple's Differences, Emotional sensitivities, External stressors or circumstances, and Pattern of interaction |
| *empathic joining* | a partner expressing their pain without accusing the other, so that the other can feel for them |
| *unified detachment* | the partners stepping back to describe their conflict pattern together in a calm, descriptive way |

#### Interpersonal Process Recall

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Norman Kagan and colleagues, Michigan State University |
| Date | 1960s; training manual 1972 |
| Scale | three people, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | After a recorded interview, the interviewer watches the replay with a trained inquirer and stops the recording whenever a feeling or thought from the interview comes back; the inquirer asks open questions about it and offers no suggestions or judgments. In mutual recall, both people from the interview review the recording together with the inquirer. |
| What it assumes | People register more during an interaction than they act on, and reviewing the recording under non-judgmental questioning lets them notice and learn from it. |
| Limits and critiques | Kagan's manual advises that the first recall take place alone with the inquirer and without observers, and warns instructors not to let group review of recordings turn into attacks on the person recorded. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. A self-discovery method using recorded replay, in which the helper shares the person's discovery rather than judging it |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, emotion, relationship present, learning skill |
| Medium | talk, technology |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED065793.pdf; https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED017946; https://archive.org/details/interpersonalprocessrecallinquirerroleandfunction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inquirer* | the third person who sits with the person reviewing the recording and asks open questions without judging or advising |
| *recall session* | the replay of a recorded interaction in which a person relives and names what they felt and thought at each moment |
| *mutual recall* | a recall session in which both participants of the recorded interaction review it together |

#### Listening triad with a witness (speaker, listener, witness)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Widespread facilitation format used in dialogue and Art of Hosting trainings and in Authentic Relating events, including the author's own; no single originator found |
| Date | undated; in current use |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | In groups of three, one person speaks from their own experience to a question, one listens and may ask deepening or clarifying questions, and one witnesses in silence, noticing images and bodily feelings; after a set time the witness gives impressions back, and the roles rotate until each person has taken all three. |
| What it assumes | A speaker hears their own experience more fully when one person listens and a second person, who watched, reports what they noticed. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, attention awareness, emotion |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/interlocutors.md` |
| Sources | https://collaboratiohelvetica.ch/en/blog/2020/1/23/triads; http://www.befrienderforum.org/2017/02/listening-triads-with-speaker-listener.html; the vault, `notes/interlocutors.md` |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *speaker* | the person who shares their own experience in response to the question, speaking in the first person |
| *listener* | the person who listens actively and leaves the speaker free to explore, asking only deepening or clarifying questions |
| *witness* | the silent observer, sometimes called the holder of the space, who notices what images and feelings arise while listening and then offers them as feedback |

#### Microcounseling (microtraining)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Allen E. Ivey, with C. J. Normington, C. D. Miller, W. H. Morrill, and R. F. Haase |
| Date | 1967-1968 |
| Scale | three people, a small group, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The trainer isolates one interviewing skill, such as attending behavior, shows a model of it, and has trainees practise only that skill in short role-played interviews that are recorded or observed, followed by feedback, before moving to the next skill. |
| What it assumes | A helper's practice changes by learning one observable skill at a time through modeling, rehearsal, and feedback. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, technology |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.allenivey.com/microcounseling; https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED021275; https://psycnet.apa.org/record/1968-17988-001 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *attending behavior* | the first microskill: culturally appropriate eye contact, vocal tone, verbal following, and body language that show the listener is following the speaker |
| *microskills* | the separate, teachable units into which the method divides a counseling interview |
| *microskills hierarchy* | the ordering of the skills from basic listening to influencing, taught step by step |

#### Narrative mediation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John Winslade and Gerald Monk |
| Date | 2000 (book); 2003 (Winslade's paper on discursive positioning) |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The mediator invites each party to tell their story of the conflict, speaks of the conflict as a problem that affects both parties rather than as a trait of either, examines the social discourses and positions the story puts people in, and builds on events that fit a counter-story of cooperation. |
| What it assumes | People in conflict tell conflict stories shaped by social discourses that position them, and change comes from questioning those stories and developing a preferred counter-story. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, identity self, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://dulwichcentre.com.au/wp-content/uploads/2021/06/08-John-Winslade.pdf; https://www.beyondintractability.org/bksum/winslade-narrative |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *externalising conversation* | talking about the conflict as something separate that acts on the parties, rather than as part of who they are |
| *discursive positioning* | the way what a person says places both the speaker and the listener into positions, such as expert and deferent, from which to respond |
| *counter-story* | an account of the relationship that does not fit the conflict story, which the mediator helps the parties develop |

#### Nonviolent Communication mediation (Mediate Your Life)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Marshall Rosenberg (Nonviolent Communication); developed as the Mediate Your Life training by Ike Lasater and John Kinyon |
| Date | 2000s (Mediate Your Life from 2002-2003) |
| Scale | three people, one person and their parts, two people as equals |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The mediator listens for and reflects the needs behind each party's point of view, makes sure each party hears the other's needs, and only then looks for strategies that meet all the needs surfaced; the mediator uses the same three steps on their own judgments before and after mediating. |
| What it assumes | Conflict is over strategies rather than needs, so when the needs behind each point of view are heard and understood, a strategy can be found that works for everyone. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, emotion, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.usherbrooke.ca/cfcdroit/fileadmin/sites/cfcdroit/documents/GERD/LASATER_MediatorSelfCare.pdf; https://johnkinyon.com/about/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *needs and strategies* | the distinction between universal needs, which do not conflict, and the strategies people use to meet them, which do |
| *enemy image* | any judgment or evaluation a mediator holds about a party or about themselves |
| *Enemy Image Process* | a three-part practice of empathy for oneself, empathy for the other person, and requests, used to clear such judgments |
| *Celebrate/Mourn/Learn* | the review after a mediation of what met and what did not meet the mediator's needs |

#### Relationship Enhancement therapy and program

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Bernard G. Guerney Jr. |
| Date | 1970s (book 1977) |
| Scale | three people, a small group, two people as equals |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The therapist teaches partners or family members to take turns as an expresser, who states their own feelings and wishes without judging themselves or the other, and as an empathic responder, who reflects what they heard, and then coaches them while they use these skills on their real issues. |
| What it assumes | Empathy is both a natural human capacity and a skill that can be taught, so relationships improve when members are taught to express themselves and listen empathically. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. It combines Carl Rogers's client-centered theory with learning and behavioral theories |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, conflict, learning skill |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://fatherhoodchannel.com/2019/03/22/bernard-guerney-89-giant-of-psychotherapy/; https://books.google.com/books/about/Relationship_Enhancement.html?id=HHNHAAAAMAAJ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *owning expresser* | the skill of stating one's feelings and wishes as one's own, without judging oneself or the other |
| *empathic responder* | the skill of listening for and reflecting back the other person's feelings and meaning |
| *discussion-negotiation* | the skill of working through an issue together once each person has been heard |

#### Skills practice triad (counsellor or coach, client, observer)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Counsellor and coach training programs; no single originator found |
| Date | undated; standard in current counsellor and coach training |
| Scale | three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Three trainees take turns as helper, client, and observer. The helper practises a named skill on a real but manageable topic the client brings, the observer watches how the skill is used and then gives specific feedback, and everyone rotates roles. |
| What it assumes | Helping skills improve through repeated practice on real material combined with immediate, specific feedback from a peer who watched. |
| Limits and critiques | The topics are kept manageable because the setting is training rather than therapy, so deeply traumatic material is out of scope. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.aspirecounsellingacademy.co.uk/blog/counselling-skills-triads-what-to-expect; https://certifiedcoach.org/path-to-mastery-how-to-be-an-observer-in-a-coaching-triad/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *observer* | the trainee who watches the session and then gives specific, respectful, and balanced feedback on how the helper used the skill |
| *client* | the trainee who brings a real topic that is suitable for a training setting and manageable in the time available |
| *rotation* | each person takes every role in turn, so that all three see the session from each position |

#### Transformative mediation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Robert A. Baruch Bush and Joseph P. Folger |
| Date | 1994 |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | The mediator follows the parties' conversation moment by moment, reflecting and summarizing what they say, and attends to openings where a party becomes clearer and more able to decide or more able to hear the other's view, while leaving decisions about both process and outcome to the parties. |
| What it assumes | Conflict leaves people weaker and more self-absorbed, and the mediator's work is to support each party's own shifts toward strength and toward responsiveness to the other, whether or not a settlement follows. |
| Limits and critiques | It measures success by these shifts rather than by settlement, and it asks the mediator to give up problem solving and control of the process. |
| Mental model | dialogic. It rests on a relational view in which conflict is a crisis in human interaction |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, relationship present, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transformative_mediation; https://imimediation.org/resources/background/styles-of-mediation |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *empowerment* | a party's movement toward being able to deliberate and make decisions in the conflict |
| *recognition* | a party's movement toward hearing the other party and starting to understand their perspective |
| *crisis in human interaction* | the model's description of conflict as a breakdown in which people become more vulnerable and more self-absorbed |

#### Triad Training Model (cross-cultural counselor training)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Paul B. Pedersen |
| Date | 1977 (first published) |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A trainee counselor interviews a role-played client from another culture while a third role player, the anticounselor, says aloud the negative things the client is thinking but not saying. In the four-person version a procounselor also says aloud the positive things the client is thinking but not saying. |
| What it assumes | A counselor learns to work across cultures by hearing, during the interview itself, what a client from another culture is thinking but not saying. |
| Mental model | dialogic. A training model built on making a client's unspoken internal dialogue audible to the counselor |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, relationship present, thought belief |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://drpaulbpedersen.home.blog/achievements/research/triad-training-model-ttm/; https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/j.2164-4918.1977.tb04491.x; https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED143733 |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *anticounselor* | the role player who voices the negative things a culturally different client is thinking but not saying |
| *procounselor* | the role player who voices the positive things the client is thinking but not saying |
| *recovery skills* | the counselor's skills for getting out of trouble after a mistake in the interview, one of the model's four training aims |

#### Troika Consulting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless (Liberating Structures) |
| Date | 2000s; published 2014 |
| Scale | three people, an organization, a large group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | In groups of three, the client states a challenge and answers clarifying questions, then turns their back while the two consultants discuss ideas and advice with each other; the client then turns around and says what was most useful, and the roles rotate so each person is the client once. |
| What it assumes | Peers can give useful perspectives on a challenge when the person who owns it listens without defending themselves or directing the discussion. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent. Liberating Structures were developed from complexity science and assume that useful knowledge is already present among peers |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | task work, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.liberatingstructures.com/troika-consulting; https://www.liberatingstructures.com/history-of-ls |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *client* | the person who brings a challenge and then listens with their back turned |
| *consultants* | the two peers who first ask clarifying questions without giving solutions and then talk with each other about the challenge |
| *turning the back* | the client facing away so that the consultants talk to each other rather than at the client, and the client's facial reactions cannot influence them |

#### Understanding-based mediation (Mediation Through Understanding)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Gary Friedman and Jack Himmelstein, Center for Understanding in Conflict (formerly the Center for Mediation in Law) |
| Date | 1980s (center founded 1982); book 2008 |
| Scale | three people, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The mediator works with both parties, and their lawyers if present, in the same room throughout, without private caucuses, and repeatedly loops: states back what a party has said, asks whether it is right, and continues until the party confirms feeling understood; the parties are taught to loop each other. |
| What it assumes | People in conflict are the ones best placed to resolve it, and they decide better together once each feels understood. |
| Limits and critiques | It deliberately rejects the private caucus that is common in commercial mediation, a choice other mediators dispute. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, decision choice, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://understandinginconflict.org/our-history/; https://understandinginconflict.org/the-loop-of-understanding/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *looping* | stating back one's understanding of what another person said and checking until they confirm it is accurate |
| *loop of understanding* | the four steps of understanding a party, expressing that understanding, seeking confirmation, and receiving it |
| *no caucus* | the rule that the mediator does not meet the parties separately, so all work happens with everyone present |

### A small group

This scale is the main one for 37 techniques, and the vault has met 8 of them.

#### Action learning set

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Reg Revans |
| Date | 1940s onward (National Coal Board) |
| Scale | a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A small set of peers, each facing a real and difficult problem at work, meets regularly; each member in turn presents their problem and the others help mainly by asking questions, and the member then acts and reports back at later meetings. |
| What it assumes | People learn most by acting on real problems and questioning their own assumptions with peers, not only by receiving expert knowledge. |
| Limits and critiques | Revans was skeptical of learning coaches because he feared that sets would come to depend on them. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | task work, learning skill, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_learning |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *L = P + Q* | Revans's formula: learning comes from programmed knowledge plus questioning |
| *programmed knowledge* | existing expert knowledge, such as that taught in courses |
| *comrades in adversity* | peers facing similar hard problems who learn from one another |
| *set* | the small group of peers that meets to work on its members' problems |

#### Authentic Relating games and trainings

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | The Authentic Relating movement that grew beside Circling; codified by groups such as Authentic Revolution (Sara Ness) and ART International (Ryel Kestano and Jason Digges) |
| Date | 2000s onward (ART International founded 2017) |
| Scale | a small group, two people as equals, three people, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A facilitator leads a sequence of short, structured exercises in pairs, threes, and the whole group, such as the Noticing game, in which people say aloud what they notice in themselves and in each other, guided by five practices: welcome everything, assume nothing, reveal your experience, own your experience, and honor self and other. |
| What it assumes | Connection deepens when people practise, in games where little is at risk, noticing and revealing their present experience and receiving each other's. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, attention awareness, creativity play |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/interlocutors.md` |
| Sources | https://authenticrelating.co/wp-content/uploads/2019/08/The-Five-Practices-%E2%80%93-FINAL-DESIGN-1.pdf; https://authenticrelating.co/our-story/; https://www.authrev.org/Relating-Games-Manual; https://circlingguide.com/history-of-circling/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *welcome everything* | the first practice: turning toward every thought, emotion, sensation, and judgment that arises, without suppressing or rejecting it |
| *reveal your experience* | saying what is happening in you instead of accusing the other or withholding |
| *own your experience* | treating your feelings as real and as yours, without holding someone else wholly responsible for them |
| *honor self and other* | keeping one's own boundaries while listening to and caring for the other's experience |

#### Circle of Trust

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Parker J. Palmer and the Center for Courage & Renewal |
| Date | late 1990s onward (The Courage to Teach, 1997; A Hidden Wholeness, 2004) |

| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A trained facilitator gathers people in a circle under agreed touchstones and offers a poem or story as a 'third thing' to reflect on, and participants speak and listen without fixing, saving, advising, or correcting one another, asking only honest, open questions. |
| What it assumes | Each person has an inner teacher that speaks when a group creates safe conditions and refrains from fixing or advising. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic. Rooted in Quaker practice but offered in secular settings such as schools and professions |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, meaning purpose, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | talk, silence, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |

|---|---|
| *touchstones* | the circle's agreed guidelines, such as extending welcome and not fixing, saving, advising, or correcting each other |
| *honest, open questions* | questions to which the asker cannot know the answer and which carry no hidden advice |
| *third things* | poems, stories, or images placed in the middle of the group so that people can approach their own truth indirectly |
| *inner teacher* | each person's own inward guide, which the circle is meant to help the person hear |

#### Circling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Guy Sengstock and Jerry Candelaria (the Arete Experience); independently Decker Cunov and Bryan Bayer; later schools include the Circling Institute, Circling Europe (Sean Wilkinson and John Thompson), and the Integral Center in Boulder (Robert MacNaughton and Decker Cunov) |
| Date | late 1990s (name first used 1998) |
| Scale | a small group, two people as equals |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A small group, led by a facilitator who also takes part, turns its attention to one person or to the relating among the members, and members say what they notice and feel in the present moment, including the other's effect on them, with the stated aim of understanding each other's worlds. |
| What it assumes | People come to know themselves and each other when a group relates openly in the present moment and says how it experiences one another. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |

| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/interlocutors.md` |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *getting each other's worlds* | the explicit aim of understanding how another person experiences life from the inside |
| *un-withheld connection* | present-moment relating in which participants say what they are experiencing instead of holding it back |
| *being with* | attending to another person without an agenda to change them, as the corpus names it in its list of presence practices |

#### Clearness committee

|---|---|
| Originators | Religious Society of Friends (Quakers); described for wider use by Parker J. Palmer |
| Date | traditional, undated |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | A person facing a concern or dilemma meets with a small committee in a meeting held as a form of worship; the members help the person find the answer within, by listening and asking questions rather than offering advice or guidance. |
| What it assumes | The person seeking clarity already has the answer, and others help best by listening and asking rather than advising. |
| Limits and critiques | The committee deliberately gives no advice or guidance, even when the person might want it. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|

| *inner light* | the Quaker principle that the light of God is present in every person, which is why the answer is sought within |
| *focus person* | in Parker Palmer's description, the person whose question the committee serves |

|---|---|

| What it assumes | When each person speaks in turn and is listened to fully, a group reaches connection and shared understanding. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, group process, memory past |
| Medium | talk, ritual object, silence |

| *fishbowl form* | a council format in which an inner circle speaks while others listen from an outer circle |

#### Definitional ceremony and outsider-witness practice

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Michael White, drawing on the anthropologist Barbara Myerhoff |
| Date | 1990s (White's texts of 1995, 1997, and 2000; Myerhoff 1982 and 1986) |
| Scale | a small group, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A person tells the story of their life to a therapist while outsider witnesses listen; the witnesses then retell what struck them, naming the expressions that caught their attention, the images these evoked, why these resonated with their own lives, and how they have been moved; finally the person responds to what they heard in the retelling. |
| What it assumes | Identity takes shape before audiences, so having one's preferred story heard and retold by witnesses makes it more real and available to the teller. |
| Limits and critiques | Witnesses are asked not to applaud, judge, give opinions, or hold up their own lives as moral examples, so the practice withholds both praise and advice. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, memory past, relationship present, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://dulwichcentre.com.au/michael-white-workshop-notes.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *definitional ceremony* | a structured occasion, named by Barbara Myerhoff, in which people tell the stories of their lives before an audience that acknowledges them |
| *outsider witness* | a listener invited to hear the telling and then retell parts of it within a tradition of acknowledgment |
| *retelling* | the layered response in which witnesses tell back what they heard and the person then responds to that retelling |
| *acknowledging transport* | the witness saying how they have been moved and where the story has taken them in their own life |

| What it assumes | Speaking from one's own experience and being listened to deeply by a community supports mindfulness and mutual understanding. |

| *sangha* | the community of practitioners |
| *bell of mindfulness* | a bell whose sound invites everyone to stop and return attention to their breathing |

#### Dream appreciation group (experiential dream group)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Montague Ullman |
| Date | late 20th century (Appreciating Dreams: A Group Approach, 1996) |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A dreamer tells a recent dream; group members then talk about it as if it were their own dream, offering feelings and meanings as their own projections, and the dreamer, who decides at each stage whether to go on, responds and later enters a dialogue with the group. |
| What it assumes | A dream's meaning belongs to the dreamer, and the projections of a safe group help the dreamer find connections without an expert interpreting. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic. A psychoanalyst's group method in which the dreamer, not an expert, keeps control of the meaning |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, emotion, creativity play, identity self |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montague_Ullman; general knowledge, unverified (the stages and the phrase 'If it were my dream') |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *If it were my dream* | the phrasing group members use so that every interpretation is offered as their own projection |
| *dream appreciation* | Ullman's name for exploring a dream in a group without imposing an interpretation |

#### Dynamic Facilitation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jim Rough |
| Date | early 1980s |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The facilitator works without an agenda, follows whoever has energy to speak, and writes each contribution on one of four charts (problem-statements, solutions, concerns, and data), making sure each person feels fully heard; when the group's thinking shifts, the facilitator records the conclusions it has reached. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when every person is heard and appreciated without the constraint of an agenda, because creative agreement can then arise in the group itself. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, decision choice, group process, creativity play |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://partizipation.at/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/dynamicfacilitation-overview-jimrough.pdf; https://www.wisedemocracy.org/2-dynamic-facilitation.html; https://www.co-intelligence.org/P-dynamicfacilitation.html |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *choice-creating* | Jim Rough's name for a quality of talking and thinking in which people pull together on a difficult issue and experience creative shifts. |
| *the four charts* | Flip charts for Solutions, Concerns, Data, and Problem-statements, on which the facilitator records every contribution. |
| *breakthrough* | A shift in the group, such as a new idea, a new sense of what the real problem is, or a change of heart. |
| *purge* | The early phase in which participants say what they came wanting to say before new thinking begins. |

#### Encounter group

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Rogers and colleagues at the Western Behavioral Sciences Institute and the Center for Studies of the Person, La Jolla, within the wider encounter movement |
| Date | 1960s-1970s (Carl Rogers on Encounter Groups, 1970) |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | A small group meets intensively, often over a weekend or longer, with a facilitator who listens and takes part as a person rather than directing, and members speak openly about their feelings toward themselves and one another. |
| What it assumes | When a group is accepting and open, it moves toward more honest relating, and its members change through that relating. |
| Limits and critiques | A 1971 study found that 9 percent of student participants in encounter groups developed psychological problems lasting at least six months, mostly in groups led by authoritarian and charismatic leaders who used emotional attacks. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, emotion, identity self, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | `notes/kurt lewin.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Rogers; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-groups |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *encounter* | direct, open meeting between members, person to person |
| *facilitator* | Rogers's term for the group's leader, who listens and participates rather than directing |

#### Family Constellations (systemic constellations)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Bert Hellinger |
| Date | late 20th century |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | In front of a group, a client chooses people to represent members of their family and places them in the room; the facilitator then guides the arrangement, drawing on what the representatives report feeling, toward a configuration the facilitator considers resolved. |
| What it assumes | The method holds that present difficulties can come from unresolved traumas in earlier generations of a family and are eased when the family's order is restored in the enactment. |
| Limits and critiques | It is widely described as pseudoscientific, has been criticized for failing psychiatric ethical standards on contract, diagnosis, supervision, and confidentiality, and its positive effects are attributed to suggestion, empathy, and placebo. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. This is the practitioners' own framing; the method's claims are widely described as pseudoscientific |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | relationship absent, memory past, trauma healing, grief |
| Medium | enactment role, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Constellations |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *representatives* | group members chosen by the client to stand in for family members |
| *entanglements* | the method's name for unresolved issues or traumas from earlier generations that are said to affect present family members |
| *orders of love* | Hellinger's rules about belonging and precedence in a family, which the method claims must be restored |

#### Family reconstruction and family sculpting (Satir)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Virginia Satir |
| Date | 1960s-1980s (Conjoint Family Therapy, 1964; Peoplemaking, 1972) |
| Scale | a small group, three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | In family sculpting, family members, or group members standing in for them, are placed in postures and positions that show how the family relates; in family reconstruction, a guide has group members role-play a person's family across generations so that the person can see earlier family life and relate to their parents differently. |
| What it assumes | The presenting problem is seldom the real problem; how people cope with it is, and seeing family patterns enacted makes new ways of relating possible. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. Satir combined family systems thinking with a humanistic, experiential stance |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, relationship absent, relationship present, emotion |
| Medium | enactment role, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Satir; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *family sculpting* | arranging people's bodies in space and posture to show the relationships in a family |
| *family reconstruction* | a guided group process in which role players enact a person's family of origin across generations |
| *coping* | Satir's point that how people cope with an issue, rather than the issue itself, creates the problem |

#### Fishbowl conversation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Classroom and group-facilitation practice; no originator found |
| Date | undated |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |

| What happens | A few people, traditionally five, talk in an inner circle of chairs while everyone else listens from outer circles; in the open form one inner chair is left empty so that any listener can join, and a current speaker then leaves. |
| What it assumes | Separating a small speaking group from a listening audience, with a way to move between the two, lets many people follow and join one conversation. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |

| What it assumes | Heightened present awareness, enacted with the leader in front of witnesses, lets unfinished feelings be completed. |

| Mental model | Gestalt |

#### Group analysis (group-analytic psychotherapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | S. H. Foulkes |
| Date | 1940s |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A carefully composed group talks freely without a set agenda while the therapist, called the conductor, takes a less intrusive role than a director, so that the group's own communication and relationships become the means of treatment. |
| What it assumes | Deep and lasting change can occur within a carefully formed group whose membership reflects the wider norms of society, because the group itself is the agent of treatment. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, group process, identity self, emotion |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |

#### Group relations study group (Tavistock method)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Wilfred Bion; the Tavistock Institute's group relations programme, including the Leicester Conference |
| Date | 1940s (Bion's group papers, collected 1961); Tavistock group relations programme afterward |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | A small study group meets with a staff member, called the consultant, whose only task is to comment on what the group as a whole is doing, for example when it behaves as if its purpose were to depend on a leader, to fight or flee, or to pair off, instead of doing its stated work. |
| What it assumes | Groups tend, without knowing it, to act on shared assumptions that divert them from their task, and members learn about authority and group life when these are named as they happen. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | group process, justice power, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilfred_Bion; https://www.tavinstitute.org/leicester-conference; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tavistock_Institute; general knowledge, unverified (the staff role called consultant) |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *work group* | the aspect of a group's functioning that is directed at its primary task |
| *basic assumptions* | Bion's three unspoken group states that divert a group from its task: dependency, fight-flight, and pairing |
| *primary task* | the work a group exists to do |

| What it assumes | Hearing voices is a meaningful, though sometimes distressing, human experience that people can explore and learn to live with, regaining some power over their lives. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics worry that the movement may discourage treatment, supporters see it as complementing clinical care, and professionals surveyed in 2021 were positive but uncertain about the evidence. |
