#### Impro (Keith Johnstone's improvisation teaching)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Keith Johnstone |
| Date | 1970s (Impro, 1979) |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The teacher runs improvisation exercises in which students accept each other's offers, play deliberately high and low status, and are told to be obvious rather than clever, while the teacher takes the blame for any failure so that the students are less afraid. |
| What it assumes | Spontaneity is blocked by fear and by the effort to be original, and it returns when a teacher removes the fear of failure and players attend to what their partner offers. |
| Limits and critiques | The vault's reading found that the teacher-takes-the-failure device depends on a teacher physically present in the room and carries over to a book only as a structural analogue. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, relationship present, learning skill |
| Medium | enactment role, talk, movement dance |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/keith johnstone.md` |
| Sources | the vault, archive/reading — the facilitated room's roots (lewin 1946, johnstone's impro).md; the vault, `notes/keith johnstone.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *status* | the continual raising and lowering of one's standing relative to another person, which Johnstone says is implied by every movement and inflection |
| *offer* | anything one player says or does that a partner can build on |
| *be obvious* | Johnstone's instruction to say the most obvious thing, because the effort to be original blocks spontaneity |

#### Interpersonal group psychotherapy (Yalom)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Irvin D. Yalom |
| Date | 1970 (The Theory and Practice of Group Psychotherapy) |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The therapist builds a cohesive group and repeatedly turns the members' attention to how they are relating to one another in the meeting, so that each person receives feedback and learns how they affect others. |
| What it assumes | Group members change through the relationships formed in the group, supported by factors such as hope, universality, and cohesiveness. |
| Limits and critiques | Evidence of good outcomes is weaker for some groups of patients, for example people with borderline personality disorder. |
| Mental model | other. Interpersonal: members change by learning, inside the group, how they relate to others; existential factors are among its listed mechanisms |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, group process, emotion, identity self |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_psychotherapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *therapeutic factors* | Yalom's list of the ways group therapy helps, including universality, instillation of hope, altruism, cohesiveness, catharsis, and interpersonal learning |
| *universality* | members discovering that others share experiences they thought were theirs alone |
| *interpersonal learning* | gaining self-awareness through the feedback of other members |
| *cohesiveness* | the members' sense of belonging to and being accepted by the group |

#### Lewis Method of Deep Democracy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Myrna Lewis and Greg Lewis, building on Arnold Mindell's concept of deep democracy |
| Date | 1990s (South Africa, first at the utility Eskom) |
| Scale | a small group, an organization, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | When a group makes a decision, the facilitator gathers all views including contradictory ones, invites people who share any part of a dissenting 'no' to say so, takes a majority vote, and then asks the minority what it would need in order to go along, adding that to the decision. |
| What it assumes | A minority's dissent usually carries information the majority needs, so decisions improve when dissent is brought out and included. |
| Mental model | process-oriented. An organizational application of the Process Work lineage of Arnold and Amy Mindell |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, conflict, group process, justice power |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_democracy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *spread the no* | inviting others who partly share a dissenting view to say so, so that the dissent does not rest with one person |
| *adding the wisdom of the minority* | asking the outvoted minority what it needs in order to support the decision and including it |
| *deep democracy* | Mindell's term, from 1992, for giving voice to all positions, including unpopular and unspoken ones |

#### Men's integration group (I-Group, ManKind Project)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | The ManKind Project, founded by Rich Tosi, Bill Kauth, and Ron Hering |
| Date | 1984 (organization founded) |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | About six men who have completed the organization's initiation weekend meet regularly, often weekly, and talk in a series of rounds so that each man is heard. |
| What it assumes | Men sustain personal change through a regular peer group in which each is heard, after a shared initiation. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics, including an anti-cult advocate, alleged coercive practices during the weekend, such as limiting sleep and diet and requiring secrecy; a wrongful-death lawsuit filed in 2007 after a participant's suicide was settled in 2008. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological. The groups follow a weekend the organization frames as a modern male initiation |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, emotion, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ManKind_Project |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *I-Group* | integration group, the ongoing men's group that follows the initiation weekend |
| *rounds* | turns in which each man speaks in order while the others listen |
| *New Warrior Training Adventure* | the organization's 48-hour initiation weekend that precedes the groups |

#### Milan systemic family therapy (circular questioning)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Mara Selvini Palazzoli, Luigi Boscolo, Gianfranco Cecchin, and Giuliana Prata |
| Date | 1971 (team founded); Paradox and Counterparadox, 1978 in English |
| Scale | a small group, three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A therapist interviews the whole family while a team observes, forming and testing hypotheses about the family's patterns and asking circular questions, such as asking one member how two others get along, so that relationships and differences become visible; the team then gives the family a message or prescription. |
| What it assumes | Symptoms belong to patterns of relationship and belief in the whole family, and questions that make those patterns visible can change them. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. Based on Gregory Bateson's cybernetics and the work of the Mental Research Institute in Palo Alto |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, thought belief, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_Selvini_Palazzoli; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; general knowledge, unverified (the observing team and the example question) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *hypothesizing* | forming a working explanation of the family's pattern before and during the session, to be tested and revised |
| *circular questioning* | asking family members about the relationships and differences among the others, so that each answer shows the system rather than one person |
| *neutrality* | the therapist's stance of not taking the side of any member or position |
| *counterparadox* | a therapeutic prescription that answers the family's paradoxical pattern with a paradox of its own |

#### Nominal group technique

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Andre Delbecq and Andrew Van de Ven (later with David Gustafson) |
| Date | 1971 |
| Scale | a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Members first write their ideas alone in silence; each then gives one idea at a time in turn while the facilitator records them without debate; the group clarifies the ideas; and finally each member ranks or votes privately and the totals decide. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from structuring a meeting so that every member contributes equally and status or strong personalities do not steer the result. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics find it mechanical and inflexible, with little room for spontaneity or for ideas to build on one another. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, task work, group process |
| Medium | writing, talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_group_technique |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *silent generation* | The first step, in which each person writes ideas independently. |
| *round-robin* | Taking turns so that each member offers one idea at a time until all are recorded. |
| *voting or ranking* | The final step, in which members privately rank the ideas and the highest total wins. |

#### Open Dialogue (network meetings)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jaakko Seikkula, Birgitta Alakare, and colleagues in Western Lapland, Finland, building on the need-adapted treatment of Yrjö Alanen and colleagues |
| Date | 1980s |
| Scale | a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Soon after a crisis, a team meets the person together with family and friends in a network meeting with no preplanned themes; the professionals respond to what each person says, share their own reflections openly in the meeting, and delay conclusions while a shared understanding forms. |
| What it assumes | Psychosis can be a way of dealing with a terrifying experience that has no words yet, and open dialogue among the person's network builds a language for it. |
| Limits and critiques | Its first five principles concern how a whole service is organized, so using the conversational method alone does not reproduce the approach. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, meaning purpose, emotion |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Dialogue; general knowledge, unverified (the team's reflecting in the meeting) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *network meeting* | a treatment meeting that includes the person, their family and friends, and the professionals |
| *tolerating uncertainty* | delaying conclusions and decisions while meaning is built together |
| *dialogism* | the principle that treatment happens through responsive dialogue that builds a shared language for the experience |

#### Playback Theatre

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jonathan Fox and Jo Salas, with the original Playback Theatre company |
| Date | 1975 |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | An audience member, the teller, comes to an empty chair beside the emcee and tells a personal story, and a row of actors with a musician immediately acts it out with mime, music, and spoken scenes while the teller watches; another teller then follows. |
| What it assumes | Seeing one's own story enacted and witnessed honors the experience and connects the people who watch it. |
| Limits and critiques | Its founders' tradition presents it as not primarily a therapeutic technique, although it is used in therapeutic settings. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | memory past, creativity play, relationship present, identity self |
| Medium | enactment role, voice music, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playback_theatre; https://www.playbackcentre.org/2022/resources/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *teller* | the audience member who tells a personal story and then watches it enacted |
| *emcee* | the host who sits beside the empty chair, asks the teller about the story, and hands it to the actors |
| *playback* | the immediate enactment of the told story by the actors, in a ritualized sequence |

#### Psychodrama (with sociometry)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jacob L. Moreno and Zerka Toeman Moreno |
| Date | 1920s-1940s (Moreno's methods in print by 1934) |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | After a warm-up, one group member, the protagonist, enacts scenes from their life, memory, or imagination on a stage, with other members playing the people involved, under a director who uses techniques such as role reversal and doubling; the session ends with sharing, in which members say how the enactment related to their own lives. |
| What it assumes | Enacting a situation in a setting where, unlike life, every step can be corrected lets a person develop new responses. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Moreno's theory of spontaneity: a person can find new responses to old situations by acting them out |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | memory past, relationship absent, emotion, trauma healing, creativity play |
| Medium | enactment role, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/the facilitated room has several independent roots and improv's distinction is theorizing play as craft.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychodrama; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociometry; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_psychotherapy; the vault, archive/reading — the routing surfaces recounted (speaking being, moreno, the scharmer count).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *protagonist* | the person whose issue is enacted |
| *auxiliary ego* | a group member who plays a person or part in the protagonist's scene |
| *role reversal* | the protagonist plays another person in the scene while someone else plays the protagonist |
| *doubling* | a person beside the protagonist voices thoughts or feelings the protagonist has not expressed |
| *sociometry* | Moreno's method of mapping the choices group members make of one another, drawn as a sociogram |

#### Reflecting team (reflecting process)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Tom Andersen and colleagues, Tromsø, Norway |
| Date | 1980s |
| Scale | a small group, three people |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Partway through a session, the family and therapist listen while a team of professionals who have been observing talk among themselves, tentatively and in front of the family, about what they heard; the family then comments on the team's conversation. |
| What it assumes | People find new meanings when they can listen to others talk about their situation without having to respond, and then choose what to take from it. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. A development of systemic family therapy toward dialogue; it influenced Open Dialogue and narrative practice |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|

#### Restorative Circles

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Dominic Barter with young residents of favelas in Rio de Janeiro |
| Date | mid-1990s |
| Scale | a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A community member guides a meeting of the three parties to a conflict, those who acted, those directly affected, and members of the wider community, toward mutual understanding, each person's self-responsibility, and agreed actions that repair harm. |
| What it assumes | Conflict belongs to a community, and bringing all three parties together with shared power allows harm to be repaired and relationships restored. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, justice power, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *three parties* | those who acted, those directly affected, and the wider community, all of whom take part |
| *mutual understanding* | each party hearing and being heard by the others about what happened and what it meant |
| *self-responsibility* | each participant taking ownership of their own part in the conflict |
| *agreed actions* | steps the participants commit to in order to repair harm and restore relationships |

#### Structural family therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Salvador Minuchin |
| Date | 1970s (Families and Family Therapy, 1974) |
| Scale | a small group, three people |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | The therapist first joins the family, then maps its subsystems and boundaries, has members act out their usual interactions in the session, and deliberately unbalances the pattern to change how the family is organized. |
| What it assumes | A symptom is maintained by the family's organization, so changing boundaries and hierarchy in the family changes the problem. |
| Limits and critiques | Feminist critics argue that the concept of enmeshment may reflect male standards of selfhood and treat women's ways of relating as pathology. |
| Mental model | systemic and family |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, group process, conflict |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/salvador minuchin.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_family_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; the vault, `notes/salvador minuchin.md`; general knowledge, unverified (the definition of unbalancing) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *joining* | the therapist accommodating to the family's style and building a working relationship with its members before trying to change anything |
| *boundaries* | the rules about who takes part in which interactions, ranging from enmeshed, meaning too diffuse, to rigid |
| *subsystems* | organized parts of the family, such as the parents or the children, with their own roles and expectations |
| *unbalancing* | the therapist temporarily supporting one member or subsystem in order to shift the family's pattern |

#### T-group (training group, sensitivity training)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kurt Lewin and his associates Leland Bradford, Ronald Lippitt, and Kenneth Benne; National Training Laboratories |
| Date | 1946-1947 |
| Scale | a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | About eight to fifteen people meet with a trainer and no explicit agenda, structure, or stated goal, so that the group's own behavior becomes its subject; members share their emotional reactions to one another as feedback and learn about themselves and about group dynamics. |
| What it assumes | People learn most about themselves and about groups by examining, with feedback, the group they are in while it is happening. |
| Limits and critiques | Concerns about psychological harm followed the method, although a 1975 review found no evidence that harms were more frequent than among comparable people not in groups. |
| Mental model | learning theory. Experiential learning in a laboratory group that studies itself, from Lewin's action research |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | group process, relationship present, learning skill, emotion |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | `notes/kurt lewin.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-groups; the vault, `notes/kurt lewin.md`; the vault, archive/reading — the facilitated room's roots (lewin 1946, johnstone's impro).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *here and now* | attention to what is happening among the members during the meeting itself |
| *feedback* | telling another member one's emotional reaction to their behavior, rather than a judgment or conclusion about them |
| *laboratory* | the setting, usually residential, in which a group studies its own process while it happens |

#### The Circle Way (PeerSpirit circle)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Christina Baldwin and Ann Linnea |

| Scale | a small group, an organization, a community or public |

| What happens | People sit in a circle with the group's purpose placed in the centre; the gathering opens with a welcome and a check-in round, the group states its agreements, a volunteer guardian of process can pause the conversation and call for reflection, and the circle closes with a check-out. |
| What it assumes | When people sit where everyone can see and hear everyone and agree on how to speak and listen, meaning and wisdom emerge from the whole group. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |

| *guardian of process* | the participant who can pause the action and call the group to reflect |
| *a leader in every chair* | the principle that leadership is shared among all participants |

#### The Game (Synanon)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Synanon, founded by Charles Dederich |
| Date | 1958 onward |
| Scale | a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | In a group session, one member talks about themselves and then receives intense, often harsh and profane criticism from peers; outside the Game members were required to treat one another civilly. |
| What it assumes | The method held that harsh confrontation by peers exposes a person's self-deceptions and so changes them. |
| Limits and critiques | It grew into a 72-hour version that Dederich acknowledged as brainwashing and was used to pressure members into submission, abortions, vasectomies, and violence; it is included as a documented case of harm. |
| Mental model | other. Confrontational attack therapy inside a residential addiction community |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, relationship present, emotion |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synanon |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the Game* | Synanon's confrontational group session |
| *attack therapy* | the description later applied to therapy that works through harsh confrontation |

#### Theater games (Spolin)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Viola Spolin, building on the recreational games of Neva Boyd |
| Date | mid-20th century (Improvisation for the Theater, 1963) |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The leader sets a game with a single point of concentration and calls short instructions to the players while they play, without approving or disapproving of what they do, so that their attention stays on the shared problem and off themselves. |
| What it assumes | Spontaneity and creative expression appear when players are absorbed in a shared problem and released from the need for approval. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, attention awareness, learning skill |
| Medium | enactment role, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
