| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *point of concentration* | the single focus a game gives the players, which keeps their attention off themselves |
| *side-coaching* | brief instructions the leader calls out during play to keep players on the focus |
| *approval/disapproval* | the evaluating responses that Spolin treated as the enemy of spontaneity |

#### Twelve-step meeting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Alcoholics Anonymous, founded by Bill Wilson and Robert Smith |
| Date | 1935 |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Members meet regularly and take turns sharing their own experience while others refrain from interrupting or commenting, and newer members work through the twelve steps with an experienced member called a sponsor. |
| What it assumes | People recover from addiction through mutual support among peers with the same problem and through working a spiritual program of steps. |
| Limits and critiques | Its effectiveness is debated and some critics object to its spiritual emphasis, although a 2020 Cochrane review found more sustained abstinence with AA participation than with cognitive behavioral therapy. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic. A mutual-aid spiritual program built on a Higher Power that each member understands in their own way |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | identity self, spirit ultimate, emotion |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-step_program |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sharing* | a member speaking about their own experience during the meeting |
| *cross talk* | interrupting or commenting on another member's share, which meetings discourage |
| *sponsor* | an experienced member who guides a newer member through the steps in a one-to-one, non-hierarchical relationship |
| *Higher Power* | the program's term for a power greater than oneself, understood in each member's own way |
| *Twelve Traditions* | the principles that govern the groups, including anonymity |

### A large group

This scale is the main one for 21 techniques, and the vault has met 4 of them.

#### 1-2-4-All

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless (Liberating Structures) |
| Date | 2000s |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | In about fifteen minutes, each person first reflects silently and writes ideas on a question, then shares and builds on them with one partner, then two pairs join as a group of four, and finally a few groups report key points to the whole room. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from letting every person form a view before the group speaks, so that ideas from quiet members reach the whole room. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | group process, thought belief, creativity play |
| Medium | writing, talk, silence |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://www.liberatingstructures.com/1-2-4-all; https://www.liberatingstructures.com/history-of-ls |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *individual reflection* | The first step, in which each person thinks and writes alone in silence before anyone speaks. |
| *form quartet* | The step in which two pairs join to refine and differentiate their ideas. |
| *all-together sharing* | The last step, in which a few groups share key points with the whole room. |

#### 25/10 Crowd Sourcing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless (Liberating Structures) |
| Date | 2000s |
| Scale | a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Each person writes their boldest idea and a first step on a card; people walk around swapping cards without reading them, and in each of five rounds each person rates the card in hand from one to five on its back, after which the cards with the highest totals are read out as the group's top ten. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from letting a whole group rate each other's ideas anonymously, so that the ideas that resonate across the room rise above those of the loudest members. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, decision choice, future vision |
| Medium | writing, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.liberatingstructures.com/25-10-crowdsourcing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *bold idea* | The most ambitious idea a person would propose, written on a card with a first step. |
| *countdown* | The closing step in which the facilitator calls out totals from twenty-five downward to find the top ten ideas. |

#### Appreciative Inquiry Summit

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Diana Whitney and David Cooperrider; James Ludema, Bernard Mohr, and Thomas Griffin |
| Date | c. 2000 |
| Scale | a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A large gathering of an organization's members and stakeholders, sometimes hundreds of people, moves through the four phases together over several days: people interview one another in pairs about the system at its best, imagine a future, design the structures to carry it, and form groups to act on the design. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a whole system inquiring into its own best moments, because the questions people ask and the pictures of the future they hold influence what they do next. |
| Limits and critiques | Gervase Bushe's review reports that energy is high after the design phase but implementation is often uneven, and that a focus on positive stories can invalidate participants' negative experiences. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | future vision, group process, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.gervasebushe.ca/AITC.pdf; https://civicus.org/documents/toolkits/PGX_B_Appreciative%20Inquiry%20Summit.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *appreciative interview* | A paired interview in which each person tells a story of the system at its best. |
| *positive core* | The strengths, resources, and best experiences of the organization, which the summit gathers and builds on. |
| *4-D cycle* | The phases of Discovery, Dream, Design, and Destiny through which the summit moves. |

#### Art of Hosting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Toke Paludan Møller, Monica Nissen, and colleagues in Denmark; now an open, worldwide community of practice |
| Date | late 1990s |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Hosts design a gathering around a purpose and a few questions, choose among participatory methods such as circle, World Café, Open Space, Appreciative Inquiry, and Pro Action Café, and plan from the start how the results will be recorded and shared; they practice being present and taking part themselves before they host others. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from well-hosted conversations about what matters most to the people present, because people commit their energy to what matters to them. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | group process, relationship present, task work |
| Medium | talk, silence, writing, art image |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `the-conversation-catalog.md` |
| Sources | https://artofhosting.org/what-is-aoh/; https://www.chriscorrigan.com/parkinglot/a-simple-way-to-explore-the-four-fold-practice-of-the-art-of-hosting/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *four-fold practice* | The pattern that people have meaningful conversations when they are present, when they participate, when they are hosted, and when they co-create something; hosts practice each in turn. |
| *harvest* | The planned recording and sharing of what a conversation produced, such as a plan, an agreement, or a better relationship, designed from the start. |
| *conversations that matter* | Conversations about questions the participants care about, which the practice exists to host. |

#### Bohm Dialogue

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Bohm |
| Date | 1980s–1990s |
| Scale | a large group, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Twenty to forty people sit in a circle and talk freely for hours or over several meetings, with no agenda and no decisions to make; each person tries to suspend their assumptions and reactions, neither acting on them nor suppressing them, so that the group can see how its thinking works. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a group attends to thought itself as a shared process, so that assumptions that usually operate unseen become visible. |
| Limits and critiques | Wikipedia notes that later practice rarely keeps Bohm's recommended group size and has departed considerably from his approach. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, attention awareness, relationship present, group process |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | archive/reading — bohm, on dialogue (1996).md |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohm_Dialogue |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *suspension* | Holding one's assumptions and reactions in view without acting on them or suppressing them, so that they can be seen. |
| *dialogue* | In Bohm's use, meaning flowing among and through the participants, as opposed to discussion, in which each side tries to win. |
| *shared meaning* | The common understanding that emerges when a group attends to its thinking together. |

#### Future Search

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Marvin Weisbord and Sandra Janoff |
| Date | 1990s |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | People from every part of a system meet for five half-day sessions over three days: they build time lines of the past, map present trends, report what their stakeholder group is proud and sorry about, describe a desired future as if it had already happened, and then confirm the common ground everyone shares and plan action on it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a whole system sees the same large picture, finds what its members already agree on, and takes responsibility for acting on it. |
| Limits and critiques | The method treats problems and conflicts as information rather than as items to work through, so it builds only on what participants already share. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | future vision, group process, decision choice, memory past |
| Medium | talk, writing, art image |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://futuresearch.net/about/; https://futuresearch.net/about/methodology/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *whole system in the room* | The principle of inviting people with authority, resources, expertise, information, and need related to the issue. |
| *common ground* | The future aims that everyone present agrees on, which become the basis for action. |
| *prouds and sorries* | Each stakeholder group reports what it is proud of and sorry about in its own conduct on the issue. |
| *mind map* | A whole-group chart of the present trends affecting the issue, which the group then weights by importance. |

#### Graphic facilitation and graphic recording

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Sibbet (The Grove Consultants International) and other early practitioners such as Geoff Ball |
| Date | 1970s (The Grove founded 1977) |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A practitioner draws the group's conversation as it happens on large paper or a wall, using words, symbols, and diagrams; a graphic facilitator also uses the drawing to lead the group through its work, while a graphic recorder draws with little interaction with the group. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a group can see its own thinking laid out in one picture, which helps it notice patterns and remember what it decided. |
| Mental model | other. Visual thinking: holds that a shared picture supports a group's learning and memory |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | group process, thought belief, memory past |
| Medium | art image, writing, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphic_facilitation; https://www.thegrove.com/about |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *graphic recording* | Drawing what is said in real time, usually with little interaction with the group. |
| *graphic facilitation* | Using diagrams, symbols, and writing to guide a group toward its goals during a meeting. |
| *explicit group memory* | The title of Geoff Ball's early paper, which argued that a shared picture is the best support for a group's learning and lasting memory. |
| *visual meetings* | Meetings run with visual techniques, a service and term used by The Grove. |

#### Group relations conference (Tavistock or Leicester model)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Tavistock Institute of Human Relations, drawing on Wilfred Bion's group theory; developed by A. K. Rice, Eric Miller, and colleagues |
| Date | 1957 (first Leicester Conference) |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Members spend several days in events such as small and large study groups whose only task is to study their own behavior as it happens; staff consultants take no leadership role in the usual sense and offer working hypotheses about what the group is doing, especially around authority, leadership, and boundaries. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from learning, through direct experience, how unconscious group processes shape the exercise of authority and leadership. |
| Mental model | psychodynamic |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | group process, justice power, identity self, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.akriceinstitute.org/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilfred_Bion |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *basic assumption* | Bion's name for an unspoken shared belief, such as dependency, fight-flight, or pairing, that a group acts on instead of its task. |
| *work group* | The side of a group's functioning that is directed at its primary task. |
| *here and now* | The rule that members study what is happening among them in the present moment, as it happens. |
| *large study group* | An event in which the whole conference membership meets to study its own behavior as a large group. |

#### Large-group awareness training (est, Lifespring, the Landmark Forum)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Werner Erhard (Erhard Seminars Training) and similar programs such as Lifespring, Insight Seminars, and the Landmark Forum |
| Date | 1970s |
| Scale | a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | A trainer leads a large audience, often hundreds of people, through several consecutive long days of lectures, exercises, public sharing, and confrontation, aiming to produce a sudden shift in how participants see themselves and their lives. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from an intense shared experience in which a person suddenly recognizes and gives up habitual patterns of thought and complaint. |
| Limits and critiques | A 1982 review by Finkelstein, Wenegrat, and Yalom found only minor changes on psychological tests and anecdotal reports of psychiatric casualties among est trainees, and other studies describe stress reactions and brief psychotic episodes attributed to such trainings. |
| Mental model | other. Commercial programs of the human potential movement; the vault traces est to the T-group and encounter lineage and to speech-act philosophy |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, thought belief, emotion, meaning purpose |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/werner erhard.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large-group_awareness_training; the vault, `notes/werner erhard.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *large-group awareness training* | The researchers' name for programs that claim to increase self-awareness and bring about personal change through intensive sessions with hundreds of participants. |
| *racket* | In the est and Landmark lineage, a fixed complaint paired with a fixed payoff that a person keeps repeating. |

#### Liberating Structures

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless |
| Date | 2000s (prototypes 2001–2003; book 2014) |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A facilitator chooses one or more short, timed meeting formats from a collection of thirty-three (forty-three in the 2026 fieldbook) and runs them in sequence; each format fixes the invitation, the arrangement of space and materials, how participation is distributed, how groups are configured, and the steps and timing, so that everyone present takes part. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when the routine formats of meetings are altered so that control is distributed and every person present can contribute. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent. Draws on complexity science's account of how living systems organize themselves |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | group process, task work, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://www.liberatingstructures.com/how-ls-works-five-design-elements; https://www.liberatingstructures.com/history-of-ls |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *microstructure* | A small, easily changed feature of a meeting, such as who sits with whom and how the discussion is formatted. |
| *five design elements* | Invitation, space and materials, participation distribution, group configuration, and steps and timing, which together specify each structure. |
| *structuring invitation* | The question or challenge with which a facilitator opens a structure. |

#### Open Space Technology

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Harrison Owen |
| Date | 1985 |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants sit in a circle around a broad theme; anyone who cares about an issue writes it on a sheet, announces it, and posts it on a wall with a time and place, and everyone then signs up for and moves between the sessions they choose. The facilitator explains the principles and the one law and then stays out of the content. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from the self-organization of people who care about an issue, once a meeting gives them a theme, open time, and freedom to move. |
| Limits and critiques | Wikipedia's article notes that the open call for topics favors confident speakers, since introverts tend not to propose topics, so the agenda can be shaped by a vocal minority. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | group process, task work, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://openspaceworld.org/wp2/hho/papers/brief-users-guide-open-space-technology/; https://openspaceworld.org/wp2/hho/open-space-technology/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_space_technology |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Law of Two Feet* | If you find yourself neither learning nor contributing, you use your feet and go somewhere else; each person is responsible for their own participation. |
| *Whoever comes is the right people* | The first of four principles: the people who come to a session are the ones who care, and they are enough. |
| *When it is over, it is over* | The fourth principle: a session ends when its work is done rather than when the schedule says. |
| *Community Bulletin Board* | The wall on which each convener posts an issue with its time and place, which becomes the agenda. |
| *Village Market Place* | The period in which participants read the wall and sign up for the sessions they want. |

#### Pro Action Café

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Rainer von Leoprechting and Ria Baeck |
| Date | 2008 (reported) |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants who bring a project or question each host a table with three empty seats, and the other participants join tables of their choice for three rounds; the rounds move from the question behind the host's question, to what is missing, to what the host has learned, what next steps they will take, and what help they still need. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from combining Open Space's self-chosen topics with World Café's rounds of mixed conversation, so that each person's project gets focused help from peers. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | task work, decision choice, creativity play |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.chriscorrigan.com/parkinglot/the-origin-of-pro-action-cafe/; https://www.sessionlab.com/methods/pro-action-cafe |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *project host* | A participant who brings a project or question and stays at a table to receive help with it. |
| *the question behind the question* | The first round's focus on the deeper aim beneath the question the host brought. |
| *what is missing* | The second round's question, which asks what would make the host's picture more complete. |

#### Real Time Strategic Change / Whole-Scale Change

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kathleen Dannemiller, Robert W. Jacobs, and Dannemiller Tyson Associates |
| Date | 1990s |
| Scale | a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A critical mass of an organization meets for two or three days at mixed tables, each table a cross-section of the diversity in the room; together they share data about their dissatisfaction, shape a vision of the future, and agree on first steps, so that the strategy is formed in the room rather than decided by top leaders and announced. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a critical mass of an organization works on shared data, vision, and first steps at the same time, so that the whole system changes together. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | task work, future vision, group process, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://orgchangewiki.blogspot.com/2010/08/whole-scale-change.html |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *D x V x F > R* | Their change formula: dissatisfaction, a shared vision, and agreed first steps, multiplied together, must exceed resistance to change. |
| *max-mix table* | A table whose members form a cross-section of all the diversity in the room. |
| *microcosm* | A group representing the full range of levels, functions, places, and views in the organization, used to plan and run the change. |
| *one brain and one heart* | The aim that a diverse group comes to share one understanding and one commitment while each person still acts individually. |

#### Robert's Rules of Order (parliamentary procedure)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henry Martyn Robert |
| Date | 1876 |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A presiding officer takes up one question at a time: a member proposes an action by making a motion, another member seconds it, the assembly debates it and may amend it under fixed rules, and the question is then decided by vote. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from fair and predictable procedure, which lets an assembly reach its general will on many questions in little time. |
| Limits and critiques | The rules are long and complex, and some organizations see elaborate procedure as a tool for obstruction. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, conflict, group process |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert%27s_Rules_of_Order |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *motion* | A formal proposal that the assembly take an action. |
| *second* | A second member's statement that a motion should be considered. |
| *point of order* | A member's claim that the rules are not being followed, which interrupts business to be ruled on. |
| *quorum* | The minimum number of members who must be present for business to be valid. |
| *previous question* | A motion to end debate and vote at once. |

#### Samoan Circle

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Larry Aggens, a founding member of the International Association for Public Participation |
| Date | late twentieth century (exact year unconfirmed) |
| Scale | a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A few chairs, some left empty, are set in an inner circle inside a larger audience; only people sitting in the inner circle may speak, so anyone who wants to speak takes an empty chair or stands behind an occupied one to wait, and the meeting runs without a chairperson until everyone who wants to speak has spoken. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when people with opposed views on a controversial issue speak and listen to each other directly, without a moderator whom some would not trust as neutral. |
| Limits and critiques | Guidance for the method warns that the discussion can stall or be monopolized if participants allow it, and the sources consulted do not document any connection between the name and Samoan custom. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, group process, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.k-state.edu/media-communication/about/icdd/discussionguide/samoan_circles.html; https://vitalysthealth.org/wp-content/uploads/2014/02/ACT-Samoan_Circle.pdf; https://www.govloop.com/community/blog/origins-of-the-samoan-circle/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inner circle* | The small ring of chairs, often around a table with microphones, where all speaking happens. |
| *leaderless meeting* | A meeting with no chair or moderator, in which the participants as a group control the discussion. |

#### Search Conference

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Fred Emery and Eric Trist (Tavistock Institute); systematized by Merrelyn Emery |
| Date | 1959 |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A community of people who share a system first scans the changes in its wider social environment, then reviews its own history and present, drafts and integrates pictures of a most desirable future, and plans action; points still disputed after clarification are put on a Disagreed List and set aside, so the community builds only on common ground. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when the people of a system learn about their changing environment together and plan their own desirable future, which produces shared commitment to carry the plan out. |
| Limits and critiques | The method deliberately sets unresolved conflicts aside rather than working them through. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent. Rests on Fred Emery's open systems theory and its democratic design principle |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | future vision, group process, conflict, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrelyn_Emery; https://www.socialsciencethatactuallyworks.com/wp-content/uploads/documents/from-theory-to-practice/Rationalizing%20Conflict%20-%202024.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *rationalization of conflict* | Merrelyn Emery's name for handling disagreement by clarifying it and setting unresolved points aside, rather than resolving, avoiding, or voting on it. |
| *Disagreed List* | The list on which points that remain disputed after negotiation are recorded and removed from the community's further work. |
| *Most Desirable World* | The picture of a desirable future that small groups draft and the whole community integrates. |
| *L22* | Open systems notation for the wider social environment, which the first phase of the conference explores. |

#### Sociodrama

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jacob L. Moreno |
| Date | 1940s |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A director helps a group choose a shared social problem, such as prejudice or a public conflict; members act out the collective roles involved, for example by taking the role of the other side, and the whole group then discusses what the enactment showed. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from enacting a collective situation, because acting out the roles in a social conflict shows a group what talking about it does not. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Moreno's action methods, built on spontaneity and role-taking |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, justice power, relationship present, trauma healing |
| Medium | enactment role, talk, movement dance |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | `notes/the facilitated room has several independent roots and improv's distinction is theorizing play as craft.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociodrama |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sociodrama* | A dramatic play in which several people act out assigned roles in order to study and remedy problems in group or collective relationships. |
| *role reversal* | Taking the role of the person or group on the other side of a relationship and speaking from it. |
| *director* | Moreno's name for the person who runs the enactment. |

#### TRIZ (Creative Destruction)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless (Liberating Structures), adapting TRIZ, a Russian engineering approach |
| Date | 2000s |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Groups first list everything they could do to produce the worst possible result for their work, then go through the list asking which of these things they are already doing in some form, and finally decide what to stop and the first step toward stopping it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from removing counterproductive habits, which a group names more honestly when it approaches them through a playful worst case. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | task work, group process, creativity play |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.liberatingstructures.com/creative-destruction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unwanted result* | The worst outcome the group can imagine for its purpose, used as the starting point. |
| *reckoning* | The step in which groups identify which current activities resemble items on their worst-case list. |
| *creative destruction* | Deliberately stopping activities in order to make room for new ones. |

#### Unconference (BarCamp)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Technology communities: the word first appeared in a 1998 announcement for an XML developers' conference; Foo Camp (O'Reilly Media, 2003) and BarCamp (2005) spread the format, drawing on Open Space Technology |
| Date | 1998–2005 |
| Scale | a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Attendees build the program at the start of the event by writing proposed sessions on a whiteboard or on sticky notes placed in a grid of times and rooms; each session is led by the person who proposed it or run as an open discussion, and every attendee is encouraged to present or contribute. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when the people attending a conference, rather than its organizers, decide what is discussed, because they know what they need to learn. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, group process, task work |
| Medium | talk, writing, technology |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unconference; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BarCamp |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *grid* | The board of time slots and rooms onto which attendees post the sessions they will lead. |
| *BarCamp* | An open, public unconference first held in Palo Alto in August 2005 as an alternative to the invitation-only Foo Camp. |

#### World Café

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Juanita Brown and David Isaacs |
| Date | 1995 |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants sit four or five to a small table and talk about a question for about twenty minutes while writing and drawing on paper tablecloths; most then move to new tables while one host stays to tell the newcomers what was said, and after three or more rounds the whole room shares the patterns it heard. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a network of small conversations whose ideas are carried from table to table until the group can see patterns that no single person held. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | group process, thought belief, future vision |
| Medium | talk, writing, art image |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://theworldcafe.com/about-us/history/; https://theworldcafe.com/key-concepts-resources/design-principles/; https://theworldcafe.com/key-concepts-resources/world-cafe-method/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *questions that matter* | The design principle that the conversation is organized around a question the participants care about. |
| *table host* | The person who stays at a table between rounds and tells the next group what happened in the previous round. |
| *harvest* | The final phase, in which insights from the tables are shared with the whole group and made visible, often by a graphic recorder. |
| *hospitable space* | The design principle of a welcoming setting in which people feel safe and respected. |

#### Worldwork

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Arnold Mindell, with Amy Mindell (Processwork) |
| Date | late 1970s–1990s |
| Scale | a large group, a community or public, an organization |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | In an open forum on a conflict, the facilitator helps the group name the sides or roles in play, invites people to speak from a role and to switch roles, and points out moments of intense feeling, differences of rank, and roles no one is voicing, so that every position and feeling in the group is expressed. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when all the roles and feelings in a group, including marginalized and unspoken ones, are expressed and the people holding them interact directly. |
| Mental model | process-oriented |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, justice power, group process, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, enactment role, breath body |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://worldwork.org/about/worldwork-deep-democracy/; http://www.aamindell.net/worldwork |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *deep democracy* | Mindell's principle that every person, every feeling, and every level of experience in a group must be represented. |
| *roles* | Viewpoints or functions in a group, such as leader or troublemaker, that different people occupy at different times. |
| *ghost role* | A role or voice that is felt or talked about but that no one present speaks for, which needs to be represented. |
| *rank* | The privilege and power one person or group holds relative to another, which the facilitator helps make visible. |
| *hot spot* | An emotional, charged moment in a group that the facilitator notices and works with. |

### An organization

This scale is the main one for 32 techniques, and the vault has met 5 of them.

#### Action research

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kurt Lewin |
| Date | 1944–1946 |
| Scale | an organization, a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Researchers and the members of a social system plan an action, carry it out, gather facts about its result, and use those facts to plan the next action, repeating the cycle so that practice and knowledge improve together. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from intervening in a system and studying what the intervention produces, because a social system shows its workings when someone tries to change it. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, group process, justice power, task work |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/kurt lewin.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_research; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_development |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *spiral of steps* | Lewin's description of action research as repeated circles of planning, action, and fact-finding about the result of the action. |
| *unfreezing, changing, refreezing* | Lewin's three phases of change: loosening old patterns, trying new behavior, and settling the new pattern. |
| *participatory action research* | A later form, advanced in Latin America by Orlando Fals-Borda, in which the people studied act as co-researchers. |

#### Advice process

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Dennis Bakke and colleagues at the energy company AES; described by Frederic Laloux in Reinventing Organizations |
| Date | late 20th century at AES; popularized 2014 |
| Scale | an organization |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Any person may make any decision after seeking advice from everyone who will be meaningfully affected and from people with expertise in the matter; the decider must take the advice into consideration but is not bound by it, and no colleague can override the decision. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from moving decisions to the people closest to an issue, while the duty to seek advice builds community, humility, and learning. |
| Limits and critiques | It is not consensus, since the decider need not follow the advice, and an organization may suspend it openly for a stated period in a crisis. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | none of the families; a single move |
| Subjects | decision choice, justice power, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://reinventingorganizationswiki.com/en/theory/decision-making/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *advice process* | The rule that anyone can make any decision after seeking advice from those affected and those with expertise. |
| *decision-maker* | Usually the person who first noticed the issue or the person most affected by it. |

#### After-action review

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | United States Army |
| Date | 1970s |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Soon after an exercise or operation, the people involved compare what was intended with what actually happened, ask why the two differed, and agree on what to keep doing and what to change next time, without assigning blame. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a team's own quick, honest comparison of plan and result, repeated after every action. |
| Limits and critiques | Assigning blame or issuing reprimands defeats the purpose of the review, so it depends on a setting where people can admit mistakes. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, task work, memory past |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After-action_review |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *intended versus actual results* | The comparison with which every review begins. |
| *the four questions* | What was supposed to happen, what actually happened, why there was a difference, and what can be learned. |

#### Agile retrospective

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Norman Kerth (Project Retrospectives, 2001); Esther Derby and Diana Larsen (Agile Retrospectives, 2006) |
| Date | 2001–2006 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | At the end of each work cycle, a team meets with a facilitator to look back on the period: it sets the stage, gathers data about what happened, generates insights about why, decides on a small number of changes to try, and closes the meeting. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a team reviewing its own recent work often and trying a few chosen improvements each time. |
| Mental model | learning theory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | group process, learning skill, task work |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pragprog.com/titles/dlret/agile-retrospectives/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrospective |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Prime Directive* | Norm Kerth's opening statement that everyone did the best job they could, given what they knew and the situation, which is read so that the review does not become a search for someone to blame. |
| *set the stage* | The first phase, which states the goal of the meeting and helps everyone begin to speak. |
| *generate insights* | The phase of asking why things happened as they did before choosing any action. |
| *iteration* | The short work cycle at whose end the retrospective is held. |

#### Appreciative Inquiry

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | David Cooperrider and Suresh Srivastva (Case Western Reserve University) |
| Date | 1987 |
| Scale | an organization, a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Instead of diagnosing problems, members of an organization choose an affirmative topic, interview one another about times the organization was at its best, imagine what could be, design the structures to support it, and act on the design. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from inquiring into what works best in a system, because the questions people ask and the pictures of the future they hold influence what they do next. |
| Limits and critiques | Scholar-practitioners reviewed by Gervase Bushe worry that a focus on positive stories can invalidate participants' negative experiences, and users often report uneven implementation after the design phase. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, meaning purpose, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry; https://www.gervasebushe.ca/AITC.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *4-D cycle* | Discovery, Dream, Design, and Destiny, the phases of an inquiry; some practitioners add a first phase, Define, to choose the focus. |
| *positive core* | An organization's strengths, resources, and best experiences, which the inquiry makes the basis for change. |
| *affirmative topic* | The focus of the inquiry, framed as what the organization wants more of rather than as a problem to fix. |
| *simultaneity principle* | The claim that inquiry and change happen at the same time, so the first question asked already begins the change. |

#### Calling the community to counsel (Rule of Saint Benedict, chapter 3)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Benedict of Nursia |
| Date | c. 530 |
| Scale | an organization, a society's customs |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | When an important matter arises, the abbot calls the whole community together, explains the matter, and hears everyone's advice, including that of the youngest; members give advice humbly without stubbornly defending their views, and the abbot then decides. For lesser matters he consults only the seniors. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from listening to every member, including the least senior, before one person decides, because insight may come from anyone. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | none of the families; a single move |
| Subjects | decision choice, spirit ultimate, justice power |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://ccel.org/ccel/benedict/rule/rule.v.html; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_Saint_Benedict |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *counsel* | The advice the abbot must seek from the community before deciding important matters. |
| *the younger* | The Rule calls everyone because 'the Lord often revealeth to the younger what is best.' |

#### Consensus decision-making (with blocks and stand-asides)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Quakers from the 17th century; spread in secular movements by groups such as the Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee and the Movement for a New Society |
| Date | 1960s–1970s (secular form) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A facilitator guides the group through discussion, a formal proposal, and a call for consensus in which each member agrees, stands aside, or blocks; the concerns raised are used to modify the proposal until no block remains, and hand signals let members show agreement without interrupting. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from decisions that every member can at least live with, which people then carry out willingly. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics point to a 'tyranny of the minority', since a block favors the existing state of affairs, to the time the process takes, and to conformity pressure that can hide real disagreement. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, conflict, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consensus_decision-making |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *block* | A member's formal objection that stops a proposal from passing, usually reserved for serious matters. |
| *stand aside* | Registering serious personal disagreement while allowing the proposal to pass. |
| *twinkling* | Wiggling the fingers to show agreement without speaking. |

#### Cynefin framework

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Dave Snowden (IBM Global Services; later the Cynefin Centre) |
| Date | 1999 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A group sorts the situations it faces into domains according to how cause and effect relate in them (clear, complicated, complex, chaotic, and confusion) and chooses a different way of acting in each; in complex situations, for example, it tries small experiments and watches what emerges. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from matching one's way of acting to the kind of situation one is in, because methods that work in ordered situations fail in complex ones. |
| Limits and critiques | Scholars have called the framework difficult and confusing and have criticized its theoretical grounding and its ambiguous terms such as 'sense' and 'categorize'. |
| Mental model | other. Complexity science and naturalistic sense-making |
| Structure | none of the families; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | decision choice, thought belief, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynefin_framework |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *probe–sense–respond* | The approach for complex situations: try small experiments, observe what emerges, then respond. |
| *sense–analyze–respond* | The approach for complicated situations, in which experts can analyze cause and effect. |
| *act–sense–respond* | The approach for chaotic situations: act first to establish order, then assess. |
| *confusion* | The central domain, also called disorder, where it is not yet clear which kind of situation one is in. |

#### Deliberately developmental organization

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Robert Kegan and Lisa Laskow Lahey, with Andy Fleming, Matthew Miller, and Deborah Helsing |
| Date | 2016 |
| Scale | an organization |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | An organization builds the development of every employee into its daily work, so that people regularly expose their weaknesses, receive frank feedback, and take on work just beyond their current ability, within a community that makes this safe. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when an organization makes adult development part of everyone's daily work rather than an occasional program. |
| Mental model | developmental |
| Structure | nested levels; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, identity self, task work, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunity_to_change |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *edge* | The drive to uncover one's weaknesses and grow past them. |
| *groove* | The daily practices through which development happens at work. |
| *home* | The supportive community that makes it safe to be vulnerable. |

#### Delphi method

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Olaf Helmer, Norman Dalkey, and Nicholas Rescher at the RAND Corporation |
| Date | 1950s–1960s |
| Scale | an organization, a text or tool, no one present |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A facilitator sends questions to a panel of experts who answer anonymously; the facilitator returns an anonymized summary of the answers and reasons, and the experts revise their answers over several rounds until the group's answers settle. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from letting experts revise their judgments in light of each other's reasoning without the social pressure of meeting face to face. |
| Limits and critiques | If the panelists are misinformed, the method may only add confidence to their ignorance, and its emphasis on consensus can limit discussion. |
| Mental model | organizational. Comes from Cold War forecasting and operations research |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, future vision, thought belief |
| Medium | writing, technology |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphi_method |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *anonymity* | Panelists' identities are not revealed, even afterward, so no one defers to reputation. |
| *iteration* | The repeated rounds of answering and revising. |
| *statistical group response* | The summary of the panel's answers, which can be updated as rounds proceed. |

#### Design thinking workshop

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | IDEO (David Kelley) and the Stanford d.school, drawing on earlier design-methods research |
| Date | 1990s–2000s |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A team studies the people it is designing for, redefines the problem from their needs, generates many ideas, builds rough prototypes, and tests them with users, repeating these steps as it learns. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from understanding users' needs at first hand and from learning by building and testing rough versions of a solution. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics such as Lee Vinsel and Natasha Iskander argue that popular design thinking borrows ideas superficially, gives confidence without real capability, and reinforces the designer's privileged position. |
| Mental model | other. Design methods and human-centered design |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, task work, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing, art image |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Design_thinking |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *needfinding* | Studying the people a design is for in order to find their actual needs. |
| *ideation* | Generating many possible ideas before judging any of them. |
| *prototype* | A quick, rough model built to learn from users' reactions. |

#### Double-loop learning

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Chris Argyris and Donald Schön |
| Date | 1970s (Theory in Practice, 1974) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, one person alone |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | When an action fails, a person or team examines not only what to do differently but also the goals, values, and assumptions that governed the choice, and changes those governing variables where needed. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from surfacing and testing the assumptions that govern action, which people usually defend without noticing. |
| Limits and critiques | Organizations often resist this kind of learning because it threatens control and raises fear of failure. |
| Mental model | learning theory. Argyris and Schön's action science |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, learning skill, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double-loop_learning |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *single-loop learning* | Correcting an error while leaving the underlying goals and rules unchanged, like a thermostat that turns on the heat when the room falls below its setting. |
| *double-loop learning* | Questioning and changing the goals and rules themselves, like a thermostat that could ask why it is set to that temperature. |
| *governing variables* | The values and assumptions that steer a person's or organization's actions and that they try to keep within limits. |
| *espoused theory and theory-in-use* | The difference between the reasons people give for their actions and the rules their actions actually follow. |

#### Ecocycle Planning

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless (Liberating Structures), building on Brenda Zimmerman and on C. S. Holling's ecological research |
| Date | 2000s |
| Scale | an organization, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Each person places the group's activities or relationships on a map of four phases (birth, maturity, creative destruction, and renewal), pairs compare their maps, the whole group builds one shared map, and small groups then decide what to do about activities caught in the two traps. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing a portfolio of activities as a cycle like that of an ecosystem, in which ending some activities is as necessary as starting others. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent. Borrows the adaptive cycle of ecosystems from ecology |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | task work, decision choice, group process, future vision |
| Medium | talk, writing, art image |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.liberatingstructures.com/ecocycle-planning |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *rigidity trap* | The place between maturity and creative destruction where a group holds on to activities that no longer add value. |
| *scarcity trap* | The place between renewal and birth where new ideas do not get enough investment to start. |
| *creative destruction* | The phase in which obsolete activities are ended and their resources recycled. |
| *renewal* | The phase of connecting creative people and preparing the conditions for new starts. |

#### Gestalt organization development (Cape Cod model)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Edwin Nevis and Sonia Nevis (Gestalt Institute of Cleveland; Gestalt International Study Center) |
| Date | 1970s–1980s (Organizational Consulting: A Gestalt Approach, 1987) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | The consultant attends closely to what they see, hear, and feel in the client system in the present moment, shares these observations to heighten the system's awareness of how it works, and supports the group as it moves from awareness through action and contact to closure. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from heightened awareness of what is happening now, because a system that sees how it works can finish what it has left incomplete and choose differently. |
| Mental model | Gestalt |
| Structure | letting something run to completion; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, group process, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, breath body |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_C._Nevis; https://www.gisc.org/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *cycle of experience* | The Gestalt sequence of sensation, awareness, mobilized energy, action, contact, and closure, used to see where a person or group gets stuck. |
| *presence* | The consultant's way of being in the room, which Nevis treated as an intervention in its own right. |

#### Holacracy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Brian Robertson (Ternary Software) |
| Date | 2007 |
| Scale | an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | An organization replaces job titles with roles held within self-organizing circles; anyone who senses a tension in their role brings it to a governance meeting or a tactical meeting, where a facilitator runs a fixed sequence of steps and the proposal is adopted unless a valid objection is raised under the written constitution. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from moving authority out of persons and into an explicit, written process that every role-holder can use to change the organization. |
| Limits and critiques | Medium abandoned it in 2016, citing problems of coordination across functions; about eighteen percent of Zappos employees left after its 2015 adoption there; and critics such as Steve Denning argue that it hides hierarchy rather than removing it. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, task work, justice power |
| Medium | talk, writing, reading text |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holacracy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *tension* | A gap a role-holder senses between how things are and how they could be, which is the starting point for change. |
| *role* | A defined purpose and set of accountabilities that a person holds, often several at once, in place of a job title. |
| *integrative decision-making* | The governance process in which a proposal is tested against objections and adjusted to integrate them. |
| *lead link and rep link* | The two roles that connect a circle with the broader circle it belongs to. |
| *constitution* | The written rulebook that defines Holacracy's roles, meetings, and processes. |

#### Ignatian communal discernment

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Ignatius of Loyola and his first companions, the founders of the Society of Jesus |
| Date | 1539 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Members pray about a question separately, then each shares in turn all the reasons they found against a proposal and, at a later meeting, all the reasons for it, without debate; the group continues praying and sharing until it reaches a decision it recognizes together. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a group seeking God's will together, with each member's reasons and inner movements treated as evidence. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, spirit ultimate, group process |
| Medium | silence, talk |
| Time | series |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *The Deliberations of Our First Fathers* | The 1539 record of how the first Jesuit companions decided together, which later communal discernment takes as its model. |
| *consolation and desolation* | Ignatius's terms for inner movements toward and away from God, to which discernment attends. |

#### Immunity to Change (immunity map)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Robert Kegan and Lisa Laskow Lahey |
| Date | 2001; 2009 |
| Scale | an organization, two people, one helping, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A person writes an improvement goal, lists what they do or fail to do that works against it, uncovers the hidden competing commitments those behaviors serve, and names the big assumptions that make the competing commitments seem necessary; they then run small, safe tests of those assumptions. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a person can look at assumptions they had taken for granted and test them, which Kegan and Lahey treat as a developmental shift rather than a new resolution. |
| Limits and critiques | Its developmental basis, Kegan's stage theory, is disputed by scholars such as Basseches and Mascolo, who prefer finer-grained accounts of how specific capacities develop. |
| Mental model | developmental |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | thought belief, identity self, learning skill |
| Medium | writing, talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the molecule catalogue divides by whether the construct existed before the formula.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunity_to_change |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *immunity map* | The worksheet of improvement goal, counterproductive behaviors, hidden competing commitments, and big assumptions. |
| *competing commitment* | A hidden commitment, often to self-protection, that the counterproductive behavior actually serves. |
| *big assumption* | A belief taken as true that makes the competing commitment feel necessary, which the person learns to treat as a hypothesis to test. |
| *from complaint to commitment* | The first of the shifts in language the method teaches, from what one complains about to what one is committed to. |

#### Kaizen and the kaizen event

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Japanese industry after the Second World War, especially Toyota, drawing on W. Edwards Deming and the U.S. Training Within Industry program; popularized abroad by Masaaki Imai |
| Date | 1950s onward; Imai's book 1986 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | Workers and managers look at the place where the work is done, identify waste, and make many small improvements, each tested through a plan-do-check-act cycle; a kaizen event concentrates this into a short, focused effort by a small team. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from many small improvements made continually by the people who do the work. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | task work, learning skill, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaizen |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *kaizen* | Japanese for improvement or change for the better, usually through the cumulative effect of many small changes. |
| *gemba* | The actual place where the work is done, where improvement begins. |
| *PDCA* | The Plan, Do, Check, Act cycle used to test each improvement. |
| *muda* | Waste, in forms such as overproduction, waiting, and unnecessary movement. |

#### LEGO Serious Play

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Johan Roos and Bart Victor (IMD), with Kjeld Kirk Kristiansen of the Lego Group |
| Date | mid-1990s; open source 2010 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The facilitator poses a question, each participant builds an answer as a model out of Lego bricks, each tells the group the story of their model, and the group reflects; individual models can then be combined into one shared model. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from building models by hand, because a model lets people express ideas they could not otherwise put into words and gives every person a turn to speak. |
| Limits and critiques | The journalist Dan Lyons criticized it as 'toy therapy' that can feel cultish and cause anxiety at work. |
| Mental model | improvisation, play, and the arts. Draws on constructionism (Papert) and complex adaptive systems theory |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | creativity play, task work, future vision, identity self |
| Medium | art image, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lego_Serious_Play |

| What happens | A leader pauses at any moment to ask whether they are above the line, open, curious, and taking responsibility, or below the line, closed, defensive, and experiencing life as happening to them, and chooses to shift if they are below it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a leader taking full responsibility for their own circumstances and wellbeing, beginning with an honest reading of their present state. |

| *locating yourself* | Noticing, in the moment, which side of the line one is on. |

#### Nemawashi

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Japanese business and organizational custom |
| Date | traditional, undated |
| Scale | an organization, a society's customs |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Before a formal meeting, the person with a proposal meets the relevant people one at a time, informally introduces the idea, hears their reactions, and adjusts it, so that the formal meeting confirms an agreement already built. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from building agreement person by person beforehand, because senior people who first hear of a proposal in a meeting may feel ignored and reject it. |
| Limits and critiques | Some question whether it is truly cooperative or whether those consulted have little choice but to agree, and it takes time. |
| Mental model | organizational. A Japanese business custom rather than a designed method |
| Structure | none of the families; a single move |
| Subjects | decision choice, relationship present, justice power |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemawashi |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *nemawashi* | Literally 'turning the roots': digging around a tree and trimming its roots some time before transplanting it, used for preparing people before a decision. |
| *ringiseido* | A related, more formal Japanese process for building consensus on a proposal. |

#### Organizational constellations

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Gunthard Weber and Klaus Grochowiak, adapting Bert Hellinger's family constellations |
| Date | 1990s |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A client, often a leader, chooses seminar participants to stand for people or parts of an organization and places them in the room according to an inner sense of their relationships; the representatives report what they perceive in their positions, and the facilitator moves them to explore arrangements that feel resolved. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing an organization's relationships represented in space, which practitioners hold shows hidden dynamics and possible new arrangements. |
| Limits and critiques | The psychologist Uwe Kanning doubts that a knowing field exists and attributes representatives' reports to suggestibility; the parent method is widely described as pseudoscientific; and Stefan Kühl criticizes systemic consulting for neglecting power. |
| Mental model | systemic and family. Adapted from Hellinger's family constellations, which combine family systems therapy and phenomenology |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | group process, relationship present, decision choice |
| Medium | enactment role, movement dance, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisationsaufstellung; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Constellations |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *representative* | A participant who stands in for a person or element of the client's organization. |
| *representative perception* | The claim, in German repräsentierende Wahrnehmung, that representatives perceive and express the actual dynamics of the system they stand for. |
| *knowing field* | Practitioners' name for the field in which representatives are said to sense the system. |

|---|---|

| What happens | At the end of the three-month rains retreat, each monk comes before the assembled community and invites the others to point out any fault of his that they have seen, heard, or suspected, so that he can correct it. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from a community's honest mutual correction, which each member asks for rather than merely receives. |
