#### Polarity Management (the polarity map)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Barry Johnson |
| Date | 1992 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, one person alone |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A group that is arguing over two options maps them as two interdependent poles, lists the upsides and downsides of each, and plans action steps and early warnings so that it can gain the benefits of both poles over time instead of choosing one. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeing that some disputes are between two goods that both need attention over time, so that each side can value the other's concern. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | holding two poles; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, conflict, thought belief |
| Medium | writing, talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.polaritypartnerships.com/; general knowledge, unverified |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Polarity Map* | A chart of the upsides and downsides of two interdependent poles. |
| *problem to solve versus polarity to manage* | The distinction between a question with one right answer and an ongoing tension between two necessary goods. |
| *upsides and downsides* | The benefits of attending to a pole and the costs of attending to it too much while neglecting the other. |

#### Process consultation and humble inquiry

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Edgar Schein |
| Date | 1969 (Process Consultation); 2013 (Humble Inquiry) |
| Scale | an organization, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Rather than arriving as an expert with answers, the consultant helps a client see and act on what is happening in its own processes, such as how people communicate, decide, and relate, by asking questions to which the consultant does not already know the answer and leaving ownership of the problem and its solution with the client. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when the client learns to see its own processes, because only the client knows enough about its situation to find a solution that will work there. |
| Mental model | humanistic and person-centered. Rooted in Kurt Lewin's group dynamics and the T-group tradition; the fit is loose |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | group process, relationship present, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Schein |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *process consultation* | Helping a client perceive, understand, and act on the processes in its own situation, as distinct from supplying expertise or diagnosing like a doctor. |
| *humble inquiry* | Schein's practice of drawing another person out by asking questions whose answers one does not already know, from genuine curiosity. |
| *helping relationship* | The relationship between helper and client, which Schein treats as the thing that must be built before help can work. |

#### Quaker business method (meeting for worship for business)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Religious Society of Friends (Quakers) |
| Date | late 17th century |
| Scale | an organization, a large group, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The meeting waits in silence, members speak briefly and without debate, and the clerk, after listening for where the meeting is led, proposes a written minute of the sense of the meeting; the meeting unites on the minute, editing it if needed, or postpones the matter, and it never votes. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from seeking together the will of God, which Friends hold can be discerned because there is that of God in every person. |
| Limits and critiques | Friends distinguish it from secular consensus, and whether it can work without its spiritual frame is debated. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, spirit ultimate, group process |
| Medium | silence, talk, writing |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | `notes/harmony is the joining of differences rather than a collapse into agreement.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker_business_method |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sense of the meeting* | The shared discernment of the right course that emerges in worship and that the clerk records. |
| *clerk* | The member who serves the meeting by guiding it, discerning its sense, and drafting minutes. |
| *minute* | The written statement of what the meeting has discerned on an agenda item. |
| *unity* | Agreement to go forward that can hold even when some members personally disagree, as distinct from unanimity. |

#### Scenario planning

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Herman Kahn (RAND Corporation); Pierre Wack and colleagues at Royal Dutch Shell |
| Date | 1950s (Kahn); 1970s (Shell) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A team identifies the forces driving change in its environment, separates what is already predetermined from what is critically uncertain, and combines the uncertainties into a few contrasting stories of how the future could unfold, which leaders then use to test their strategies and assumptions. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from rehearsing several plausible futures, which leads leaders to question their fixed assumptions and prepares them to recognize change early. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics say it relies on subjective judgment rather than tested method, and forecasters are part of the social context they describe and may influence it. |
| Mental model | organizational |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, decision choice, thought belief |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scenario_planning; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Wack |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *driving forces* | The basic factors that shape how the future could unfold. |
| *predetermined elements* | Things about the future that are already knowable, such as demographics or mineral reserves. |
| *critical uncertainties* | Important factors whose outcome could go in several significantly different directions. |

#### Sociocracy (Sociocratic Circle-Organization Method)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Gerard Endenburg, building on the school practice of Kees Boeke |
| Date | late 1960s–1970s (Boeke's school from 1926) |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | An organization is divided into semi-autonomous circles, each of which makes its policy decisions by consent in rounds where each member speaks in turn; each circle takes part in the next circle up through two people, its leader and an elected representative, and people are chosen for roles by open discussion and consent. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from giving everyone affected a reasoned objection over policy, which lets an organization steer itself through feedback in the way a self-regulating system does. |
| Mental model | organizational. Grounded in cybernetics and systems thinking |
| Structure | nested levels; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociocracy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *consent* | A policy decision is made when no informed member has a remaining paramount objection; members ask whether a proposal is good enough for now and safe enough to try. |
| *double linking* | Each circle is represented in the next higher circle by two full members, its leader and a representative, so that information and decisions flow both ways. |
| *circle* | A semi-autonomous team that makes the policy decisions for its own area of work. |
| *election by consent* | Filling a role through open nomination and discussion, decided by consent rather than by secret ballot. |

#### Sociocracy 3.0

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Bernhard Bockelbrink, James Priest, and Liliana David |
| Date | 2014 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Teams choose from a free library of patterns only the practices they need; they describe the driver behind each piece of work, and proposals are adopted once members have raised, sought out, and resolved objections to them. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from many small, consented agreements that let people organize around what they are actually responding to. |
| Mental model | organizational. Adapts sociocracy for agile organizations |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, task work, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *driver* | A person's or a group's motive for responding to a specific situation. |
| *tension* | A personal experience of dissonance between how one perceives a situation and what one expects or prefers. |
| *objection* | An argument showing that a proposal or activity has consequences worth avoiding, or that it could be improved in a worthwhile way. |
| *domain* | A distinct area of responsibility and authority within an organization. |
| *pattern* | One of the practices in the Sociocracy 3.0 library, which a group adopts when it needs it. |

#### Systemic Modelling (Clean Language for groups)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Caitlin Walker (Training Attention), developing David Grove's Clean Language |
| Date | 2000s–2010s |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | Team members learn to ask each other clean questions, which use only the other person's own words and metaphors, so that each person can describe how they work at their best and the group builds shared models of its members' patterns and preferences without imposing labels. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when people become curious about the metaphors and patterns behind each other's behavior instead of judging it. |
| Mental model | other. Clean Language (David Grove) applied to groups and organizations |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, group process, learning skill, thought belief |
| Medium | talk, art image |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.trainingattention.co.uk/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_language |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clean questions* | Questions that add nothing of the asker's own and use only the speaker's words and metaphors. |
| *Clean Setup* | A tool for agreeing at the start of a shared task what each person needs in order to work at their best. |
| *from contempt to curiosity* | Walker's name for the shift the method aims at, from judging others to being curious about how they think. |

#### The learning organization's five disciplines

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Peter Senge |
| Date | 1990 |
| Scale | an organization, a small group, one person alone |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | An organization practices five disciplines together (systems thinking, personal mastery, mental models, building shared vision, and team learning), with systems thinking joining the others, so that its members keep learning as a group. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from an organization's capacity to learn as a whole, which depends on its members seeing the system they are part of. |
| Mental model | learning theory. Joins system dynamics with dialogue and personal development |
| Structure | combining elements; a model of what a person or group is made of |
| Subjects | learning skill, thought belief, future vision, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |

| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *systems thinking* | The cornerstone discipline: seeing wholes, feedback, and patterns of interrelation rather than isolated events. |
| *personal mastery* | The discipline of continually clarifying what one wants while seeing current reality clearly. |
| *mental models* | Deeply held assumptions that shape how people understand the world and act, which the discipline brings to the surface and tests. |
| *team learning* | The discipline by which a team learns to think together, including through dialogue. |

#### Theory U (presencing)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | C. Otto Scharmer (MIT; Presencing Institute) |
| Date | 2007 |
| Scale | an organization, a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A team moves down one side of a U by suspending habitual judgments and observing its system closely together, pauses at the bottom, often in silence or alone, to reflect on who they are and what they most want to bring about, and moves up the other side by clarifying a vision and building quick prototypes to test it in practice. |
| What it assumes | Change depends on the quality of attention with which people act, and goes deeper when they observe the whole system and reflect on what they most want to create before deciding. |
| Limits and critiques | The sociologist Stefan Kühl argues that Theory U oversimplifies organizational complexity, overlooks structural conflicts, and focuses on individual change while claiming effects on whole systems. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent. Draws on phenomenology and on Scharmer's interviews with innovators; the fit is loose |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | attention awareness, future vision, identity self, group process |
| Medium | talk, silence, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the basic practice posture is specified as simultaneous conditions rather than as a sequence.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_U |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *presencing* | Scharmer's term for the step at the bottom of the U in which people let go of old assumptions and attend to the future they most want to bring about. |
| *letting go and letting come* | Giving up old habits of thought and old self-images at the bottom of the U so that new ideas and intentions can form. |
| *four levels of listening* | Downloading, factual, empathic, and generative listening, from listening only for what confirms one's habits to listening for new possibilities. |
| *voices of judgment, cynicism, and fear* | The three inner resistances that block an open mind, an open heart, and an open will. |

### A community or public

This scale is the main one for 29 techniques, and the vault has met 1 of them.

#### Asset-based community development (ABCD)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John P. Kretzmann and John L. McKnight (Northwestern University; the ABCD Institute) |
| Date | 1993 |
| Scale | a community or public, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Residents and organizers map what a neighbourhood already has, including the gifts and skills of individuals, citizens' associations, and local institutions, instead of listing its needs. They then connect these assets with one another so that residents can act on what they care about. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in the resources a community already holds, holding that solutions to community problems already exist among its residents and their associations. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics argue that the approach can imply that disadvantaged communities have enough resources of their own, which can give governments a justification for cutting public services. |
| Mental model | participatory and emergent |
| Structure | combining elements; a method |
| Subjects | task work, relationship present, future vision |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asset-based_community_development |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *asset mapping* | making a visual inventory of a community's existing resources in place of a map of its needs |
| *capacity inventory* | a questionnaire that records the skills a resident has in home, work, and community life |
| *gifts* | the abilities and knowledge of individual residents, treated as the first kind of asset a community has |
| *associations* | the informal voluntary groups through which citizens already act together |

#### Citizens' assembly

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | First modern use by the Province of British Columbia, Canada (Citizens' Assembly on Electoral Reform); since adopted by many governments |
| Date | 2004 |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Organizers choose members from the public by lot, stratified so that the group resembles the population. The members study the question with experts, hold public hearings and read submissions, deliberate in facilitated plenary and small-group sessions, and then make recommendations by vote or consensus. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in ordinary people who, given time, information, and a fair process, can judge a public question on behalf of everyone. |
| Limits and critiques | An assembly's recommendations are usually not binding, and critics ask whether a small random body can speak for a public that did not take part in its deliberation. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, learning skill, justice power |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://participedia.net/method/citizens-assembly; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens'_Assembly_on_Electoral_Reform_(British_Columbia) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sortition* | choosing members by random lottery rather than by election or appointment |
| *learning phase* | the weeks in which members study the issue through expert presentations and small-group discussion |
| *listening phase* | the period of public hearings and written or spoken submissions from the wider public |
| *deliberation phase* | the facilitated sessions in which members weigh the options and form their recommendations |

#### Consciousness-raising

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | New York Radical Women, including Kathie Sarachild and Anne Forer, drawing on civil-rights organizing |
| Date | 1967-1968 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| What happens | A small group of women meets regularly, and a question is taken around the room so that each woman gives testimony from her own life. The group compares the accounts to test existing theories against shared experience and to reach truer generalizations about women's situation, as a basis for organizing. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates knowledge in pooled personal experience, holding that women's own feelings and experience should be taken more seriously than any theory that fails to explain them. |
| Limits and critiques | Sarachild insisted that it was not therapy and not an effort to change women; the change sought was in the conditions women face, and she warned that rigid rules such as 'no interruptions' could make going around the room 'deadening and not at all informative'. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | telling the real thing from its counterfeit; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, identity self, emotion, thought belief |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.rapereliefshelter.bc.ca/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/Feminist-Revolution-Consciousness-Raising-A-Radical-Weapon-Kathie-Sarachild.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *consciousness-raising* | studying the whole range of women's own feelings and experience together in order to understand oppression and act on it |
| *going around the room* | hearing each woman's testimony in turn, to keep the discussion on one point and gather as many experiences as possible |
| *testimony* | a woman's account of her own experience, offered as evidence for the group's analysis |

#### Culture circle (círculo de cultura)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Paulo Freire, with Elza Freire and teams of coordinators, in north-eastern Brazil |
| Date | early 1960s (Angicos, 1963); later Chile |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A team first studies the vocabulary and problems of a community and chooses generative words from it. In the circle, a coordinator shows a picture that presents a situation from the learners' lives, the group discusses and decodes it, and the word is broken into syllables from which learners build new words, so that learning to read and examining their own situation happen together. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in people naming and questioning their own situation in dialogue, holding that literacy and critical awareness grow together. |
| Limits and critiques | The educator John L. Elias asked whether the method is open to the charge of indoctrination or subtle manipulation, since coordinators choose the words and themes that frame the discussion. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, justice power, thought belief |
| Medium | talk, art image, reading text, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `notes/the facilitated room has several independent roots and improv's distinction is theorizing play as craft.md` |
| Sources | https://angicos50anos.paulofreire.org/en/cronologia/; https://mje.mcgill.ca/article/download/7044/4986/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *culture circle* | the learning group that replaces the classroom, in which a coordinator and adult learners talk as equals about their own lives |
| *generative word* | a word from the learners' own vocabulary that has strong meaning for them and whose syllables can form new words |
| *codification* | a picture or scene that presents a situation from the learners' lives so that the group can discuss and decode it |
| *conscientization (conscientização)* | the process by which people come to understand the social reality that shapes their lives and their power to change it through action |

| What it assumes | The technique locates change in open talk about death among ordinary people, which it expects to increase awareness of mortality and help people live more fully. |
| Limits and critiques | Death Cafe describes itself as a discussion group rather than grief support or counselling. |

| Structure | tending conditions; a method |

| *café mortel* | Bernard Crettaz's earlier French-language gatherings for talking about death, on which the model is based |

#### Deliberative Polling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | James S. Fishkin (Stanford University, Center for Deliberative Democracy) |
| Date | proposed 1988; first held 1994 (Manchester, England) |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Organizers survey a random sample of the public, invite a second random sample to meet in one place, and send them balanced briefing materials. Participants discuss the issue in small groups with trained moderators, put their questions to competing experts in plenary sessions, and then answer the same survey again so that the change in their opinions can be measured. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in informed discussion among a representative sample, and it treats the difference between the first and second surveys as what the public would think if it were engaged with the issue. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics argue that participants may change their views partly because they know they are being studied, and that the result shows opinion under controlled conditions rather than actual public opinion. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, learning skill, group process |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://participedia.net/method/deliberative-polling |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Deliberative Poll* | the trademarked name for an event that measures what a random sample of the public thinks after it has studied and discussed an issue |
| *balanced briefing materials* | documents vetted by a panel of experts that set out the arguments on each side before the event |
| *considered opinions* | the views participants hold after information and discussion, compared with their answers to the first survey |

#### Design charrette

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | The nineteenth-century École des Beaux-Arts in Paris (the word); urban planners and the National Charrette Institute (the modern planning workshop) |
| Date | 19th century (term); late 20th century (planning method) |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A design team works intensively for at least three or four consecutive days, drawing proposals and bringing them back to residents, officials, and developers at several stakeholder and public meetings, and revising the drawings after each round of feedback. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from short, repeated cycles of design and public feedback, which give the people affected joint ownership of the plan. |
| Limits and critiques | The residents who take part may not represent all the residents of a community. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | future vision, decision choice, task work, conflict |
| Medium | art image, talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charrette |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *charrette* | French for cart: Beaux-Arts students worked 'en charrette' until a cart collected their drawings, and the word now names an intensive, deadline-driven design workshop. |
| *feedback loop* | A cycle in which designers present options, stakeholders respond, and the design is revised; the National Charrette Institute's model includes three. |

#### Family group conference (FGC)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Aotearoa New Zealand, modelled on the Māori whānau hui (family meeting) and written into the Children, Young Persons and Their Families Act 1989 |
| Date | 1989 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A state-appointed coordinator convenes the child or young person, parents, extended whānau, and, in youth-justice cases, the victim. After everyone hears the concerns or the facts of the offending, the family considers them on its own and forms a plan, which the conference then agrees. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in the family's own decision-making, holding that kin who are given the facts and time together can make a better plan than officials. |
| Limits and critiques | The Māori social worker Paora Moyle reports that Māori remain over-represented in the care system and that the conference has often been used as a state-centred rather than a family-centred process, although the model was sourced from Māori practice. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational. A state legal process that adapted Māori kin-based decision-making; it is also used as a youth-justice restorative conference |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, relationship present, justice power, conflict |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://practice.orangatamariki.govt.nz/our-work/interventions/family-group-conferencing/about-family-group-conferencing; https://devnet.org.nz/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/Moyle,%20Paora.%20A%20moa%20in%20the%20room.%20Maori%20social%20workers%20experiences%20of%20family%20group%20conferencing%20[paper].pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *whānau* | extended family and kin network, wider than the parents alone |
| *whānau hui* | a traditional Māori family meeting for solving problems, which inspired the conference |
| *coordinator* | the official, either a care and protection coordinator or a youth justice coordinator, who is legally responsible for convening the conference |
| *tikanga* | Māori customary practices that give the process balance, stability, and safety |

#### Forum Theatre (Theatre of the Oppressed)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Augusto Boal |
| Date | 1973 (Peru); Legislative Theatre 1992-1996 (Rio de Janeiro) |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Actors perform a short scene in which a protagonist fails to overcome an oppression the audience knows from its own life, and then the scene is played again. Any spectator may call 'stop', replace the protagonist, and try another action, while the joker asks the audience to judge each attempt and to question 'magic' solutions that could not work in reality. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in people rehearsing action against oppression, holding that practising a change on stage empowers them to practise it in life. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, conflict, creativity play, decision choice |
| Medium | enactment role, talk, movement dance |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forum_theatre; https://ptoweb.org/aboutpto/a-brief-biography-of-augusto-boal/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *spect-actor* | an audience member who stops being a passive spectator and steps into the scene to act |
| *joker* | the facilitator who stands between actors and audience, runs the session, and asks the audience to evaluate each intervention |
| *magic solution* | an intervention that could not happen in real life, which the joker asks the audience to decide about |
| *Legislative Theatre* | Boal's later form, developed while he was a Rio de Janeiro city councillor, in which forum sessions became the basis for actual legislation |

#### Gacaca courts

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Rwandan custom of settling community disputes on the grass, revived by the Government of Rwanda for genocide cases |
| Date | traditional; revived by the Gacaca Law of 2001, courts sat 2002-2012 |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | Communities elected lay judges, called persons of integrity, who held public hearings in each locality, collected information about the genocide, and tried the accused. Residents gave evidence by raising a hand to speak, and accused people who confessed could receive reduced sentences and community service. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates justice and repair in the local community that witnessed the crimes, holding that neighbours can establish the truth and judge together. |
| Limits and critiques | Human Rights Watch reported that the accused had no right to a lawyer, that some judges were biased or corrupt, that witnesses were intimidated, and that crimes by soldiers of the ruling Rwandan Patriotic Front were not prosecuted. |
| Mental model | other. State-run transitional justice adapted from a customary practice |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, memory past, trauma healing, conflict |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://participedia.net/case/83; https://www.hrw.org/report/2011/05/31/justice-compromised/legacy-rwandas-community-based-gacaca-courts |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *gacaca* | Kinyarwanda for the grass or lawn where community disputes were traditionally settled |
| *inyangamugayo* | 'persons of integrity', the elected lay judges |
| *confession* | an accused person's public admission of guilt, which could reduce the sentence |

#### Highlander residential workshop

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Myles Horton and Don West, Highlander Folk School, Monteagle, Tennessee; the Citizenship Schools were led by Septima Clark and Bernice Robinson |
| Date | 1932; Citizenship Schools from 1957 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | People who face a shared problem in their own communities come to live and study together for two to eight weeks. The school gives no grades, credits, or examinations, lets the students' needs set the curriculum, and uses discussion, visiting speakers, films, drama, and music so that participants build on what they already know and return home to act together. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates the answers to social problems in the experience of the ordinary people who face them, and it treats education as helping them build on that knowledge and act collectively. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk, voice music, enactment role |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/highlander-folk-school/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *residential workshop* | a live-in course of several weeks for fifteen to forty people, held away from their home communities |
| *citizenship school* | Highlander-sponsored adult classes, first held in 1957 on the South Carolina Sea Islands, that taught the literacy needed to register to vote |

#### Jirga

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Pashtun tradition (Pashtunwali), in present-day Afghanistan and Pakistan |
| Date | traditional, undated |
| Scale | a community or public, a society's customs, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | Disputing parties turn to a mediator, who forms a council of respected elders that includes supporters of both sides. The elders sit in a circle as equals with no chairman, hear both sides, and seek a settlement acceptable to both, and their decision binds the parties, with fines or collective sanctions for anyone who breaks it. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates settlement in the authority and impartiality of respected elders applying a shared code, with the community enforcing the result. |
| Limits and critiques | Women are excluded from jirgas even in cases that concern them, and the researcher Maryam Nesar reports that the exchange of girls to settle disputes continues. |
| Mental model | other. Customary law: a council of elders settles disputes by consensus under a shared code of honour |
| Structure | none of the families; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, justice power, decision choice |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.irenees.net/bdf_fiche-defis-258_en.html; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jirga; https://www.eurac.edu/en/blogs/gender-matters/a-traditional-code-and-its-consequences-how-pashtunwali-affects-women-and-minorities |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *jirgamar (marakachi)* | the elders and experts who sit on a jirga |
| *prikra* | the jirga's final judgment, which binds the parties |
| *narkh* | the customary law, drawn from Pashtunwali, that the jirga applies |
| *Pashtunwali* | the Pashtun social code, which stresses honour, hospitality, and justice |

#### National Issues Forums

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kettering Foundation under David Mathews; now carried by the National Issues Forums Institute |
| Date | 1981 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A trained, neutral moderator leads a forum through an issue guide that frames a public problem as three or four broad approaches. Participants weigh the costs, consequences, and trade-offs of each approach and look for a direction they can accept together. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in citizens weighing hard choices together in public, which its founder describes as a form of learning that democracy depends on. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, conflict, group process |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | session |

|---|---|

| *trade-offs* | what each approach would cost or require people to give up, which participants are asked to weigh |
| *common ground for action* | a direction the group can accept together while its members still disagree on other points |

#### Participatory action research (PAR)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Orlando Fals Borda and the Colombian collective La Rosca de Investigación y Acción Social |
| Date | early 1970s; international symposium in Cartagena, 1977 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Researchers work with an organized community, such as a peasant movement, and investigate its problems together with its members, recovering the community's history through collective memory. They then return the findings to the organization and its members in forms they can use, such as workshops, illustrated histories, and radio programmes, so that the research serves the community's own action. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates knowledge and change in ordinary people studying their own situation with researchers, holding that research should serve the community's action rather than an academic audience. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | learning skill, justice power, memory past |
| Medium | talk, writing, art image, enactment role |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.dukeupress.edu/cowards-don-t-make-history; https://www.redalyc.org/journal/5515/551556446012/html/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *critical recovery* | recovering a community's history through collective memory and its own records, to serve its present struggles |
| *systematic devolution* | returning research results to the organization and its members in an orderly way and in forms suited to their situation |
| *popular science* | the practical, common-sense knowledge that ordinary people hold as part of their culture, treated as real knowledge |

#### Participatory budgeting (orçamento participativo)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Workers' Party (PT) city government of Porto Alegre, Brazil, under Mayor Olívio Dutra |
| Date | 1989 |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Each year residents attend open regional and thematic assemblies where they propose and rank priorities for public investment and elect delegates and councillors. The Participatory Budget Council then works with city staff to build the investment plan and supervise spending, and the cycle begins again the next year. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates fairer public spending in the direct participation of residents alongside the institutions of representative democracy. |
| Limits and critiques | After the Workers' Party lost Porto Alegre's elections in 2004, later governments engaged less with the process and observers reported a return of clientelistic relationships. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, justice power, task work |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://participedia.net/case/5524 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *regional and thematic assemblies* | open public meetings, by district and by policy area, where residents set priorities |
| *delegates* | residents elected at the assemblies, in Porto Alegre one for every ten participants, who represent the assemblies' priorities in the later stages |
| *Participatory Budget Council (COP)* | the elected council that plans and supervises the municipal budget's revenues and expenditures |

#### Peacemaking circle (circle process)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Kay Pranis, Judge Barry Stuart, and Mark Wedge, drawing on the circle practice of First Nations people of the Yukon, including Wedge and Harold Gatensby |
| Date | 1990s; the book Peacemaking Circles appeared in 2003 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants sit in a circle without tables around a centerpiece, and an opening ceremony marks the time as set apart. The group names its values and creates its own guidelines, then a talking piece passes around the circle so that only its holder speaks, while the keeper offers guiding questions and closes with a ceremony. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in the circle itself, where equal voice, shared values, and unhurried speaking let people meet one another beyond their usual roles. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, relationship present, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk, ritual object, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://elderjusticemn.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/Talking-Circle-Keeper-Manual.pdf; https://emu.edu/now/news/2015/circle-process-course-offered-by-kay-pranis-trains-facilitators-for-dialogue-healing-in-variety-of-settings/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *keeper* | the facilitator, whose role Kay Pranis describes as helping participants create 'a space where each can speak and listen from the heart' and then watching the quality of that space, rather than enforcing the guidelines |
| *talking piece* | an object passed from hand to hand; only the person holding it speaks, which shares control of the process with everyone |
| *centerpiece* | objects on a cloth in the middle of the circle that represent the group's values and purpose |
| *guidelines* | the agreements about conduct that participants create together and adopt by consensus |

#### Photovoice

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Caroline C. Wang and Mary Ann Burris, with village women in Yunnan Province, China |
| Date | 1992 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Community members receive cameras and training in ethical and safe photography, and they photograph their own lives and surroundings. In regular group meetings each photographer shows pictures and explains why they took them, the group discusses what the pictures reveal, and the project ends with an exhibition and action aimed at policy makers. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in people recording and discussing their own reality, and in putting those images before the people who make policy. |
| Limits and critiques | The Community Tool Box warns that photographers may face threats, arrest, or physical danger, and that they must learn when it is ethical to photograph others and how to ask permission. |
| Mental model | critical and liberatory |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, identity self, learning skill |
| Medium | art image, talk, technology |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://ctb.ku.edu/en/table-of-contents/assessment/assessing-community-needs-and-resources/photovoice/main |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *photovoice* | a participatory method in which people document their own community in photographs and discuss them together |
| *critical consciousness* | Paulo Freire's idea, adopted by the founders, of understanding how social and political power shape one's own situation |

#### Public narrative (story of self, story of us, story of now)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Marshall Ganz (Harvard Kennedy School; Leading Change Network), from the stories told by social movement leaders |
| Date | 2000s (working paper and worksheet, 2009) |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group, two people as equals |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A leader learns to tell three linked stories: a story of self about the values that called them to act, a story of us about the values the listeners share, and a story of now about an urgent challenge and a specific choice for action. People practise in small groups by telling, listening, reflecting, and telling again, with feedback from coaches. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates the motivation for collective action in values made vivid through story, which Ganz describes as translating values into the motivation to act. |
| Mental model | narrative and constructionist |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | meaning purpose, identity self, future vision, justice power |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://leadingchangenetwork.org/resource_center/what-is-public-narrative-self-us-and-now-public-narrative-worksheet-working-paper/; https://commonslibrary.org/the-power-of-story-the-story-of-self-us-and-now/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *story of self* | a story that communicates the values that called the speaker to leadership |
| *story of us* | a story that communicates the values shared by the people who will act together |
| *story of now* | a story of an urgent challenge to those shared values that demands action now |
| *challenge, choice, outcome* | the shape of each story: a character meets a challenge, makes a choice, and the choice yields an outcome |

#### Reflective Structured Dialogue

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Laura Chasin and colleagues from the Family Institute of Cambridge; the Public Conversations Project, now Essential Partners |
| Date | 1989 |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Facilitators prepare participants in advance and invite the group to adopt communication agreements, including the right to pass. They pose opening questions that each person answers in timed go-rounds, with pauses before and after speaking, and later invite questions asked from genuine curiosity rather than to argue. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in the pattern of communication between people who disagree, holding that a different way of speaking and listening interrupts the cycle of polarization. |
| Limits and critiques | Essential Partners does not ask participants to change their views; the aim is understanding and trust, and the differences remain. |
| Mental model | dialogic. Its founders applied family therapy, mediation, and systems theory to public disputes |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, relationship present, thought belief, identity self |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://whatisessential.org/our-method; https://whatisessential.org/history; https://frasercentre.ca/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/RSD-15.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *communication agreements* | commitments the group adopts about how members will speak and listen; the facilitator proposes them and the group has the final say |
| *go-round* | a turn in which each person speaks in order without interruption, so that everyone is heard |
| *pass agreement* | the standing rule that anyone may decline to speak at any point without explaining why |
| *genuine questions* | questions asked because the asker does not know the answer and wants to understand, not statements disguised as questions |

#### Relational meeting (the one-to-one)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Industrial Areas Foundation, founded by Saul Alinsky in 1940; made a central organizing practice by Edward T. Chambers |

Organizers use these meetings to find leaders who show a willingness to act and a controlled anger, and after actions they hold candid one-to-one meetings to challenge leaders to grow. |

| Time | session |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|

| *self-interest* | what a person actually cares about and will act for, understood as tied to relationships rather than as selfishness |
| *iron rule* | the IAF principle that one should never do for others what they can do for themselves |
| *agitation* | an organizer's deliberate challenge to a leader to face what blocks their growth |

#### Study circle (studiecirkel)

|---|---|

In the United States form the sessions move from sharing personal experience and values, to examining the arguments, to proposals for action. |

| *Dialogue to Change* | Everyday Democracy's name for its process that connects small-group dialogue to community action |

|---|---|
| *jaha* | the delegation of respected mediators who conduct the whole process |
| *hudna* | a truce in which the injured family agrees not to retaliate for a set period |
| *'atwa* | a first payment or gesture from the offending family, whose acceptance is evidence that the truce is in force |
| *diya* | compensation set by the jaha, understood as symbolic rather than as the value of a life |
| *musalaha* | reconciliation and forgiveness, completed in the public ceremony |

#### Sustained Dialogue

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Harold H. Saunders (former U.S. diplomat, Kettering Foundation), from the Dartmouth Conference Regional Conflicts Task Force and the Inter-Tajik Dialogue; continued by the Sustained Dialogue Institute |
| Date | 1980s-1990s; set out in A Public Peace Process (1999) |
| Scale | a community or public, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A moderating team brings the same members of conflicting groups together again and again over months or years, recording each meeting in an analytical memo. The group moves, not in a fixed line, through five stages: deciding to engage, mapping relationships and naming problems, probing the problems and relationships, designing action, and acting together. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in the relationships between groups, which it holds can be changed only by the same people meeting over a long time. |
| Limits and critiques | Saunders presents it for groups too divided, suspicious, and angry to meet on their own, and says problem-solving or deliberative forums may suit communities whose members can already talk easily. |
| Mental model | dialogic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | conflict, relationship present, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.civicus.org/documents/toolkits/PGX_D_Sustained%20Dialogue.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *relationship* | the object of the work, analysed in five arenas: identity, interests, power, perceptions, and patterns of interaction |
| *dialogue about dialogue* | the first stage, in which participants conclude that they need to talk together about a problem in a more organized way |
| *cumulative agenda* | the practice of letting the questions left open at one meeting set the agenda for the next |
| *moderator* | the person responsible for the whole long process, not only the facilitator of single meetings |

#### Talanoa

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Pacific Island peoples; adapted as the Talanoa Dialogue under Fiji's presidency of the UN climate conference |
| Date | traditional, undated; Talanoa Dialogue 2017–2018 |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group, a society's customs, two people as equals, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants share stories and experiences in an open and inclusive setting in which no one is blamed, so that each can learn from the others and the group can move toward consensus and a decision; in 2017–2018 the UN climate process used this form for countries and other actors to share how they could raise their commitments. |
| What it assumes | Change comes from people sharing their own stories in a setting without blame, which builds the trust needed to decide together. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, decision choice, nature ecology, group process |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talanoa_Dialogue |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *talanoa* | A Pacific practice of storytelling and open conversation that leads to consensus-building and decision-making. |
| *Talanoa Dialogue* | The 2017–2018 UN climate process, launched at COP23 under Fiji's presidency, that used talanoa to take stock of collective progress. |

#### Transformative Scenario Planning

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Adam Kahane (Reos Partners) |
| Date | 1990s–2010s (Mont Fleur exercise in South Africa, 1991–92; book 2012) |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group, an organization |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A team of leaders drawn from across a divided social system, including people who oppose each other, meets in a series of workshops to observe what is happening, write several plausible stories about what could happen, and then work out what they can and must do together to influence which future occurs. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when people who distrust each other build a shared understanding of possible futures and then decide to act on it together. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | future vision, conflict, justice power, decision choice |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://reospartners.com/reos-management/adam-kahane; general knowledge, unverified |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *transformative scenario planning* | Kahane's term for using scenarios to change what will happen, as distinct from adaptive scenario planning, which uses them to adapt to what might happen. |
| *Mont Fleur* | The 1991–92 South African scenario exercise that brought political opponents together to write stories about the country's possible transitions. |
| *a team from across the whole system* | The convening principle that the team includes members from every part of the system, including those who distrust one another. |

#### Truth and Reconciliation Commission (South Africa)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Government of South Africa under the Promotion of National Unity and Reconciliation Act 34 of 1995; chaired by Archbishop Desmond Tutu |
| Date | 1995 (Act); hearings from 1996 |
| Scale | a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | Victims of gross human rights violations told their own accounts at public hearings, and perpetrators could apply for amnesty by making full disclosure of acts committed for a political objective. A third committee recommended reparations and rehabilitation for victims. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in public acknowledgment, holding that a nation told the truth about its violations can move toward reconciliation without trying every crime. |
| Limits and critiques | Critics argued that its narrow focus on gross violations left out the everyday harms of apartheid, such as forced removals and pass laws; most amnesty applications were refused. |
| Mental model | other. Transitional justice that exchanged amnesty for public truth-telling rather than prosecuting every crime |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | justice power, trauma healing, memory past, conflict, grief |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://sahistory.org.za/article/truth-and-reconciliation-commission-trc-1995 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *full disclosure* | the condition for amnesty: the applicant had to reveal all the relevant facts of the politically motivated acts |
| *Human Rights Violations Committee* | the committee that gathered victims' testimony about gross abuses |
| *Amnesty Committee* | the committee that decided the applications for amnesty |
| *Reparation and Rehabilitation Committee* | the committee that recommended long-term reparations and short-term relief for victims |

#### Victim-offender reconciliation (VORP), later victim-offender mediation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Mark Yantzi (probation officer) and Dave Worth (Mennonite Central Committee volunteer), Kitchener, Ontario, with Judge Gordon McConnell |
| Date | 1974 |
| Scale | a community or public, three people |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A mediator brings the person who caused harm face to face with the person harmed, and together they talk about what happened and agree on restitution. In the first case, in Elmira, Ontario, two young men visited each household whose property they had damaged and were ordered to repay the losses. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates repair in a direct meeting between the people involved, so that the offender pays the debt to the person harmed rather than only to the state. |
| Mental model | other. Restorative justice; the founders acted from Mennonite Christian peacemaking |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | conflict, justice power, relationship present |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://mcc.org/our-stories/resorative-justices-birth-canadian-legal-system |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *reconciliation* | the aim of restoring understanding between victim and offender, not only settling the damage |
| *restitution* | payment or work by the offender that repairs the victim's actual loss |
| *VORP* | Victim Offender Reconciliation Program, the name of the first program, run by Mennonite Central Committee Ontario |

#### Wisdom Council

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jim Rough |
| Date | 1993 |
| Scale | a community or public, an organization, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | About twelve people are drawn at random from a large system such as a city, a school, or a company; they meet for a few days with a dynamic facilitator, reach unanimous statements on the issues they choose, and present them to the whole system, and the process is repeated with new people at regular intervals. |
| What it assumes | Change comes when a random cross-section of a system reaches shared conclusions and presents them to everyone, so that the whole system can hear and consider its own thinking. |
| Mental model | civic and deliberative |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | decision choice, justice power, group process |
| Medium | talk, writing |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://partizipation.at/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/dynamicfacilitation-overview-jimrough.pdf; https://participedia.net/method/5367 |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *random selection* | Members are drawn by lot so that the council is a small cross-section of the larger system. |
| *unanimous results* | The council's statements are ones every member agrees with, reached through Dynamic Facilitation. |
| *Bürgerrat* | German for citizens' council: the name of the version developed by the Austrian state of Vorarlberg. |

#### Yarning

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples of Australia; set out as a research method by Dawn Bessarab (a Bard and Yjindjabandi woman) and Bridget Ng'andu |
| Date | traditional; research method 2010 |
| Scale | a community or public, a society's customs, two people as equals |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A yarn opens with a social yarn in which the two people share who they are and build trust before any topic is raised. The participant then tells their story from lived experience, and the listener follows the story and listens for what bears on the question rather than working through a fixed interview schedule. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates knowledge and care in relationship first, holding that the researcher must establish a relationship with the participant before gathering their story. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, memory past, trauma healing |
| Medium | talk |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |

| *yarn* | an Indigenous cultural form of conversation and storytelling, in which people tell their stories from lived experience |
| *social yarn* | the opening informal talk that establishes rapport and trust |

### A society's customs

This scale is the main one for 29 techniques, and the vault has met 4 of them.

#### Bar and bat mitzvah

| Originators | Jewish tradition; the first public bat mitzvah in the modern form was held by Rabbi Mordecai M. Kaplan for his daughter Judith in New York |

| Scale | a society's customs, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | At twelve or thirteen a Jewish young person becomes responsible for their own actions and for knowing Jewish law and ethics. After preparation, they are usually called up to the Torah on the first Sabbath after the birthday to recite the blessings or read the portion, and some give a d'var Torah, a short teaching on the reading. |
| What it assumes | The rite locates adulthood in public accountability, marked when the community calls the young person up to read and bless in front of it. |

#### Carnival and ritual role reversal

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | European Christian festival before Lent, with antecedents in festivals such as the Roman Saturnalia; theorized as 'the carnivalesque' by Mikhail Bakhtin |
| Date | medieval, with ancient antecedents |
| Scale | a society's customs, a large group, a community or public |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | In the days before Lent, communities hold parades and street festivities in which masks and costumes let people set aside their everyday identities, social roles are reversed, and ordinary norms are suspended. In Bakhtin's account a mock king is crowned and then uncrowned, and people of every rank meet in free and familiar contact before the season of fasting begins. |
| What it assumes | The custom locates renewal in a bounded time when hierarchy is suspended and everyone takes part, after which ordinary order and the penitential season resume. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological |
| Structure | holding two poles; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | justice power, creativity play, identity self |
| Medium | movement dance, enactment role, voice music |
| Time | ongoing-community |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivalesque |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *reversal ritual* | a rite in which social roles are inverted and the norms of proper behaviour are suspended for a set time |
| *carnivalesque* | Bakhtin's term for the spirit of carnival, which suspends hierarchy through shared laughter and celebrates change |
| *crowning and uncrowning* | the carnival rite of making a mock king and then dethroning him, which shows that every authority is relative |
| *Shrovetide* | the name for the carnival season in some places, when Christians also confessed their sins before Lent |

#### Dadirri (inner deep listening and quiet still awareness)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | The Ngan'gikurunggurr and Ngen'giwumirri peoples of the Daly River region, Northern Territory, Australia; presented by Miriam-Rose Ungunmerr-Baumann |
| Date | traditional; presented publicly in 1988 |
| Scale | a society's customs, one person alone, the more-than-human, a community or public |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A person sits on a riverbank or walks among the trees in silent awareness, listening without hurrying and without need of words. In community the same listening means hearing the stories told and sung over the years and waiting for the right time, the right people, and proper preparation before ceremonies and meetings. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates renewal and understanding in patient listening and waiting; Miriam-Rose Ungunmerr-Baumann says that when she experiences dadirri she is made whole again. |
| Limits and critiques | Ungunmerr-Baumann asks that the listening go both ways: other Australians are to listen to Aboriginal people and wait with them, not only take up the practice for themselves. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, spirit ultimate, grief, nature ecology |
| Medium | silence, nature place |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.dadirri.org.au/wp-content/uploads/2015/03/Dadirri-Inner-Deep-Listening-M-R-Ungunmerr-Bauman-Refl1.pdf; https://www.naidoc.org.au/award-finalist/dr-miriam-rose-ungunmerr-baumann-am |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *dadirri* | in Miriam-Rose Ungunmerr-Baumann's words, 'inner, deep listening and quiet, still awareness' |
| *Ngangikurungkurr* | the name of her people, meaning 'Deep Water Sounds' |

| Date | traditional; taught in the West by Sobonfu Somé (died 2017) |
| Scale | a society's customs, a large group |

| What happens | A ritual keeper gathers a community to grieve together over several days rather than hours. Karla McLaren reports that the ritual she attended with Sobonfu Somé lasted two full days and that in Sobonfu's village such rituals last three. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates the completion of grief in a community mourning together over days, rather than in private sorrow. |

#### Día de los Muertos (Indigenous festivity dedicated to the dead)

| Field | Entry |

| Date | traditional; inscribed by UNESCO in 2008 (proclaimed 2003) |
| Scale | a society's customs, a large group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | At the end of October and the beginning of November, families lay paths of flower petals, candles, and offerings from the cemetery to the home, cook the dead relatives' favourite dishes, and decorate home shrines and tombs with flowers and handicrafts such as paper cut-outs. The festivity receives the dead, who are believed to return to the living for a short time. |
| What it assumes | The custom locates the relationship with the dead in yearly hospitality, in which families prepare to receive relatives who are believed to return. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological |

| Medium | ritual object, art image |
| Time | ongoing-community |

|---|---|

#### Hajj

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Islam; one of the Five Pillars, with its rites taught by the Prophet Muhammad |
| Date | AD 632 (Muhammad's pilgrimage), with older roots at the Kaaba |
| Scale | a society's customs, a large group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Pilgrims enter the state of ihram, men wearing two white seamless cloths, and recite the talbiyah. From the eighth to the twelfth or thirteenth day of Dhu al-Hijjah they walk seven times around the Kaaba, go seven times between the hills of Safa and Marwah, stand in vigil at Arafat, spend a night at Muzdalifah, stone three pillars at Mina, offer a sacrifice, cut or shave their hair, and circle the Kaaba again. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates change in fulfilling a religious duty in the company of millions of other Muslims, performing in order the rites that Muhammad taught. |
| Limits and critiques | Only Muslims may enter Mecca to perform it, and it is required once in a lifetime only of those who are physically and financially able. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, identity self |
| Medium | movement dance, ritual object, voice music, nature place |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajj |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ihram* | the state of consecration, with plain white garments for men, that pilgrims enter before the rites |
| *tawaf* | walking seven times counter-clockwise around the Kaaba |
| *sa'i* | going seven times between the hills of Safa and Marwah |
| *wuquf* | standing in contemplative vigil at Arafat on the ninth day, one of the most significant rites |

#### Hoʻoponopono (family practice)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Native Hawaiian families; described by Mary Kawena Pukui (the Kaʻū family style) in Nānā I Ke Kumu with E. W. Haertig and C. A. Lee (Queen Liliʻuokalani Children's Center) |
| Date | traditional; documented 1972 |
| Scale | a society's customs, a small group, a community or public |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | The family agrees to five commitments, including truthfulness and confidentiality, and chooses a haku, a fair leader respected by all. After an opening prayer and a clear statement of the problem, the family discusses each layer of the trouble, members confess, ask for and give forgiveness, release one another, and the session closes with prayer. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates healing in the family's restored relationships, reached through truthful speaking, mutual forgiveness, and release in the presence of the family and its spiritual guardians. |
| Limits and critiques | A modern solitary version, Self I-Dentity through Hoʻoponopono, introduced by Morrnah Simeona around 1980 and now widely sold with a four-phrase formula, is practised by one person alone and differs from the family practice Pukui described. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | conflict, relationship present, spirit ultimate, emotion |
| Medium | talk, silence |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://peacemaking.narf.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/5.-Hooponopono-paper.pdf; https://www.hawaiimagazine.com/how-hooponopono-can-bring-people-together/; https://hooponopono-asia.org/en/about-morrnah/morrnah-nalamaku-simeona-lifetime-history/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *haku* | the unbiased leader of the session, agreed on by everyone present |
| *kūkulu kumuhana* | the gathering of the family's strength toward a shared goal, which includes the haku's statement of the problem |
| *mahiki* | the discussion that peels away the problem layer by layer |
| *mihi* | forgiveness, repentance, apology, and confession |
| *kala* | to release, untie, and free one another from the fault |

#### Ifoga (Samoan ceremonial apology)

| Scale | a society's customs, a community or public |

| What happens | The family of the person who caused serious harm sits in front of the house of the wronged family, covered with fine mats, traditionally at night and bringing firewood, stones, and banana leaves. Forgiveness is given when the wronged family removes the mats, and speeches and gifts are then exchanged. |
| What it assumes | The rite locates repair in the offending family's public humbling before the injured family, which leaves the power to forgive with the injured family. |
| Limits and critiques | An ifoga does not stop criminal prosecution, though courts may consider it at sentencing as a show of remorse. |

#### Inípi (Lakota purification ceremony; sweat lodge)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Lakota people, one of their Seven Sacred Ceremonies; related sweat ceremonies belong to other Plains and Indigenous nations of the Americas |
| Date | traditional, undated |
| Scale | a society's customs, a small group, the more-than-human |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | In a low, dome-shaped lodge made of saplings and coverings, water is poured over heated stones, and participants pray and sing in the heat and steam under a ceremonial leader. The phrase Mitákuye Oyás'iŋ is spoken at the beginning and end of Lakota ceremonies. |
| What it assumes | The ceremony locates purification and renewal in prayer, heat, and breath shared in the lodge, within a kinship that includes all living things. |
| Limits and critiques | Lakota, Dakota, and Nakota representatives declared in 1993 that these ceremonies are only for their nations, their spiritual leaders state that only someone who speaks a Plains language and has earned the rite may run it, and untrained non-Native imitations have caused injuries and deaths, including three deaths at James Arthur Ray's retreat near Sedona in 2009. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | spirit ultimate, body, relationship present, nature ecology |
| Medium | breath body, voice music, ritual object, nature place |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inipi; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweat_lodge; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitakuye_Oyasin |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *inípi (iníkaǧapi)* | 'they revitalize themselves', the Lakota name for the purification ceremony |
| *Mitákuye Oyás'iŋ* | 'all my relatives': a phrase that ends prayers and names kinship with people, animals, plants, stones, and waters |
| *Tunkašila (Tun-ca-s'i-la)* | the Sacred Grandfathers, whom the leader addresses in prayer |

#### Japanese tea ceremony (chanoyu, chadō)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Sen no Rikyū and the wabi-cha tradition; carried on by the Omotesenke, Urasenke, and Mushakōjisenke schools |
| Date | 16th century; the phrase ichi-go ichi-e fixed by Ii Naosuke in the mid-19th century |
| Scale | a society's customs, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A host prepares and serves tea to a few guests in a prescribed order, from a simple gathering with sweets and thin tea to a formal gathering of up to four hours with a meal, thick tea, and thin tea. Guests enter through a low door that makes each of them bow, examine the utensils, and treat the occasion as a meeting that will happen only once. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates attention and respect in a precisely ordered act of hospitality, in which host and guests meet fully on an occasion that will not recur. |
| Mental model | contemplative, nondual. The fit is loose: chanoyu is a disciplined practice of hospitality and attention governed by four named principles |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, relationship present, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | ritual object, silence |
