The hosts make speeches, recite oral histories, perform dances, publicly transfer names and titles to new holders, affirm rights to territories and resources, and give away wealth to the guests. |
| What it assumes | The custom locates status, rights, and relationships in their public affirmation before invited guests, confirmed by giving away wealth; for nations such as the Haida it remains a basis of governance. |
| Limits and critiques | Canada criminalized the potlatch as part of its assimilation policy, and the practice continued underground until the ban was repealed in 1951. |

#### Rites of passage (rites de passage)

| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | A community first separates a person from their former status, then keeps them for a time in an in-between condition outside ordinary rules, and finally incorporates them into the community in a new status. Turner describes the middle phase as liminal, 'betwixt and between', and the bond that forms among people passing through it together as communitas. |
| What it assumes | The model locates change in a socially marked crossing: a status changes when a community separates a person, keeps them in an in-between condition, and receives them back. |

| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, meaning purpose, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | ritual object, enactment role, movement dance |
| Time | series |

| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_of_passage |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A person who has done wrong abandons the wrong and resolves never to repeat it, regrets the past, and confesses aloud. Before Yom Kippur, people seek forgiveness from anyone they have wronged, because the day atones for wrongs between a person and God but not for wrongs between people until the person wronged has been appeased. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates change in the person's own turning, completed by spoken confession and, for harm done to others, by going to them and seeking their forgiveness. |
| Limits and critiques | The Mishnah warns that someone who says 'I will sin and then repent' is not given the opportunity to repent, so the practice refuses to serve as a planned escape. |
| Mental model | contemplative, theistic |
| Structure | moving through stages; a method |
| Subjects | relationship present, spirit ultimate, conflict, identity self |
| Medium | talk, reading text |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.sefaria.org/Mishneh_Torah,_Repentance.2.2; https://www.sefaria.org/Mishnah_Yoma.8.9 |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *teshuvah* | repentance, literally 'return': abandoning a wrong, regretting it, confessing it aloud, and resolving not to repeat it |
| *Yom Kippur* | the Day of Atonement, which does not atone for harm done to another person until that person is appeased |
| *Days of Awe* | the period from Rosh Hashanah to Yom Kippur, given to repentance |

#### The Irish wake and keening (tórramh; caoineadh)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Irish tradition |
| Date | traditional; keening declined from the 18th century and was nearly extinct by the mid-20th century |
| Scale | a society's customs, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | Family and friends keep a continuous watch over the dead person at home for one or two days before burial, while the family offers hospitality, the Rosary may be said, and people tell stories, sing, and sometimes dance. In the older form a keening woman led a sung lament naming the dead person's lineage and virtues and the survivors' grief, improvised as feeling dictated, while mourners rocked and knelt. |
| What it assumes | The custom locates the work of mourning in the community's continuous presence with the dead and the bereaved; the folklorist Patricia Lysaght reads its revelry as a way of reasserting the life of the community in the face of death. |
| Limits and critiques | Church opposition and changing funeral practices contributed to the near disappearance of keening by the middle of the twentieth century. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | grief, death finitude, relationship absent |
| Medium | voice music, talk, ritual object, movement dance |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keening; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_(ceremony) |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *tórramh (faire)* | the Irish words for the wake, the watch kept over the dead before burial |
| *caoineadh* | keening: a sung, partly improvised lament for the dead |
| *bean chaointe* | the keening woman, often a hired mourner, who led the lament |
| *gol* | the cry, the wordless part of the keen |

### The more-than-human

This scale is the main one for 12 techniques, and the vault has met 2 of them.

#### Animal-assisted therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Boris Levinson, who found a non-verbal child talking to his dog; earlier, Sigmund Freud kept his chow Jofi in analytic sessions |
| Date | 1961 (Levinson's observation) |
| Scale | the more-than-human, two people, one helping |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A trained professional includes an animal, often a dog, in goal-directed sessions meant to improve a patient's social, emotional, or cognitive functioning. Levinson came to the method after leaving his dog alone with a child who did not speak and returning to find the child talking to the dog. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates part of the help in the animal's presence, which does not react with shock to what a person says and can calm and engage them. |
| Limits and critiques | Reviews find it unclear how much the animal itself contributes, and there are risks of infection for immunocompromised patients and of stress for the animals. |
| Mental model | other. A clinical adjunct used within many kinds of therapy |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, body, trauma healing |
| Medium | touch, talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal-assisted_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *animal-assisted therapy (AAT)* | goal-directed treatment delivered by a professional with an animal as part of the work |
| *animal-assisted activities* | visits and contact with animals that have no structured therapeutic goal |

#### Council of All Beings

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | John Seed and Joanna Macy, with Pat Fleming and Arne Naess (Thinking Like a Mountain: Toward a Council of All Beings) |
| Date | 1980s; the book appeared in 1988 |
| Scale | the more-than-human, a small group, a large group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | Participants spend time outdoors until another being or part of the natural world presents itself to them, make a mask for it, and then sit in council speaking as that being about what is happening to it and what it asks of humans. At points the humans present set their masks aside and listen. |
| What it assumes | The ritual locates change in people imaginatively taking the place of other beings and listening to them, which it holds extends a person's sense of self to include other living beings. |
| Mental model | other. Deep ecology, the movement named by Arne Naess in 1973 |
| Structure | the self or group as many; a method |
| Subjects | nature ecology, identity self, grief, creativity play |
| Medium | enactment role, art image, talk, nature place |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Seed; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joanna_Macy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_ecology; general knowledge, unverified (the steps of the ritual) |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Council of All Beings* | a ritual gathering in which people speak on behalf of other beings of the Earth |
| *deep ecology* | Arne Naess's philosophy, which rejects human supremacy and holds, in his words, a 'biospherical egalitarianism' in which all living beings share in value |

#### Ecotherapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Howard Clinebell (Ecotherapy: Healing Ourselves, Healing the Earth); grounded in the ecopsychology of Robert Greenway and Theodore Roszak |
| Date | 1990s (Roszak named ecopsychology in 1992; Clinebell's book 1996) |
| Scale | the more-than-human, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A counsellor or therapist brings the person's relationship with the natural world into the work, for example by working in natural settings and by exploring how the person's distress connects to the state of the living world. The pain is examined in that wider connection rather than only as individual or family pathology. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates part of personal distress in a damaged relationship with the natural world and seeks healing by restoring that connection. |
| Mental model | other. Ecopsychology, which places personal pain in its connection to the living world |
| Structure | reading a difficulty by what it lacks; a method |
| Subjects | nature ecology, emotion, spirit ultimate, body |
| Medium | nature place, talk |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Clinebell; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotherapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ecotherapy* | healing of persons together with the Earth, from the title of Howard Clinebell's 1996 book |
| *ecopsychology* | the field, named by Theodore Roszak in 1992, that studies the emotional bond between humans and the Earth |
| *more-than-human world* | the living world beyond people, in whose connection ecopsychology places personal pain |

#### Equine-assisted psychotherapy (equine-facilitated psychotherapy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Mental health practitioners in the United States, organized through bodies such as PATH International and EAGALA; hippotherapy developed earlier in Germany, Austria, and Switzerland |
| Date | 1990s (hippotherapy 1960s) |
| Scale | the more-than-human, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A mental health professional works with one or more clients and one or more horses, often on the ground rather than riding, through tasks such as grooming, feeding, and leading. Clients reflect on what happens between them and the horse in order to learn about themselves and others. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in live physical interaction with a large animal, whose responses give the client immediate experience to reflect on. |
| Limits and critiques | Reviews find too little high-quality evidence to justify promoting equine therapies for mental disorders, and critics warn that they should not divert resources from evidence-based treatments. |
| Mental model | other. Experiential therapy with horses; programmes differ on whether the horse is a co-facilitator or a tool |
| Structure | awareness turned back on itself; a method |
| Subjects | emotion, relationship present, body, identity self |
| Medium | touch, movement dance, nature place |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equine-assisted_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *equine-assisted psychotherapy (EAP)* | psychotherapy that uses experiential work with horses and may involve no riding |
| *equine-assisted learning (EAL)* | an experiential approach with horses aimed at developing life skills |
| *groundwork* | work with the horse from the ground, such as leading and grooming, rather than from the saddle |
| *hippotherapy* | physical, occupational, or speech therapy that uses the horse's movement to challenge the rider's posture and balance |

#### Gratitude and reciprocity with the land (the gift economy)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Robin Wall Kimmerer, botanist and enrolled member of the Citizen Potawatomi Nation, drawing on Potawatomi and other Indigenous teachings |
| Date | Braiding Sweetgrass, 2013; 'The Serviceberry', 2022 |
| Scale | the more-than-human, one person alone, a community or public |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A person receives what the land provides, such as berries, as a gift rather than as a resource and takes only what they need. They respond first with gratitude and then with a gift in return, such as weeding, watering, a song of thanks, or support for protecting habitat, and they pass the abundance on by sharing it. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates right relationship with the land in naming the world as a gift, which Kimmerer says makes a person both happy and accountable. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | nature ecology, relationship present, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | nature place, voice music |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | `archive/reading — abram and kimmerer on the more-than-human (1996 and 2013).md` |
| Sources | https://emergencemagazine.org/essay/the-serviceberry/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Wall_Kimmerer |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *gift economy* | a way of providing for one another in which wealth means having enough to share and the currency is relationship, expressed as gratitude and reciprocity |
| *reciprocity* | giving a gift in return for what has been received, directly to the land or indirectly |
| *Windigo* | the monster of Potawatomi culture, who suffers from the illness of taking too much and sharing too little |

#### Haudenosaunee Thanksgiving Address (Greetings to the Natural World)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Haudenosaunee (Six Nations) tradition; the widely used English version is by John Stokes and Kanawahienton (David Benedict, Mohawk), from the Mohawk of Rokwaho (Dan Thompson), with original inspiration from Tekaronianekon (Jake Swamp) |
| Date | traditional; this English version published 1993 |
| Scale | the more-than-human, a society's customs, a large group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | At the start of a school day or of a social, cultural, or political gathering, a speaker thanks in turn the people, the Earth, the waters, fish, plants, food plants, medicine herbs, animals, trees, birds, the four winds, the thunderers, the sun, the moon, the stars, the enlightened teachers, and the Creator. Each greeting ends with the words 'Now our minds are one', and the closing leaves it to each individual to send thanks for anything that was left out. |
| What it assumes | The address locates the readiness of a gathering in shared gratitude to the whole natural world, spoken at the beginning so that the minds of everyone present are brought together. |
| Limits and critiques | Robin Wall Kimmerer asked the Onondaga spiritual leader Oren Lyons for permission before publishing the address in Braiding Sweetgrass, since some Indigenous teachings are not meant to be shared with outsiders. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | nature ecology, spirit ultimate, group process, relationship present |
| Medium | talk, nature place |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://americanindian.si.edu/environment/pdf/01_02_Thanksgiving_Address.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haudenosaunee; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braiding_Sweetgrass |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Now our minds are one* | the refrain after each greeting, by which the gathered people join their minds together |
| *Greetings to the Natural World* | the English title of the address, which thanks each part of creation in order |

#### Shinrin-yoku (forest bathing)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Tomohide Akiyama of Japan's Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries |
| Date | 1982 |
| Scale | the more-than-human, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| What happens | A person spends a few unhurried hours walking or resting in a forest for relaxation, sometimes over several days. Japan promoted the practice from 1982 to encourage people to visit forests and to reduce stress during a period of rapid industrialization. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates recovery in the body's response to forest surroundings, which studies associate with lower stress and better mood. |
| Limits and critiques | A 2012 systematic review found positive effects but not enough evidence to support clinical guidelines or to show causation, and the reported immune effects come from small trials. |
| Mental model | somatic |
| Structure | shifting a state directly; a method |
| Subjects | body, attention awareness, nature ecology, emotion |
| Medium | nature place, breath body, movement dance |
| Time | session |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_bathing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *shinrin-yoku* | 'forest bathing': spending time in the atmosphere of a forest for relaxation and health |
| *forest therapy* | the wider practice of using time in forests to improve mental or physical health |

#### Sit spot and Coyote Mentoring (nature connection mentoring)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Jon Young and Ingwe (Norman Powell), Wilderness Awareness School; Young was mentored by Tom Brown Jr. |
| Date | 1983 (Wilderness Awareness School); Coyote's Guide to Connecting with Nature, 2007 |
| Scale | the more-than-human, one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| What happens | A person returns again and again to one chosen place in nature and sits there in quiet awareness, getting to know it in all weathers, times of day, and seasons and noticing what the birds and animals are doing. A mentor draws the learning out through questions, starting with ones the learner can answer and building toward the edge of what the learner knows, instead of giving answers. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates connection with nature in repeated attentive presence in one place and in curiosity that a mentor draws out with questions. |
| Mental model | learning theory. Mentoring by questioning, informed by Young's study of mentoring among hunter-gatherer peoples |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a method |
| Subjects | attention awareness, nature ecology, learning skill |
| Medium | nature place, silence, talk |
| Time | lifelong |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://wildernessawareness.org/articles/sit-spots/; https://wildernessawareness.org/articles/coyote-mentors/; https://www.wildernessawareness.org/about/; https://www.8shields.org/origins |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *sit spot* | a particular place in nature that a person visits over and over to sit in quiet awareness and build a relationship with it |
| *Coyote Mentoring* | teaching that draws learning out of students through their curiosity rather than forcing it in |
| *the art of questioning* | a sequence of questions that moves from what the learner already knows toward new discovery |
| *edge* | the limit of a learner's engagement or knowledge, which a mentor watches for and works at |
| *core routines* | repeated practices, such as the sit spot, that build awareness of nature |

#### The Work That Reconnects

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Joanna Macy, with colleagues including Molly Young Brown; first called Despair and Empowerment Work |
| Date | late 1970s-1980s (Despair and Personal Power in the Nuclear Age, 1983; Coming Back to Life, 1998) |
| Scale | the more-than-human, a large group, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | A workshop leads participants through a spiral of four stages: beginning with gratitude, honouring their pain for the world, seeing with new eyes, and going forth into action. The turning point comes when participants stop privatizing, repressing, or pathologizing their grief and fear for the world and honour them together. |
| What it assumes | The work locates change in people honouring their shared pain for the world, which it holds leads to new perception and to action on behalf of the living world. |
| Mental model | other. Ecological and Buddhist: Macy joined the Buddhist teaching of mutual causality with general systems theory and deep ecology |
| Structure | a repeating cycle; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | nature ecology, grief, future vision, spirit ultimate |
| Medium | talk, enactment role |
| Time | series |
| In the vault | `ops/queue/candidates.md` |
| Sources | https://www.joannamacy.net/main; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joanna_Macy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *the spiral* | the four-stage sequence of the work: gratitude, honouring our pain for the world, seeing with new eyes, and going forth |
| *pain for the world* | grief, fear, and despair about the state of the world, which the work honours instead of treating as private or as illness |
| *the Great Turning* | Macy's name for the shift from an industrial growth society to a sustainable civilization |
| *Despair and Empowerment Work* | the original name of the work |

#### Welcome to Country

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples of Australia; contemporary form from a welcome at the 1973 Aquarius Festival on Bundjalung Country and one devised by Richard Walley and Ernie Dingo for Māori performers in Perth (dated 1976 in one account and 1978 in another); named by Rhoda Roberts in the 1980s |
| Date | traditional; contemporary form from the 1970s |
| Scale | the more-than-human, a society's customs, a community or public, a large group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | At the opening of an event, a recognised elder or Traditional Owner of the local clan or language group welcomes those present onto their Country, sometimes with a smoking ceremony, music, or dance. When no Traditional Owner is available, the event's host gives an Acknowledgement of Country instead, paying respect to the Traditional Owners and their elders. |
| What it assumes | The ceremony locates respectful presence on land in recognition by, and welcome from, the people whose Country it is. |
| Limits and critiques | Only a recognised elder or Traditional Owner may perform a Welcome, and as the ritual has become common at public events, many people work to keep its words meaningful rather than routine. |
| Mental model | Indigenous and relational |
| Structure | a word said aloud that acts; a method |
| Subjects | nature ecology, relationship present, justice power, identity self |
| Medium | talk, ritual object, voice music, movement dance |
| Time | moment |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welcome_to_Country |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Country* | a people's ancestral lands and seas, understood as an interdependent relationship that includes culture, law, kin, and identity |
| *Welcome to Country* | the welcome given by a Traditional Owner to visitors on their land |
| *Acknowledgement of Country* | a statement of respect for the Traditional Owners, given by the host when a welcome cannot be performed |
| *smoking ceremony* | burning native plants; it may once have given visitors the scent of the home group to show they had been welcomed |

#### Wilderness therapy (outdoor behavioral healthcare)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Roots in Kurt Hahn's Outward Bound; developed as a therapeutic field in North America |
| Date | 1940s roots; therapeutic expeditions from the 1960s-1970s |
| Scale | the more-than-human, a small group |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| What happens | Staff lead clients, often adolescents and young adults, on extended expeditions of hiking, camping, and survival skills, combined with individual and group therapy. Programmes vary widely in clinical oversight, length, and remoteness. |
| What it assumes | The technique locates change in challenge, group living, and immersion in nature away from ordinary surroundings, combined with therapy. |
| Limits and critiques | The field lacks a common definition and consistent regulation, research results are mixed, deaths and abuse have been reported in some programmes, and scholars question whether the involuntary transport of young people into treatment can be ethical. |
| Mental model | other. Combines psychotherapy, group process, and experiential learning in wild settings |
| Structure | tending conditions; a method |
| Subjects | trauma healing, identity self, learning skill, body |
| Medium | nature place, movement dance, talk |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilderness_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *outdoor behavioral healthcare* | the field's formal name for therapy delivered through wilderness expeditions |
| *Outward Bound* | Kurt Hahn's outdoor education organization, whose mix of physical challenge, teamwork, and nature preceded the field |

#### Wilderness vision fast (School of Lost Borders)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Steven Foster and Meredith Little (School of Lost Borders), adapting the rite-of-passage model of Arnold van Gennep |
| Date | 1970s onward |
| Scale | the more-than-human, one person alone, a small group |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| What happens | After preparation with guides and a group, each participant goes alone into wild country to fast for several days and nights and then returns to the group. Each tells the story of the time alone, and the guides reflect the story back so the person can hear its meaning, rather than interpreting it for them. |
| What it assumes | The practice locates change in a chosen period of solitude and fasting in wild nature, completed by telling the story to witnesses on return. |
| Limits and critiques | Indigenous leaders have criticized non-Native programmes that borrow the term 'vision quest' as cultural appropriation, and the 1993 Lakota declaration against exploiters of Lakota spirituality names the vision quest among the ceremonies to be protected. |
| Mental model | ritual and anthropological |
| Structure | moving through stages; a sequence of phases |
| Subjects | identity self, meaning purpose, nature ecology, death finitude |
| Medium | nature place, silence, talk |
| Time | residential |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_quest; general knowledge, unverified (founders' terms and format; the School of Lost Borders website refused access) |
| Verified at those sources | no |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *severance, threshold, incorporation* | the three phases of the fast: leaving ordinary life, the time alone in the wilderness, and the return to community |
| *mirroring* | the guides' practice of telling a participant's story back to them without imposing an interpretation |

## Part 2. The single moves, by what they do

### Questions

#### the open question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks a question that cannot be answered with yes, no, or a single fact, such as 'What is that like for you?' |
| Why it is used | It invites the person to tell their story in their own words without the question choosing the answer for them. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals, a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Other names | open questions, the O in OARS (motivational interviewing); open-ended questions (counselling skills training) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivational_interviewing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the clean question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks a short question of fixed form that uses only the person's own words, such as 'And what kind of X is that X?' or 'And is there anything else about X?', and adds no ideas, metaphors, or assumptions of their own. |
| Why it is used | It keeps the content entirely the person's own, so that what they discover comes from their own words and images. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | clean questions (Clean Language, David Grove); the questions of Symbolic Modelling (James Lawley and Penny Tompkins, who built on Grove's work) |
| In the vault | `notes/how much structure makes recognition possible without determining what is recognized.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_language |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the circular question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The therapist asks one family member about the relationship or the differences between other members who are present, for example who is most upset when a certain thing happens, and uses each answer to shape the next question. |
| Why it is used | It lets the family hear how each member sees the pattern between them, so the problem appears as a pattern of relationships rather than as one person's fault. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, an organization |
| Other names | circular questioning (Milan systemic family therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_Selvini_Palazzoli; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7364036/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the scaling question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks the person to place their situation on a scale, usually from 0 to 10, then asks what puts it at that number rather than lower and what one step higher would look like. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person measure progress by their own standard and name the next small step themselves. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | scaling questions (solution-focused brief therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://solutionfocused.net/what-is-solution-focused-therapy/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the miracle question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks the person to imagine that a miracle happens overnight while they sleep and the problem is solved, then asks them to describe in detail what they would notice first the next morning. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person describe the change they want in concrete, observable terms that they chose themselves. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | the miracle question (solution-focused brief therapy, Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg); questions of the Dream phase, envisioning 'what might be' (Appreciative Inquiry) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://solutionfocused.net/what-is-solution-focused-therapy/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the exception question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks about times when the problem was absent, weaker, or handled well, and then asks what was different at those times. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person find evidence of their own capacity in their own history instead of receiving it from the helper. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization, a community or public |
| Other names | exception questions, such as 'Are there times when this has been less of a problem?' (solution-focused brief therapy); coping questions, such as 'How have you managed to carry on?' (solution-focused brief therapy); unique outcomes, moments that contradict the problem story (narrative therapy); Discovery questions about people's experiences at their best (Appreciative Inquiry) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://solutionfocused.net/what-is-solution-focused-therapy/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### asking what is alive

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A person asks another, or themselves, what is alive in them right now, meaning which feelings are present and which needs those feelings point to. |
| Why it is used | In Rosenberg's account it turns attention from judgment and blame to feelings and needs, which he calls 'the language of life'. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group, one person alone |
| Other names | 'What's alive in you?' (Nonviolent Communication, Marshall Rosenberg); Calling the Room, which names what is alive between the people in it (a practice form in the book's own corpus) |
| In the vault | `notes/the practice forms specify when to begin and not how to tell one is finished.md` |
| Sources | https://www.naturalchild.org/articles/marshall_rosenberg/interview.html |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### asking 'what else?'

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | After the person answers, the listener asks whether there is anything else or anything more, and keeps asking until the person says there is nothing more. |
| Why it is used | It keeps the listener from settling on the first answer, which is rarely the whole or the most important one. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | 'And is there anything else about...?' (Clean Language); 'Is there more about that?' (Imago Dialogue); 'And what else?', the AWE question (Michael Bungay Stanier, The Coaching Habit, 2016) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_language; https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf; https://www.tobysinclair.com/post/book-summary-coaching-habit-by-michael-bungay-stanier |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the Socratic question

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The helper asks a sequence of questions that lead the person to examine the evidence, assumptions, and consequences of a belief, so that the person reaches a conclusion by their own reasoning. |
| Why it is used | It aims for the person to discover the conclusion themselves instead of being told it. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, one person alone |
| Other names | Socratic questioning and guided discovery (cognitive therapy, Aaron Beck and Judith Beck); the midwife's questioning, maieutics (Socrates in Plato's Theaetetus); Socratic questioning in critical-thinking education (Richard Paul and Linda Elder) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — plato, meno and theaetetus on drawing-out and wonder (c. 380 bce).md |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socratic_questioning; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_therapy; the vault, archive/reading — plato, meno and theaetetus on drawing-out and wonder (c. 380 bce).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### asking permission before offering

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | Before giving advice, information, or an observation, the helper asks whether the person wants to hear it, and accepts a no. |
| Why it is used | It leaves the person in charge of what enters the conversation, which also makes them more able to hear what is offered. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, one person and their parts |
| Other names | asking permission to give advice (motivational interviewing); getting permission from protective parts (Internal Family Systems) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivational_interviewing; https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/evolution-internal-family-systems-model-dr-richard-schwartz-ph-d |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

### Reflecting and receiving

#### reflecting back

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The listener says back, in their own words or the speaker's, what they understood the speaker to mean or feel, and leaves room for the speaker to correct it. |
| Why it is used | It lets the speaker hear their own meaning from outside and correct the listener, so the speaker's own account stays the reference. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals, a small group |
| Other names | reflection of feeling (client-centered therapy, Carl Rogers); testing understandings, or checking perceptions (Rogers's own proposed renaming, 1986); reflective listening (motivational interviewing); accurate reflection (the second level of validation, dialectical behavior therapy); paraphrasing (counselling skills training) |
| In the vault | `notes/reflection that returns authorship differs from reflection that takes it.md` |
| Sources | https://adpca.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/07/PCR14A2-Rogers-C-1986-Carl-Rogerss-Column.pdf; https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing; https://psychcentral.com/blog/emotionally-sensitive/2012/02/understanding-the-levels-of-validation |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### summarizing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The listener gathers several things the speaker has said into one short account, offers it back, and asks whether anything is missing. |
| Why it is used | It lets the speaker see the parts of what they said side by side and choose which part matters most to them next. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals, a small group, a large group |
| Other names | summaries, the S in OARS (motivational interviewing); summary mirror (Imago Dialogue) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing; https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### normalizing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The helper tells the person that their reaction is one that many people have in the same circumstances. |
| Why it is used | It lowers shame, so the person can look at the reaction instead of defending against it. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, a text or tool, no one present |
| Other names | normalizing, recognizing reactions that anyone would have (the fifth level of validation in Marsha Linehan's scheme, dialectical behavior therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://psychcentral.com/blog/emotionally-sensitive/2012/02/understanding-the-levels-of-validation |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### validation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The listener communicates that the other person's feeling or view makes sense given their situation or history, without having to agree with it. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person feel understood enough to keep speaking and to consider change without first having to defend themselves. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, two people as equals, a small group |
| Other names | validation, described in six levels from being present to radical genuineness (dialectical behavior therapy, Marsha Linehan, 1997); validation, the second step of the Imago Dialogue ('You make sense to me, and what makes sense is...') |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.researchgate.net/publication/232561580_Validation_and_psychotherapy; https://psychcentral.com/blog/emotionally-sensitive/2012/02/understanding-the-levels-of-validation; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotional_validation; https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the contact statement

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The therapist makes a very short statement that names the person's present experience rather than the content of their story, such as 'Sad, huh?' |
| Why it is used | It shows the person that they are being followed moment by moment and turns their attention toward what they are experiencing now. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | contact statements (Hakomi method, Ron Kurtz); brief reflection of feeling (person-centered practice) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### tracking

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator continuously watches the person's present experience as it shows in posture, breath, voice, gesture, and small changes of expression, without commenting on all of it. |
| Why it is used | It lets the facilitator follow the person's own process and pace instead of an agenda. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | tracking (Hakomi method) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### labelling a feeling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person, or a helper with them, puts a present feeling into a word, such as 'angry' or 'afraid'. |
| Why it is used | Putting a feeling into words has been measured to reduce the response of the brain's amygdala to distressing images, so the person can think about the feeling instead of reacting to it automatically. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, two people as equals, a text or tool, no one present |
| Other names | affect labeling (Matthew Lieberman and colleagues, 2007); 'name it to tame it' (Daniel Siegel and Tina Payne Bryson); naming feelings and the needs behind them (Nonviolent Communication) |
| In the vault | `archive/research — counterpoint round background.md` |
| Sources | https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2007.01916.x; https://www.naturalchild.org/articles/marshall_rosenberg/interview.html; the vault, `archive/research — counterpoint round background.md` |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the witness role

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | One person is given the role of watching another person's process without taking part, and afterward speaks about what they saw and what it stirred in them, without judging or interpreting the other. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person being witnessed be seen and received, and gives them a second view of their experience that stays theirs to use. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | three people, a small group, two people as equals |
| Other names | the witness, who watches the mover 'without judgement, projection or interpretation' (Authentic Movement, Mary Starks Whitehouse and Janet Adler); outsider witnesses in a definitional ceremony (narrative therapy); the audience, who witness an enactment and later share (psychodrama); the triad witness who 'speaks to the field, not just the content' (the author's Authentic Relating triad design, March 2026) |
| In the vault | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authentic_Movement; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy; https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2018.01263/full; the vault, `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### mirroring

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | In a structured dialogue between partners, the receiver repeats the sender's message word for word or in close paraphrase and asks 'Did I get that?' and 'Is there more about that?' before responding at all; then the two switch roles. |
| Why it is used | It keeps the receiver listening to what is said rather than to their own reactions and lets the sender feel fully heard. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people as equals, two people, one helping |
| Other names | mirroring, the first step of the Imago Dialogue ('Let me see if I've got you. You said...'); summary mirror (Imago Dialogue) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

### Slowing, pausing, and staying

#### silence and waiting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator or the group stays silent after a question or a statement for longer than ordinary conversation allows and does not fill the gap. In unprogrammed Quaker worship a whole meeting sits in silence, a person speaks only when moved to, and several minutes often pass between speakers. |
| Why it is used | It gives a person time to find their own answer before anyone else speaks. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, a large group, a society's customs |
| Other names | wait time (Mary Budd Rowe, science education, coined 1972); unprogrammed meeting for worship, sitting in silence until someone is moved to speak (Quakers); the silent form of circle (The Circle Way) |
| In the vault | `notes/teaching without words keeps the astonishing thing a live mystery the person meets directly.md` |
| Sources | https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED061103; https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/002248718603700110; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meeting_for_worship; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### pendulation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The practitioner guides the person's attention back and forth between activation or distress in the body and a place of settling or ease, in small cycles. |
| Why it is used | It lets the nervous system discharge activation gradually and builds the person's capacity to tolerate difficult sensation without being overwhelmed. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Other names | pendulation (Somatic Experiencing, Peter Levine) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4316402/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### titration

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The practitioner approaches difficult material in very small amounts, one small piece at a time, and lets the body settle before going further. |
| Why it is used | It prevents the person from being overwhelmed or re-traumatized. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone, a small group |
| Other names | titration, approaching trauma 'drop by drop' (Somatic Experiencing, Peter Levine) |
| In the vault | `notes/felt-shift.md` |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4316402/; https://traumahealing.org/se-101/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### resourcing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The practitioner helps the person find, and feel in the body, something that gives safety, strength, or comfort, such as a supportive person, a place, or a sensation of steadiness, before or during difficult work. |
| Why it is used | It gives the person a felt sense of safety or strength to return their attention to, so that difficult material can be approached without overwhelm. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, one person alone, a small group |
| Other names | resourcing (Somatic Experiencing); coping questions, such as 'How have you managed to prevent things from becoming worse?' (solution-focused brief therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4316402/; https://solutionfocused.net/what-is-solution-focused-therapy/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### grounding

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person brings attention to physical contact with the ground and to the immediate surroundings, for example by feeling the feet on the floor or naming things they can see and hear right now. |
| Why it is used | It returns attention to the present body and place when a person is overwhelmed by memory or emotion, or is dissociating. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group, a text or tool, no one present |
| Other names | grounding (bioenergetic analysis, Alexander Lowen); 5-4-3-2-1 sensory grounding, naming things one sees, hears, feels, smells and tastes (trauma therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Lowen; https://www.therapistaid.com/therapy-article/grounding-techniques-article |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### noting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The meditator silently names each experience as it arises, such as 'rising, falling' for the breath or 'hearing' for a sound, without further thinking about it. |
| Why it is used | It trains precise, moment-by-moment awareness and keeps the meditator from following the content of each thought. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| Scale | one person alone, a small group, a text or tool, no one present |
| Other names | noting, or mental noting, such as 'rising, falling' (Mahasi Sayadaw's vipassana method); noting (Shinzen Young's teaching, which adapts Mahasi's technique) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahasi_Sayadaw |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the pause

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | Someone stops the conversation or activity for a short, agreed time, such as three slow breaths, and everyone breathes and waits before going on. In The Circle Way a volunteer guardian rings a bell to call the pause, rings it again to end it, and anyone may ask for one. |
| Why it is used | It stops the ongoing activity so that people can notice what has just happened and choose how to continue. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group |
| Other names | practice the pause, called by the circle guardian's bell (The Circle Way) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### checking a word against the felt sense

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person goes back and forth between a word or image and the bodily felt sense it is meant to describe and checks whether the word fits; when it does not fit, they wait for the felt sense to give a better word. |
| Why it is used | It lets the body, rather than the fluency of a phrase, decide whether a description is accurate. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | one person alone, two people as equals, two people, one helping |
| Other names | resonating (Focusing, Eugene Gendlin); the felt sense's refusal of a word that does not fit, which the vault holds is what separates the felt sense from fluency (notes/the felt sense rejects formulations and that friction is what separates it from fluency.md) |
| In the vault | `notes/gendlin's focusing protocol already guards against the counterfeit shift.md` |
| Sources | https://www.focusing.org.uk/an-introduction-to-focusing; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focusing_(psychotherapy); the vault, archive/reading — gendlin, focusing and a process model (1978, 1997).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### suspending assumptions

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A person notices an assumption or reaction arising and keeps it in view without acting on it and without suppressing it; in Bohm's words, 'You don't believe them, nor do you disbelieve them'. |
| Why it is used | It lets a group or a person examine the thinking behind their reactions instead of defending it. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, one person alone |
| Other names | suspension (David Bohm's dialogue); epoché or bracketing (Edmund Husserl's phenomenology) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — bohm, on dialogue (1996).md |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohm_Dialogue; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoch%C3%A9; the vault, archive/reading — bohm, on dialogue (1996).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### staying with what arises

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person keeps attention on a feeling or sensation that is present, especially an unpleasant one, and lets it be there without trying to change, fix, or get away from it. |
| Why it is used | It lets a feeling change or end on its own, or show what it is about, which avoiding it would prevent. |
| Who decides | A, the person decides all content |
| Scale | one person alone, two people, one helping, one person and their parts, a small group |
| Other names | 'stay with it' (Gestalt therapy); keeping it company (Inner Relationship Focusing, Ann Weiser Cornell and Barbara McGavin); Allow, the second step of RAIN (Tara Brach's teaching); acceptance, letting unwanted experiences come and go without struggling with them (Acceptance and Commitment Therapy); the welcome (Joe Hudson's Art of Accomplishment) |
| In the vault | `notes/joe hudson.md` |
| Sources | http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm; https://iacp.ie/files/UserFiles/IJCP-Articles/2023/A%20simulated%20interview%20with%20Fritz%20Perls%20Part%202%20%E2%80%93%20The%20process%20of%20therapy%20sessions%20James%20C.%20Overholser,%20Ph.D.,%20ABPP.pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Relationship_Focusing; https://www.tarabrach.com/rain/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acceptance_and_commitment_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

### Structures for a group

#### the check-in

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | At the start of a meeting each person in turn says briefly how they are arriving and what brings them, usually without discussion or response from others. |
| Why it is used | It lets each person be heard once before the work begins and tells the group how its members are showing up; in The Circle Way's words, 'Nobody needs "fixing"'. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Other names | check-in, the first round of sharing (The Circle Way); the opening round (group facilitation) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf; https://www.iirp.edu/images/conf_downloads/IQxXAe_Components_of_Circle_Way.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the check-out or closing circle

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | At the end of a gathering each person in turn says briefly what they are taking with them, what they learned, or what they appreciated, and the facilitator then marks the close, for example with a short silence. |
| Why it is used | It signals that the gathering is over and lets each person name what they will take back into ordinary life. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Other names | check-out (The Circle Way); ceremonies that mark the closing of a circle (peacemaking circles, Kay Pranis); the closing circle (the author's own event script, May 2026) |
| In the vault | `notes/the book was written across the author's move to seattle to live in earnest.md` |
| Sources | https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf; https://www.beyondintractability.org/bksum/pranis-little |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the talking piece

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | An object is passed around the circle, and only the person holding it speaks while the others listen. |
| Why it is used | It slows the conversation, gives each voice uninterrupted time, and prevents cross-talk. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, a community or public, a society's customs |
| Other names | talking piece (peacemaking circles, Kay Pranis, drawing on Native American traditions); talking piece circle (The Circle Way) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.beyondintractability.org/bksum/pranis-little; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the round or go-around

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks every person in the circle to speak in turn on the same question, one after another, with no one skipped and no cross-talk. In the Gestalt game called making the rounds, a single person instead says something to each member in turn. |
| Why it is used | It gathers every perspective in the room, not only those of the quickest or most confident speakers. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Other names | rounds, in which each person speaks in turn during decisions (sociocracy); making the rounds, in which one person addresses each group member in turn (Gestalt group therapy, Levitsky and Perls); talking piece circle (The Circle Way) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociocracy; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf; https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED082090.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the fishbowl

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A few people, traditionally five, talk in an inner circle while everyone else sits around them and listens. In an open fishbowl one inner chair stays empty, anyone from the outer circle may take it, and a current member then leaves. |
| Why it is used | It lets a large group hear a real conversation up close while keeping the number of speakers small. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | open fishbowl and closed fishbowl (group facilitation and classroom teaching) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishbowl_(conversation) |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the pair-share

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | People first think alone for a minute, then talk in pairs, and then share with a larger group; 1-2-4-All adds a round in fours before the whole group. |
| Why it is used | It lets everyone form and test their own view before the full group hears it, which lowers the risk of speaking and includes quieter people. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a large group, a small group, an organization, two people as equals |
| Other names | think-pair-share (Frank Lyman, University of Maryland, 1981); 1-2-4-All (Liberating Structures, Henri Lipmanowicz and Keith McCandless) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.liberatingstructures.com/1-2-4-all; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Think-pair-share |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### harvesting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The hosts decide in advance how what a group discovers will be gathered and made visible, such as notes, drawings, a shared report, or an agreement to act, and they collect it during and after the conversations. |
| Why it is used | It makes the group's collective insight visible and usable after the event; the Art of Hosting's own description is 'we plan our conversation design around the harvest we want to produce'. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a large group, an organization, a community or public, a small group |
| Other names | the harvest (Art of Hosting); harvesting and sharing collective discoveries, the seventh design principle of the World Café (Juanita Brown and David Isaacs); debriefing documents (Open Space Technology) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://artofhosting.org/what-is-aoh/; https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/574692/the-world-cafe-by-juanita-brown-with-david-isaacs-and-the-world-cafe-community/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Space_Technology |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the consent round

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator asks each person in turn whether they have a reasoned, paramount objection to a proposal, meaning a reason it would harm the group's ability to work toward its aim; the proposal passes when no such objection remains. |
| Why it is used | It lets a group decide without requiring everyone's agreement while giving every member a real voice to stop a harmful decision. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, an organization, a community or public |
| Other names | consent round, asking for any reasoned and paramount objection (sociocracy, Gerard Endenburg) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociocracy; https://www.sociocracy.info/consensus-consent-objections/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the advice process

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | Any person may make any decision after seeking advice from everyone who will be meaningfully affected and from people with expertise; the advice must be considered, but the decision-maker does not have to follow it. |
| Why it is used | It distributes decision-making authority across an organization while keeping each decision informed by the people it affects. |
| Who decides | E, no live facilitator; a structure, text, or custom does the work |
| Scale | an organization |
| Other names | the advice process (Dennis Bakke at AES Corporation); the advice process in self-managing organizations (Frederic Laloux, Reinventing Organizations) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://reinventingorganizationswiki.com/en/theory/decision-making/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### group agreements

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | At the start, the group states or builds a short list of commitments about how members will treat one another, such as keeping confidentiality and listening without judgment, and returns to them when needed. |
| Why it is used | It gives participants a stated, shared basis for trust, so they know what to expect from each other. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization, a community or public |
| Other names | agreements, such as 'Listen with curiosity and compassion, withholding judgment' (The Circle Way); guidelines agreed by consensus (peacemaking circles, Kay Pranis); community agreements (education and anti-oppression facilitation); the five agreements of the author's Authentic Relating events |
| In the vault | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Sources | https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf; https://www.beyondintractability.org/bksum/pranis-little; https://cep.barnard.edu/crafting-community-agreements; the vault, `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### step up, step back

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | Each participant watches their own share of the talking: people who usually stay quiet are invited to speak more, and people who usually speak a lot are invited to hold back so others can speak. |
| Why it is used | It distributes speaking time more evenly, especially where social power shapes who usually talks. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, an organization |
| Other names | step up, step back (community agreements in education); move up, move up (a variant in anti-oppression facilitation) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://cep.barnard.edu/crafting-community-agreements |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the appreciation round or blessing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | Near the end of a gathering, people take turns speaking an appreciation, a thanks, or a good wish to the group or to particular people. |
| Why it is used | It closes the gathering by acknowledging what was received, so that people leave with recognition rather than with things left unsaid. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, a large group, a society's customs |
| Other names | a closing round in which people say what they learned, heard, or appreciated (The Circle Way); leaving 'with the blessings of the group' (The Circle Way) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://static1.squarespace.com/static/55597e72e4b0f7284bff49e0/t/689f7ba1117af90b50d52531/1755282337035/The+Circle+Way+Pocket+Guide.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

### Enactment and experiment

#### the little experiment in mindfulness

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | While the person is in a quiet, mindful state, the therapist offers a small deliberate input, such as a sentence ('Your life belongs to you'), a touch, or a gesture, and both notice together what happens inside the person in response. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person discover their own beliefs and reactions from their direct response rather than being told about them. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | the probe, 'What happens when I say...' (Hakomi method, Ron Kurtz); the experiment (Gestalt therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf; https://hakomi.com/a-little-history/; http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### doubling

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A group member stands beside or behind the protagonist and speaks aloud the thoughts and feelings the protagonist has not yet expressed. |
| Why it is used | It lets the protagonist hear their own unspoken thoughts and feelings from outside and respond to them. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping |
| Other names | doubling, played by an auxiliary ego (psychodrama, J. L. Moreno) |
| In the vault | `notes/the facilitated room has several independent roots and improv's distinction is theorizing play as craft.md` |
| Sources | https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2018.01263/full; the vault, archive/reading — the routing surfaces recounted (speaking being, moreno, the scharmer count).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### role reversal

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The protagonist steps into the role of another person in their life, such as a parent, and speaks and acts as that person, while someone else plays the protagonist. |
| Why it is used | It lets the protagonist experience the situation from the other's position and see themselves from outside. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, three people |
| Other names | role reversal (psychodrama, J. L. Moreno) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2018.01263/full |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### sculpting

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | One person arranges other people, or objects, in space and in postures to show how they experience the relationships in a family or group, without using words; then the group looks at the arrangement and speaks from it. |
| Why it is used | It makes relationships visible as closeness, distance, and posture, which can show what words have not said. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, a community or public |
| Other names | family sculpture (David Kantor with Fred and Bunny Duhl, Boston Family Institute, from 1969); family sculpting and family reconstruction (Virginia Satir); choreography (Peggy Papp); Image Theatre, in which one person shapes others into silent statues (Augusto Boal, Theatre of the Oppressed); placing representatives in space (Bert Hellinger's Family Constellations) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://dgsf.org/service/wissensportal/andras-wienands-zur-verwendung-der-systemischen-familienskulptur-..; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kantor; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_Oppressed; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Constellations |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the empty chair

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person speaks to an imagined person placed in an empty chair, often someone absent or dead with whom they have unresolved feelings, and says what was never said. |
| Why it is used | It brings unfinished business with someone absent into the present, where the feelings can be expressed and completed. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, one person alone |
| Other names | the empty chair (Gestalt therapy, Fritz Perls); the auxiliary chair (psychodrama, which Perls adapted); empty-chair work for unfinished business (emotion-focused therapy, Leslie Greenberg) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm; https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2018.01263/full; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/591ba00cd482e99ce66a51f7/t/59cb34767131a5f5d7539923/1506489465300/EFT+Summary+&+Overview+-+L+Greenberg+2015.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### the two-chair dialogue

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person gives two conflicting parts of themselves a chair each, such as an inner critic and the criticized self, and moves between the chairs so that each part speaks directly to the other. |
| Why it is used | It puts the two sides 'into live contact with each other', in Greenberg's words, so each can hear the other; in emotion-focused therapy the aim is a softening of the critical voice. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Other names | the two-chair technique to resolve splits (Gestalt therapy); games of dialogue between 'top dog' and 'under dog' (Gestalt therapy, Levitsky and Perls, 1970); two-chair work for self-critical splits (emotion-focused therapy, Leslie Greenberg) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/591ba00cd482e99ce66a51f7/t/59cb34767131a5f5d7539923/1506489465300/EFT+Summary+&+Overview+-+L+Greenberg+2015.pdf; https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED082090.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### trying on a sentence

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator offers a short sentence and asks the person to say it aloud, or to hear it said, and to notice what happens inside them in response, such as whether it feels true, false, or stirs something. |
| Why it is used | It lets the person test a possible meaning against their own felt response instead of accepting or rejecting it as an idea. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | the probe, 'What happens when I say...' (Hakomi method); feeding a sentence (Gestalt therapy; the phrase 'May I feed you a sentence?' is attributed to Levitsky and Perls but was not checked at their text) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf; https://hakomi.com/a-little-history/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the sentence stem

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator supplies the opening words of a sentence, and the participant completes it in their own words, often several times in a row. |
| Why it is used | It lowers the effort of starting while leaving the content to the person. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people as equals, three people, a small group, one person alone |
| Other names | lead sentences of the Imago Dialogue, such as 'You make sense to me, and what makes sense is...'; sentence stems (Authentic Relating, in the author's own practice record) |
| In the vault | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Sources | https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf; the vault, `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### accepting an offer ('yes, and')

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A participant accepts what another has just proposed as given and then adds something that builds on it, instead of rejecting or correcting it. |
| Why it is used | It keeps a shared scene or idea developing, where blocking the offer would stop it; Johnstone wrote that 'A block is anything that prevents the action from developing'. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, two people as equals, an organization |
| Other names | 'Yes, and' (improvisational theatre, later used in brainstorming and business); accepting offers rather than blocking them (Keith Johnstone, Impro, 1979) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — the facilitated room's roots (lewin 1946, johnstone's impro).md |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yes,_and...; the vault, archive/reading — the facilitated room's roots (lewin 1946, johnstone's impro).md |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

### Parts and protection

#### honoring a protective part

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator, or the person alone, addresses the part of the self that guards against pain with respect, asks about its concerns, and asks its permission before going toward what it protects, instead of trying to push past it. |
| Why it is used | It treats protection as having a reason, so the protective part can relax on its own terms rather than being overpowered. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | one person and their parts, two people, one helping, one person alone |
| Other names | getting permission from the parts that protect, before approaching what they protect (Internal Family Systems, Richard Schwartz); taking over, supporting a defense instead of fighting it (Hakomi method); meeting the gatekeeper with a bow, never a fight (the book's own corpus) |
| In the vault | `notes/the gatekeeper met with a bow is the threshold-guardian honored that rites of passage and internal family systems each named.md` |
| Sources | https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/internal-family-systems-model-outline; https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/evolution-internal-family-systems-model-dr-richard-schwartz-ph-d; https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### stepping back from a part or a thought

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The person notices a feeling, thought, or inner voice and relates to it as something they have, at a small distance, rather than as what they are, for example by saying 'a part of me is afraid' or by imagining setting a concern down beside them. |
| Why it is used | It gives the person a position from which to see the thought or part clearly and to respond to it by choice. |
