| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | one person and their parts, one person alone, two people, one helping, a text or tool, no one present |
| Other names | unblending, asking a part to step back so the Self can be present (Internal Family Systems); cognitive defusion (Acceptance and Commitment Therapy, Steven C. Hayes); disidentification, 'I have a body, but I am not my body' (psychosynthesis, Roberto Assagioli); clearing a space, setting each concern down at a distance (Focusing, Eugene Gendlin) |
| In the vault | `notes/internal family systems.md` |
| Sources | https://ifs-institute.com/resources/articles/internal-family-systems-model-outline; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acceptance_and_commitment_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychosynthesis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focusing_(psychotherapy); the vault, archive/reading — gendlin, focusing and a process model (1978, 1997).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

### Offering meaning

#### externalizing the problem

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The therapist talks about the problem as a separate thing with its own name and asks how it influences the person's life and how the person influences it. |
| Why it is used | It separates the person's identity from the problem, following Michael White's statement 'The person is not the problem, the problem is the problem', so the person can take a position toward it. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, a community or public |
| Other names | externalising conversations (narrative therapy, Michael White and David Epston) |
| In the vault | `ops/reading queue.md` |
| Sources | https://dulwichcentre.com.au/courses/what-is-narrative-practice-a-free-course/lessons/externalising/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### reframing

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The helper offers a different frame in which the same facts make a different sense, for example describing a behavior as protection rather than as stubbornness. |
| Why it is used | It changes what a situation means to the person without changing the facts, which can make possible responses that the old meaning ruled out. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | reframing and relabeling (Mental Research Institute brief therapy; the term was set out by Paul Watzlawick, John Weakland and Richard Fisch in Change, 1974); reframing (structural family therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; http://www.sietmanagement.fr/wp-content/uploads/2016/04/Watzlawick-Change.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### challenge or confrontation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The helper points out directly a discrepancy, a defense, or an avoided fact, for example the gap between what the person says and what they do. |
| Why it is used | It aims to make the person aware of something they are avoiding, so they can decide what to do about it. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | confronting the patient's ego with its defenses (psychoanalysis); the Game, a harsh group confrontation (Synanon, 1958 to 1991) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychoanalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synanon; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivational_interviewing |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### interpretation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The analyst tells the patient a proposed meaning of what the patient has said, dreamed, or done, often linking it to an unconscious conflict, a defense, or feelings the patient has toward the analyst. |
| Why it is used | It aims to make unconscious material conscious, so the patient can understand and change a pattern that repeats. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | interpretation (psychoanalysis and psychodynamic therapy) |
| In the vault | `notes/the book may interpret only the author because interpretation is licensed where its subject can answer back.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychodynamic_psychotherapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychoanalysis; https://iacp.ie/files/UserFiles/IJCP-Articles/2023/A%20simulated%20interview%20with%20Fritz%20Perls%20Part%202%20%E2%80%93%20The%20process%20of%20therapy%20sessions%20James%20C.%20Overholser,%20Ph.D.,%20ABPP.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### amplification

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The analyst or the dreamer first gathers the dreamer's own associations to a dream image, then sets the image beside parallel images from myth, religion, history, and culture, and then returns to the dreamer's own life. |
| Why it is used | It widens the possible meanings of an image beyond the personal, while keeping each association connected to the original image rather than following a chain of associations away from it, a difference the International Association for Analytical Psychology draws with Freud's free association. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, one person alone |
| Other names | amplification (Jungian analytical psychology) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://iaap.org/jung-analytical-psychology/short-articles-on-analytical-psychology/amplification-2/ |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### paradoxical intention or prescribing the symptom

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The therapist asks the person to do deliberately, or even to increase, the very thing they fear or are trying to stop, for example to try to produce the feared symptom on purpose. |
| Why it is used | It interrupts the cycle in which the effort to avoid a symptom keeps it going. |
| Who decides | D, the facilitator directs or interprets with authority |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Other names | paradoxical intention (Viktor Frankl, logotherapy, term coined 1939); prescribing the symptom, and restraining or going slow (Mental Research Institute brief therapy); counterparadox (Milan systemic therapy) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradoxical_intention; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_Selvini_Palazzoli |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

### Speaking from oneself

#### the here-and-now statement

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A person says what they are experiencing, or what is happening between the people present, at this moment, instead of reporting events from elsewhere. In Yalom's account the group then examines what the moment showed. |
| Why it is used | It makes the relationship in the room itself the material to learn from. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | a small group, two people, one helping, two people as equals, three people |
| Other names | working in the here-and-now, followed by illumination of what happened (group psychotherapy, Irvin Yalom); the principle of the now (Gestalt therapy, Levitsky and Perls, 1970); Reveal Your Experience, first-person, present-tense and bodily (Authentic Relating, in the author's own practice record); noticing the relational field (Circling, in the author's own practice record) |
| In the vault | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Sources | https://www.yalom.com/what-is-the-here-and-now-in-therapy/; https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED082090.pdf; the vault, `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### self-disclosure by the facilitator

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | The facilitator shares something of their own feeling, reaction, or experience with the person, briefly and for the person's benefit. |
| Why it is used | It makes the relationship more mutual, and disclosure by the therapist is often thought to encourage more disclosure by the client. |
| Who decides | C, the facilitator offers content as an option |
| Scale | two people, one helping, a small group, two people as equals |
| Other names | therapist self-disclosure (psychotherapy); the therapist's self-expression of observations, preferences, feelings and experience (Gestalt therapy, as Gary Yontef describes presence) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-disclosure; http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### the I-statement

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What happens | A person speaks about their own feeling or reaction in the first person and connects it to a specific event, for example 'When you said that, I felt hurt', instead of describing the other person's character. |
| Why it is used | It reports the speaker's own experience without blame, which makes it easier for the other person to hear. |
| Who decides | B, the facilitator shapes the process; the people supply the content |
| Scale | two people as equals, a small group, an organization |
| Other names | I-message (Thomas Gordon, Parent Effectiveness Training, 1970); observations, feelings, needs and requests (Nonviolent Communication, Marshall Rosenberg); 'I' language in the Imago Dialogue; revealing impact, 'when you said X, I felt Y' (Authentic Relating, in the author's own practice record) |
| In the vault | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-message; https://www.naturalchild.org/articles/marshall_rosenberg/interview.html; https://www.integralpsychology.org/uploads/1/5/3/0/15300482/the_imago_dialogue_101.pdf; the vault, `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

## Part 3. The stances a facilitator can hold

#### accompaniment

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Christian spiritual direction, with early guidelines from John Cassian in the fourth century |
| Date | 4th century onward |
| What it is | The spiritual director accompanies rather than rules: they may advise and help discern, but they do not replace the choices and decisions of the person accompanied, and the Holy Spirit is held to be the true guide. |
| Related names | holding space (Heather Plett); the midwife (Socrates); servant leadership (Robert Greenleaf) |
| In the vault | `notes/ignatius of loyola.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_direction |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### attunement

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Daniel Stern (developmental psychology); the term affect attunement was not confirmed at an opened source |
| Date | 1980s (The Interpersonal World of the Infant, 1985) |
| What it is | Stern studied how a caregiver matches and responds to the quality of an infant's inner state. In helping work, attunement means the helper does the same with the other person's state, so the person senses that it has been felt and shared. |
| Related names | co-regulation (developmental psychology and attachment research); tracking (Hakomi method); empathic understanding (Carl Rogers) |
| In the vault | `notes/grief cannot complete alone.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Stern_(psychologist) |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

#### beginner's mind (shoshin)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Shunryu Suzuki, drawing on the Japanese Zen tradition |
| Date | 1970 (Zen Mind, Beginner's Mind) |
| What it is | The practitioner keeps the openness and lack of preconceptions of a beginner even after long practice. Suzuki wrote: 'In the beginner's mind there are many possibilities, but in the expert's mind there are few.' |
| Related names | not-knowing (collaborative therapy); wonder, 'a lot like curiosity except for you're not looking for the answer' (Joe Hudson's VIEW); negative capability (Keats and Bion) |
| In the vault | `notes/dogen holds that the practice is the realization enacted rather than its means.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoshin; the vault, archive/reading — dogen, bendowa on shusho itto (c. 1231).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

#### brave space

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Brian Arao and Kristi Clemens |
| Date | 2013 |
| What it is | The facilitator frames a discussion of difficult social issues as needing courage rather than promising safety, with agreements such as 'controversy with civility', 'owning intentions and impacts', 'challenge by choice', 'respect', and 'no attacks'. |
| Related names | safe space, the idea it answers; group agreements (circle practice); trauma-informed practice |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_space |

#### co-regulation

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | developmental psychology and attachment research |
| Date | not confirmed |
| What it is | Two people continuously adjust to each other's changing actions and states, as when a caregiver comforts a distressed infant; over time the child takes these patterns in and develops self-regulation. Attachment has been defined as 'the dyadic regulation of emotion'. |
| Related names | attunement (Daniel Stern); containment (Wilfred Bion); pendulation and resourcing (Somatic Experiencing) |
| In the vault | `notes/the field between two people does work.md` |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-regulation |

| Field | Entry |

| Originators | Aaron Beck and colleagues (cognitive therapy) |
| Date | 1970s (Cognitive Therapy of Depression, 1979) |
| What it is | Therapist and patient work as fellow investigators who examine the evidence for and against the patient's beliefs, rather than the therapist telling the patient what is true. |
| Related names | the working alliance (Edward Bordin); co-operative inquiry, research 'with' rather than 'on' people (John Heron and Peter Reason) |

#### congruence

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Rogers |
| Date | 1957 |
| What it is | The helper is genuine in the relationship: what they express matches what they are actually experiencing, without presenting a professional manner that differs from what they feel. |
| Related names | radical genuineness, the sixth level of validation (dialectical behavior therapy, Marsha Linehan); presence as self-expression, in which the therapist shares observations, preferences and feelings (Gestalt therapy, as described by Gary Yontef); vulnerability, 'to speak your truth even when it's scary' (Joe Hudson's VIEW) |
| In the vault | archive/reading — rogers, the necessary and sufficient conditions (1957).md |

#### containment

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Wilfred Bion |
| Date | Bion's work from the 1960s; the exact date was not confirmed at an opened source |
| What it is | In Bion's model one person, the container, receives and processes feelings and experiences that another person, the contained, cannot yet bear. |

#### cultural humility

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Melanie Tervalon and Jann Murray-García |
| Date | 1998 |
| What it is | The practitioner keeps an interpersonal stance that is open to the other person in the aspects of cultural identity most important to them, reflects on their own biases, treats learning as lifelong, and shares power by treating the client as the expert on their own lived experience. It was proposed as an alternative to claiming cultural competence. |
| Related names | not-knowing (collaborative therapy); humble inquiry (Edgar Schein) |
| In the vault | not yet met |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_humility |

| Date | 1987 ('Hypothesizing, Circularity, and Neutrality Revisited: An Invitation to Curiosity') |

#### eldership

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Arnold Mindell (process work) |
| Date | 1990s (Sitting in the Fire, 1995) |

It rests on deep democracy, the view that all viewpoints, including emotional and irrational ones, matter to a group's decisions. |

#### empathic understanding

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Originators | Carl Rogers |
| Date | 1957 |
| What it is | The helper senses the person's inner frame of reference and tries to communicate that understanding so the person can perceive it. Rogers later preferred to call the helper's responses 'testing understandings', because the aim is to check the understanding with the person. |
| Related names | inclusion, experiencing the other's side while keeping one's own presence (Martin Buber, taken into Gestalt therapy); empathy, 'to be with a person in their emotions' (Joe Hudson's VIEW); empathy, the third step of the Imago Dialogue |

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|

| Date | 1912 ('Recommendations to Physicians Practising Psycho-Analysis') |

| Originators | Richard Schwartz (Internal Family Systems) |
| Date | 1980s |

Practitioners summarize the qualities of Self as eight words beginning with C, including curiosity, compassion, and calm. |

| Field | Entry |

## Part 4. The mental models

#### Psychodynamic and psychoanalytic

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to be shaped by unconscious wishes, fears, and conflicts formed in early relationships, which show up as defenses and as feelings transferred onto present relationships. It locates change in bringing that material into awareness, largely through the relationship with the analyst and through interpretation. |
| Its people | Sigmund Freud, Carl Jung, Melanie Klein, Donald Winnicott, Wilfred Bion |
| Typical moves | interpretation, amplification, silence and waiting, challenge or confrontation |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychoanalysis; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychodynamic_psychotherapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Winnicott; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilfred_Bion |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *transference* | the patient's projection onto the analyst of feelings about figures from early life |
| *countertransference* | the analyst's own reactions to the patient, which can be a source of information when recognized |
| *free association* | saying whatever comes to mind without censoring it |
| *resistance* | the ways a person avoids painful material during treatment |
| *holding environment* | Winnicott's term for reliable care that keeps impingements to a minimum |

#### Humanistic and person-centered

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to have an inborn tendency toward growth and fulfillment that develops when conditions allow it. It locates change in a relationship in which the person feels accepted, understood, and met by a genuine other, so the helper supplies conditions rather than direction. |
| Its people | Carl Rogers, Abraham Maslow, William R. Miller and Stephen Rollnick (motivational interviewing, described as a person-centered approach) |
| Typical moves | reflecting back, summarizing, the open question, validation |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person-centered_therapy; https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *unconditional positive regard* | accepting the person without conditions on what they must feel or do |
| *congruence* | the helper's genuineness, with what is expressed matching what is felt |
| *empathic understanding* | sensing the person's inner frame of reference and communicating that understanding |
| *actualizing tendency* | an inbuilt tendency toward growth and fulfillment |
| *client-centered* | the view that the answers lie within the client rather than the therapist |

#### Existential and phenomenological

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to be a free and responsible being who must face given conditions of existence, such as death, freedom, isolation, and the absence of ready-made meaning. It locates change in facing those conditions honestly in an authentic relationship and choosing how to live with them, and it treats distress about them as human rather than as illness. |
| Its people | Ludwig Binswanger, Medard Boss, Viktor Frankl, Rollo May, Irvin Yalom, R. D. Laing, Emmy van Deurzen, Edmund Husserl (phenomenological method) |
| Typical moves | the here-and-now statement, paradoxical intention or prescribing the symptom, suspending assumptions |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existential_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoch%C3%A9; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradoxical_intention; https://www.yalom.com/what-is-the-here-and-now-in-therapy/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *ultimate concerns* | Yalom's four givens of existence: death, freedom and responsibility, isolation, and meaninglessness |
| *epoché or bracketing* | setting aside assumptions in order to describe experience as it appears |
| *paradoxical intention* | Frankl's technique of deliberately rehearsing the feared pattern |
| *here-and-now* | working with what is happening between the people present, now |

#### Gestalt therapy

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to be an organism in constant contact with an environment, whose difficulties arise where contact is interrupted or where past experience remains unfinished. It locates change in full awareness of what one is doing now, and by Arnold Beisser's paradoxical theory of change a person changes by becoming more fully what they already are rather than by trying to be different. |
| Its people | Fritz Perls, Laura Perls, Paul Goodman, Arnold Beisser, Gary Yontef |
| Typical moves | the empty chair, the two-chair dialogue, staying with what arises, the here-and-now statement, the little experiment in mindfulness |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group, one person and their parts |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestalt_therapy; http://gestalt.org/yontef.htm; https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED082090.pdf |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *awareness* | being in touch with one's own existence and with what is |
| *contact boundary* | the point at which a person meets and is affected by others and the environment |
| *unfinished business* | unexpressed feelings from past relationships that keep pressing on the present |
| *experiment* | a task the therapist proposes to expand direct experience |
| *paradoxical theory of change* | change happens when a person becomes what they are, not when they force themselves to be otherwise |

#### Cognitive and behavioral

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's feelings and actions to depend heavily on how they interpret events and on learned patterns of thought and behavior. It locates change in identifying those thoughts and patterns, testing them against evidence, and practicing new ones in a structured collaboration. |
| Its people | Aaron Beck, Judith Beck, Marsha Linehan, Steven C. Hayes |
| Typical moves | the Socratic question, validation, stepping back from a part or a thought, labelling a feeling |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group, a text or tool, no one present, one person alone |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acceptance_and_commitment_therapy; https://psychcentral.com/blog/emotionally-sensitive/2012/02/understanding-the-levels-of-validation |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *automatic thoughts* | quick, habitual thoughts that often carry negative views of the self, the world, or the future |
| *collaborative empiricism* | therapist and patient investigating the evidence for a belief together |
| *guided discovery* | the therapist designing questions and experiences through which the patient learns |
| *cognitive defusion* | in Acceptance and Commitment Therapy, reducing the tendency to treat thoughts as literal facts |
| *validation* | in dialectical behavior therapy, communicating that a response makes sense |

#### Somatic and body-centered

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's history, including trauma and defense, to be present in the body as posture, muscular tension, breath, and patterns of nervous-system arousal. It locates change in working directly with bodily sensation and movement, in small and tolerable steps, so that defensive responses that were cut short can complete and the nervous system can settle. |
| Its people | Wilhelm Reich, Alexander Lowen, Peter Levine, Ron Kurtz |
| Typical moves | pendulation, titration, resourcing, grounding, tracking, the contact statement |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, one person alone, a small group |
| Sources | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4316402/; https://traumahealing.org/se-101/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Lowen; https://hakomiinstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Final-Hakomi-Chapter-with-references-Oct-4-2005.pdf; the vault, `notes/wilhelm reich.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *character armor* | Reich's term for protection organized into posture and breathing that outlasts the threat it answered |
| *grounding* | Lowen's term for feeling one's contact with the ground through the feet and legs |
| *titration* | approaching difficult material in very small amounts |
| *pendulation* | moving attention back and forth between activation and settling |
| *discharge* | the release of survival energy, for example through shaking and trembling |

#### Experiential and Focusing-oriented

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to have an implicit bodily sense of their situation that is richer than what they can yet say. It locates change in attending to that felt sense until a fitting word or image comes, which brings a felt shift, a bodily release that Gendlin describes as the situation 'carrying forward'. |
| Its people | Eugene Gendlin, Ann Weiser Cornell, Barbara McGavin, Leslie Greenberg (emotion-focused therapy) |
| Typical moves | checking a word against the felt sense, stepping back from a part or a thought, staying with what arises, the two-chair dialogue |
| Typical scales | one person alone, two people as equals, two people, one helping |
| Sources | https://www.focusing.org.uk/an-introduction-to-focusing; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Relationship_Focusing; https://static1.squarespace.com/static/591ba00cd482e99ce66a51f7/t/59cb34767131a5f5d7539923/1506489465300/EFT+Summary+&+Overview+-+L+Greenberg+2015.pdf; the vault, archive/reading — gendlin, focusing and a process model (1978, 1997).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *felt sense* | a vague, still unformed bodily sense of a situation or issue |
| *handle* | a word, phrase, or image that fits the quality of the felt sense |
| *resonating* | checking the handle against the felt sense to see whether it fits |
| *felt shift* | an opening or release in the body, perhaps with a sigh, when something fits |
| *Focusing partnership* | two people who take turns focusing and listening |

#### Systemic and family

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's difficulty to be part of a pattern of relationships in a family or other system, a pattern that keeps itself going through feedback. It locates change in altering the pattern, through questions that reveal it, new boundaries, or interventions that interrupt it, rather than in changing one individual alone. |
| Its people | Gregory Bateson, Paul Watzlawick, John Weakland, Richard Fisch, Salvador Minuchin, Virginia Satir, Mara Selvini Palazzoli, Luigi Boscolo, Gianfranco Cecchin, Giuliana Prata, Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy, David Kantor |
| Typical moves | the circular question, reframing, paradoxical intention or prescribing the symptom, sculpting |
| Typical scales | a small group, two people, one helping, an organization |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_therapy; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_Selvini_Palazzoli; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iv%C3%A1n_B%C3%B6sz%C3%B6rm%C3%A9nyi-Nagy; https://dgsf.org/service/wissensportal/andras-wienands-zur-verwendung-der-systemischen-familienskulptur-..; the vault, `notes/gregory bateson.md`; the vault, `notes/salvador minuchin.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *circular questioning* | asking family members about relationships and differences among the others |
| *neutrality* | the Milan team's guideline that the therapist not side with any member or explanation |
| *joining* | structural family therapy's term for the therapist's entry into the family's way of relating |
| *multidirected partiality* | siding with each family member in turn to evoke responsible mutual position-taking |
| *prescribing the symptom* | asking the family or person to continue the problem behavior on purpose |

#### Narrative and social constructionist

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to live by the stories told about them and by them, and some of those stories become problem-saturated and limit what the person notices about their own life. It locates change in separating the person from the problem and co-authoring a richer account that includes the moments the problem story left out. |
| Its people | Michael White, David Epston, Harlene Anderson, Harry Goolishian, David Cooperrider (Appreciative Inquiry, which takes a social constructionist approach) |
| Typical moves | externalizing the problem, the exception question, the witness role |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group, a community or public, an organization |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_therapy; https://dulwichcentre.com.au/courses/what-is-narrative-practice-a-free-course/lessons/externalising/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlene_Anderson; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *externalizing conversations* | talking about the problem as separate from the person |
| *problem-saturated story* | an account of a life dominated by the problem |
| *unique outcomes* | moments that contradict the problem story |
| *re-authoring* | building a new account of oneself around one's values |
| *outsider witnesses* | listeners who respond to a person's preferred story with what resonated for them |

#### Solution-focused

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to already have resources and moments when the problem is absent or smaller, and it treats a detailed picture of the preferred future as more useful than an analysis of the problem's causes. It locates change in finding and building on what already works, in small steps the person names. |
| Its people | Steve de Shazer, Insoo Kim Berg |
| Typical moves | the miracle question, the scaling question, the exception question, resourcing |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group, an organization |
| Sources | https://solutionfocused.net/what-is-solution-focused-therapy/ |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *miracle question* | imagining the problem solved overnight and describing what one would notice |
| *scaling question* | placing the situation on a scale from 0 to 10 and asking what one step higher would be |
| *exceptions* | times when the problem is less severe or absent |
| *coping questions* | questions about how the person has managed to carry on |
| *compliments* | direct and indirect acknowledgment of what the person is doing well |

#### Dialogic

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to come into being in relation and in speech with others, so meaning is made between people rather than inside one of them. It locates change in conversation in which different voices stay distinct without merging and assumptions are noticed, so that something new can emerge between the participants. |
| Its people | Martin Buber, Mikhail Bakhtin, David Bohm, Paulo Freire, Harlene Anderson |
| Typical moves | suspending assumptions, reflecting back, the round or go-around, the here-and-now statement |
| Typical scales | two people as equals, a small group, a large group, a community or public |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bakhtin; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohm_Dialogue; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedagogy_of_the_Oppressed; the vault, archive/reading — buber, i and thou and elements of the interhuman (1923, 1965).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *I–Thou* | Buber's term for meeting another as a whole person rather than as an object |
| *polyphony* | Bakhtin's term for 'a plurality of independent and unmerged voices and consciousnesses' |
| *unfinalizability* | Bakhtin's view that a person can never be fully and finally defined from outside |
| *suspension* | Bohm's practice of holding assumptions in view without acting on or suppressing them |
| *problem-posing education* | Freire's alternative to 'banking' education, in which learners create knowledge through dialogue |

#### Contemplative, nondual

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's deepest nature to be an awareness that is already present and not separate from what it knows, though it is habitually overlooked. It locates change in recognizing what was always the case, reached through practices that let a person notice, and stop relying on, the habit of treating thoughts and a separate self as final, rather than in adding something new. |
| Its people | Utpaladeva and Abhinavagupta (Kashmir Śaiva Pratyabhijñā), Śaṅkara (Advaita Vedānta), Longchenpa (Dzogchen), Dōgen, Shunryu Suzuki |
| Typical moves | noting, staying with what arises, stepping back from a part or a thought, silence and waiting |
| Typical scales | one person alone, a small group, two people, one helping |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoshin; the vault, archive/reading — the kashmir śaiva loci (spanda, śiva sūtra, pratyabhijñāhṛdaya, ipk).md; the vault, `archive/reading — brihadaranyaka upanishad 2.3.6 and shankara on neti neti.md`; the vault, archive/reading — dogen, bendowa on shusho itto (c. 1231).md; the vault, archive/reading — the seamless mirror (henry, longchenpa, śaṅkara on self-knowing).md |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *pratyabhijñā* | recognition, in the Kashmir Śaiva school, of what never left |
| *neti neti* | 'not this, not this', the Upaniṣadic and Advaita method of negation |
| *shushō-ittō* | Dōgen's teaching that practice and realization are one |
| *shoshin* | beginner's mind, keeping a beginner's openness in long practice |

#### Contemplative, theistic

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to be made for relationship with God, whose presence and guidance can be discerned in prayer, in community, and in the inner movements of feeling that the tradition attends to. It locates change in consenting to and discerning that presence, often with a companion or a community, rather than in the person's own effort alone. |
| Its people | George Fox (the Quakers), Ignatius of Loyola, John Cassian, Thomas Keating, Basil Pennington, William Meninger, Henri Nouwen |
| Typical moves | silence and waiting, staying with what arises, the open question |
| Typical scales | a small group, one person alone, two people, one helping, a society's customs |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clearness_committee; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meeting_for_worship; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centering_prayer; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_direction; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Nouwen; the vault, archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *clearness committee* | a Quaker group that helps a person reach clarity by questioning and reflection rather than advice |
| *Inner Light* | the Quaker conviction that the light of God is present in all people |
| *sacred word* | in centering prayer, a word used as a symbol of consent to God's presence and action |
| *spiritual direction* | accompanying a person's relationship with God without replacing their own choices |
| *discernment of spirits* | Ignatius's rules for telling apart inner movements of consolation and desolation |

#### Developmental

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's way of making meaning to grow through a sequence of stages or orders, each able to handle more complexity than the one before. It locates change in the move from being embedded in a way of seeing to being able to look at it, a move that needs both challenge and support from the surrounding environment. |
| Its people | Erik Erikson, James Marcia, Robert Kegan, Ken Wilber, Mary Belenky and colleagues |
| Typical moves | challenge or confrontation, reflecting back, the Socratic question |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, an organization, one person alone |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Kegan; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpersonal_psychology; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_Ways_of_Knowing; the vault, `notes/erik erikson.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *meaning-making* | Kegan's term for the activity of understanding experience, which he treats as the activity of being a person |
| *self-authoring mind* | an order of consciousness that is internally directed rather than defined by others' expectations |
| *culture of embeddedness* | Kegan's term for the environment that holds, challenges, and releases a person at each stage |
| *immunity to change* | the title of Kegan's 2009 approach to what keeps a person from making changes they want |
| *identity crisis* | Erikson's term for the turning point in which a person works out who they are |

#### Critical and liberatory

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's suffering and silence to be shaped by social structures of power and oppression, and it holds that education or therapy that ignores this can reproduce it. It locates change in critical consciousness, in which people name their situation together through dialogue and act to change it. |
| Its people | Paulo Freire, Augusto Boal, bell hooks |
| Typical moves | sculpting, step up, step back, group agreements, the round or go-around |
| Typical scales | a small group, a community or public, a large group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedagogy_of_the_Oppressed; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_Oppressed |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *conscientização* | critical consciousness, recognizing and challenging systems of domination |
| *banking model* | Freire's name for education that deposits knowledge in passive learners |
| *problem-posing education* | education in which learners create knowledge with the teacher through dialogue |
| *spect-actor* | Boal's term for an audience member who steps in to act |
| *Forum Theatre* | a performance the audience stops so that audience members can replace the protagonist and try other actions |

#### Participatory and emergent

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a group to hold more knowledge and more capacity to organize itself than any single leader, and what it needs emerges when the conditions for conversation are right. It locates change in inviting participants to set the agenda and supply the content themselves, while hosts look after the process and the harvest rather than the answers. |
| Its people | Harrison Owen, Juanita Brown, David Isaacs, Henri Lipmanowicz, Keith McCandless, John Heron, Peter Reason, David Cooperrider |
| Typical moves | harvesting, the pair-share, the fishbowl, the check-in, the round or go-around |
| Typical scales | a large group, an organization, a community or public, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Space_Technology; https://artofhosting.org/what-is-aoh/; https://www.liberatingstructures.com/1-2-4-all; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-operative_inquiry; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appreciative_inquiry |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Open Space* | an event format in which participants create the agenda and schedule on arrival |
| *fully present and invisible* | Owen's description of the ideal Open Space facilitator |
| *harvest* | the planned gathering of what a group has discovered |
| *co-operative inquiry* | research carried out 'with' rather than 'on' people, as co-researchers |
| *1-2-4-All* | a structure of thinking alone, then in pairs, then in fours, then all together |

#### Ritual and anthropological

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a society to support its members through customs and rites that mark transitions in status and give each change a shared form. It locates change in a structured sequence: separation from the old status, a liminal period between, and incorporation into a new status that the community recognizes. |
| Its people | Arnold van Gennep, Victor Turner |
| Typical moves | the check-out or closing circle, the talking piece, the appreciation round or blessing, silence and waiting |
| Typical scales | a society's customs, a community or public, a large group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_of_passage; the vault, `notes/victor turner.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *rites of separation* | acts that mark leaving a former status behind |
| *liminality* | the threshold period in which a person is between statuses |
| *incorporation* | the rejoining of society in a new, recognized status |
| *communitas* | Turner's term for the bond among equals in the liminal phase |

#### Transpersonal

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's identity to extend beyond the individual ego and treats spiritual, mystical, and self-transcending experiences as part of human development. It locates change partly in such experiences and in integrating them into ordinary life, using practices drawn from Eastern and Western traditions. |
| Its people | Abraham Maslow, Stanislav Grof, Anthony Sutich, Roberto Assagioli, Ken Wilber, Michael Washburn |
| Typical moves | stepping back from a part or a thought, staying with what arises |
| Typical scales | one person alone, two people, one helping, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpersonal_psychology; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychosynthesis |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *transpersonal* | beyond the personal; experiences in which identity extends past the individual self |
| *disidentification* | Assagioli's exercise of recognizing 'I have a body, but I am not my body' |
| *subpersonalities* | Assagioli's term for the different roles and selves a person acts from in different settings |
| *self-transcendence* | going beyond the concerns of the individual self |

#### Learning theory and reflective practice

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person to learn from experience only when the experience is reflected on and the reflection is tested in further action. It locates change in a cycle of experience, reflection, conceptualizing, and experimenting, often in groups or organizations that study their own practice. |
| Its people | John Dewey, David Kolb, Kurt Lewin, Chris Argyris, Donald Schön, Frank Lyman, Mary Budd Rowe |
| Typical moves | the pair-share, the Socratic question, the check-out or closing circle, silence and waiting |
| Typical scales | a small group, an organization, a large group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Argyris; https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED061103; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Think-pair-share; the vault, `notes/the experiential learning cycle.md`; the vault, `notes/donald schon.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *experiential learning cycle* | the sequence by which experience becomes learning through reflection and a further act |
| *double-loop learning* | Argyris's term for learning that questions the governing assumptions behind an action |
| *espoused theory and theory-in-use* | the difference between what people say guides them and what actually guides their actions |
| *reflection-in-action* | Schön's term for thinking about practice while doing it |
| *wait time* | Rowe's term for the pause between a teacher's question and the student's answer |

#### Improvisation, play, and the arts

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person's spontaneity and imagination to be resources that approval, fear, and habit tend to block. It locates change in play, enactment, and art-making, where people take on roles and respond in the moment, so that new responses are rehearsed in action rather than only discussed. |
| Its people | J. L. Moreno, Viola Spolin, Keith Johnstone, Augusto Boal, Mary Starks Whitehouse, Janet Adler |
| Typical moves | accepting an offer ('yes, and'), doubling, role reversal, sculpting, the witness role |
| Typical scales | a small group, a large group, two people as equals |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_Oppressed; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authentic_Movement; https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2018.01263/full; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yes,_and...; the vault, archive/reading — the facilitated room's roots (lewin 1946, johnstone's impro).md; the vault, archive/reading — the routing surfaces recounted (speaking being, moreno, the scharmer count).md; the vault, `notes/viola spolin.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *spontaneity training and warm-up* | Moreno's named methods for training a person toward spontaneous action |
| *offer and block* | Johnstone's terms for proposing something in a scene and for preventing it from developing |
| *point of concentration* | Spolin's shared focal task that keeps attention off the self |
| *spect-actor* | Boal's term for an audience member who steps in to act |
| *mover and witness* | the two roles of Authentic Movement |

#### Indigenous and relational

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | Many Indigenous traditions, and the southern African ethic of ubuntu, take a person to be constituted through relationships with kin, community, ancestors, and the living world rather than to be a self-contained individual. They locate healing and change within those relationships, through shared forms such as circles, ceremony, and reciprocity, with knowledge kept and passed on by elders and communities. |
| Its people | Albert D. Marshall and Murdena Marshall (Mi'kmaq Elders), Robin Wall Kimmerer, Kay Pranis (a non-Indigenous practitioner who credits Native American circle traditions) |
| Typical moves | the talking piece, the round or go-around, group agreements, the check-out or closing circle |
| Typical scales | a community or public, a society's customs, the more-than-human, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-Eyed_Seeing; https://www.beyondintractability.org/bksum/pranis-little; the vault, `archive/reading — abram and kimmerer on the more-than-human (1996 and 2013).md`; the vault, `notes/ubuntu personhood.md` |
| Verified at those sources | in part |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *Two-Eyed Seeing (Etuaptmumk)* | seeing with the strengths of Indigenous ways of knowing through one eye and of Western ways through the other, using both together |
| *talking piece* | an object passed around a circle that gives its holder the turn to speak |
| *keeper* | the circle keeper, who maintains the shared space without directing the group |
| *ubuntu* | the view that a person becomes a person through relation with others |
| *reciprocity* | a relationship of mutual exchange between people and a living world whose beings are addressed as subjects rather than objects |

#### Process-oriented (process work)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes a person or group to have experiences it identifies with and experiences it finds hard to identify with, and it treats disturbances as signals of what is trying to emerge. It locates change in following those signals through channels such as body feeling, movement, relationship, and world events, and in giving every viewpoint in a group a voice. |
| Its people | Arnold Mindell |
| Typical moves | tracking, staying with what arises, the here-and-now statement |
| Typical scales | two people, one helping, a small group, a large group, a community or public |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Process_Oriented_Psychology; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Mindell |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *primary and secondary process* | the experiences a person identifies with, and those they find hard to identify with |
| *channels* | the modes in which experience appears: visual, auditory, movement, body feeling, relationship, and world |
| *deep democracy* | the stance that all viewpoints, including emotional and irrational ones, matter to a group's decisions |
| *rank* | social, psychological, and spiritual power differences among people |
| *worldwork* | process work applied to conflict, leadership, and social issues in groups |

#### Organizational learning and management

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes an organization to be a system whose people, structures, and shared habits of thinking determine what it can do. It locates change in learning at the level of the whole: examining shared assumptions, redistributing decisions, and improving how teams think and talk together. |
| Its people | Kurt Lewin, Edgar Schein, Chris Argyris, Peter Senge, Robert Greenleaf, Dennis Bakke, Frederic Laloux, Gerard Endenburg |
| Typical moves | the advice process, the consent round, the check-in, harvesting |
| Typical scales | an organization, a small group, a large group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Argyris; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fifth_Discipline; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Schein; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Servant_leadership; https://reinventingorganizationswiki.com/en/theory/decision-making/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociocracy; the vault, `notes/kurt lewin.md` |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *double-loop learning* | learning that questions the assumptions behind a group's actions |
| *learning organization* | Senge's term for an organization built on systems thinking, personal mastery, mental models, shared vision, and team learning |
| *process consultation* | Schein's model of consulting as a helping relationship |
| *servant leadership* | Greenleaf's model in which the leader's first aim is to serve |
| *advice process* | a rule that anyone may decide after seeking advice from those affected and those with expertise |

#### Civic and deliberative

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| What it takes a person to be, and where it locates change | This model takes citizens to be capable of reaching better and more legitimate public decisions when they deliberate together with good information, balanced views, and equal consideration of arguments, rather than only voting or deferring to experts. It locates change in structured public conversations and representative citizen bodies whose conclusions inform decisions. |
| Its people | Jürgen Habermas, Joshua Cohen, James Fishkin, Joseph M. Bessette |
| Typical moves | group agreements, the round or go-around, the fishbowl, harvesting |
| Typical scales | a community or public, a large group, a small group |
| Sources | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deliberative_democracy |
| Verified at those sources | yes |

| Its own word | What it means |
|---|---|
| *deliberative democracy* | democracy in which deliberation is central to decision-making |
| *deliberative polling* | Fishkin's method of bringing a representative sample of citizens together for informed discussion |
| *citizens' assembly* | a body of ordinary citizens convened to deliberate on a public issue |
| *mini-public* | a small, representative group of citizens who deliberate on behalf of the wider public |
| *public sphere* | Habermas's term for the space in which citizens discuss matters of common concern |

## Part 5. The vault's holdings, by first scale

Each row is one thing the vault holds, placed on the survey's coordinates by one researcher. The last column is that researcher's summary of the vault file, with the basis the researcher recorded for it.

### One person alone

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A practice round ends when the body has refused a formulation (composted) | practice note | `notes/a practice round is finished when the body has refused at least one formulation.md` | E | experiential and Focusing-oriented | held at second hand | A vault proposal held that a practice round ends once the body has refused at least one wording, but a reading of Gendlin's Focusing found that a round ends at the shift, so the note is kept only as a record. (Basis: vault's own words (a forest-panel proposal of 2026-08-08).) |
| Abhinavagupta — the four means and the no-means (anupāya) | reading record | `archive/reading — abhinavagupta on the anupaya (the no-means).md` | E | contemplative, nondual | held at second hand | Abhinavagupta's four means to recognition are graded by the intensity of grace, and the highest, the no-means (anupāya), is entry 'at once, without doing anything', reserved for the most graced. (Basis: lineage teacher's rendering (Lakshmanjoo Academy), not the Sanskrit.) |
| Abraham Maslow — peak and plateau experience | thinker | `notes/abraham maslow.md` | E | humanistic and person-centered | held at second hand | Maslow named the peak experience and later argued that the sustained plateau built by long living matters more than the brief visit, which matches the book's own statement that the peak is only an opening and the work is what follows it. (Basis: vault summary from secondary knowledge; the book's side quoted from abridged ch12.) |
| Advaita's neti neti (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | The survey sorts 'not this, not this' as subtractive privation: the real is reached by stripping away every specification. (Basis: survey's words over the Upaniṣad's.) |
| al-Junayd and al-Qushayrī — annihilation completed by return | reading record | `archive/reading — al-junayd and al-qushayri on fana and baqa.md` | E | contemplative, theistic | held at second hand | Al-Junayd's school of sobriety completes the annihilation of the self in God (fanā) by a return to consciousness and ordinary distinction (baqā), so that the return, not staying dissolved, finishes the path. (Basis: scholars' words (Musharraf and others) quoting al-Junayd through al-Qushayrī.) |
| Alexander's structure-preserving transformation (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | B | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | The survey sorts Alexander's preserve-and-strengthen as compositional and as the only procedure in the vault for how a fold opens: find what is already alive and intensify it. (Basis: survey's words over the vault's secondary account.) |
| Aristotle — activity complete in itself (energeia) | reading record | archive/reading — aristotle, metaphysics IX.6 on energeia and kinesis.md | E | other | read at the primary source | Aristotle distinguishes an activity complete at every moment, such as seeing or living well, from a movement incomplete until its end, such as learning or being cured, which the vault uses for the clause that a practice is not a means to something outside it. (Basis: thinker's words (Ross translation).) |
| Aristotle — privation as a principle of change | thinker | `notes/aristotle on privation.md` | E | other | read at the primary source | Aristotle made privation — the specific absence of a form in a thing able to have it — one of the three principles of change, which the vault holds as the ancestor of the book's diagnostic move of naming what is missing. (Basis: vault summary on the page; the reading record quotes the Physics and Metaphysics in translation.) |
| Aristotle — privation in Physics I and Metaphysics V | reading record | archive/reading — aristotle, physics I and metaphysics V on privation.md | E | other | read at the primary source | Aristotle's Physics I and Metaphysics V.22 make privation a principle of change and define it as the lack of something a thing naturally has, but neither takes the therapeutic step of prescribing its restoration. (Basis: thinker's words (Hardie and Gaye; Ross translations).) |
| Bateson's learning levels (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | systemic and family | read at the primary source | The survey lists Bateson's learning levels, each a change in the process of the level below, among the vault's levels models. (Basis: survey's words over Bateson's.) |
| Bhartṛhari's levels of speech and Kṣemarāja on contraction | reading record | archive/reading — bhartṛhari's levels of speech and kṣemarāja on saṅkoca (vākyapadīya i; pratyabhijñāhṛdayam).md | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | Bhartṛhari names three levels of speech and ranks the soundless above the spoken, the Trika adds a fourth level (parā) that it holds is present whole in every uttered word, so that nothing of parā is lost when speech becomes sound, and Kṣemarāja holds that contraction is freely assumed and is itself consciousness. (Basis: thinkers' words (Sanskrit with Iyer, Pillai, Singh, and Padoux).) |
| Building containers where people, ideas, and a self become more fully themselves | the author's practice | `notes/an externalized system can be built to hold a self rather than only its information.md` | B | participatory and emergent | from the author's record | The author describes all his work as one practice: 'I'm a poet, a systems thinker, and an Authentic Relating facilitator, and the thing running underneath all of it is the same: I build containers where things — people, ideas, a self — become more fully themselves.' (Basis: author's words ([D], 19 June 2026).) |
| Byron Katie's The Work — four questions and a turnaround | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | C | cognitive and behavioral | held at second hand | The corpus records Byron Katie's axiom that 'suffering is arguing with what is' and her method of four questions and a turnaround applied to a believed thought, which the survey sorts as seeing through a false content. (Basis: corpus's words (arc-gloss ch7; walked IX).) |
| Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad and Śaṅkara — neti neti | reading record | `archive/reading — brihadaranyaka upanishad 2.3.6 and shankara on neti neti.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | The Upaniṣad describes Brahman only as 'not this, not this', and Śaṅkara reads the negation as removing the obstructions of ignorance rather than producing the real. (Basis: text's words (Madhavananda translation).) |
| Channeling — the affective practice between repression and venting | practice note | `notes/the affective practice completes a feeling's descent with consent between repression and venting.md` | E | experiential and Focusing-oriented | from the book's drafts | The book's emotional practice has a feeling felt fully, allowed to deliver its message, and expressed at the time and in the direction the whole person chooses, as a third way between repression, which the corpus describes as a feeling 'walled off', and venting, which it describes as a feeling 'detonated' on someone. (Basis: corpus's words (atlas §VII, walked VII), checked against McLaren's own text.) |
| Christopher Alexander — structure-preserving transformation | thinker | `notes/christopher alexander.md` | B | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | Alexander's structure-preserving transformation grows a whole through a sequence of small changes that each keep and strengthen the living structure already present, and the vault reads it as the book's own account of change and of how the corpus was written. (Basis: vault summary from secondary knowledge (The Nature of Order unread).) |
| Cutting the String | the book's form | `abridged` p. 42 | E | other | from the book's drafts | The reader ends a tie to the past that has come to restrict them, such as a grudge, a resentment, or an identification with an old wound, while keeping the relationship itself and what was formative in it. (Basis: draft's words (abridged ch14, form 10).) |
| Daniel Ingram — maps of the insight stages | thinker | `notes/daniel ingram.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | held at second hand | Ingram states the Theravada stages of insight plainly enough that a lay practitioner can locate where they are, including the difficult stages that follow the Arising and Passing Away event, and treats being in those stages as expected rather than as failure. | Delegation between consumption and participation | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | other | held at second hand | The vault holds that delegating a task, including to a machine, can deepen a person's participation or destroy it, depending on whether it frees attention for what should remain one's own. | Donald Schön — reflection-in-action | thinker | `notes/donald schon.md` | E | learning theory | read at the primary source | Schön describes skilled practitioners knowing in the doing and reframing a problem while they work, in a reflective conversation with a situation that talks back, rather than applying rules given in advance. (Basis: thinker's words (five primary texts quoted).) |

| The crisis triage — body, holding, contact | practice note | `notes/the crisis triage collapses the diagnostic to body holding and contact.md` | E | somatic | from the book's drafts | In acute distress the book sets aside its detailed diagnosis and asks three questions — am I in my body, am I held, am I in I–Thou contact with someone — answered with the moves Anchor, Sanctuary, and Calling the Room, done fewer and more often. | The Dead Stop | the book's form | `abridged` p. 41 | E | somatic | from the book's drafts | The reader stops all forward motion, whether typing, walking, or talking, for three to ten seconds, so that the reader registers what is happening before continuing and has a pause in which to choose what to do next. | The experiential learning cycle (Dewey, Kolb) | thinker | `notes/the experiential learning cycle.md` | B | learning theory | held at second hand | Dewey held that experience teaches only when it is reflected on and connected to what follows, and Kolb formalized a four-station cycle — concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualization, active experimentation — in which skipping a station stalls learning. | Utpaladeva — recognition of what is already present | thinker | `notes/utpaladeva.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | Utpaladeva's Īśvarapratyabhijñākārikā holds that liberation adds nothing: one recognizes the Lord already present as one's own self, as a maiden recognizes the beloved who has been standing before her, and consciousness is by nature reflexive awareness. | Wilber's lattice, lines, stages, and pre/trans (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | C | transpersonal | read at the primary source | The survey sorts Wilber's lattice of states against structures, his lines and stages, and his holarchy as the levels family the vault does not carry as a structure of experience, and his pre/trans fallacy as a guard the book lacks. | William James — fruits, not roots | thinker | `notes/william james.md` | E | other | read at the primary source | James holds reports of transformation as observations, judges an experience by its fruits in the life that follows rather than by its roots or its feeling, and notes that mystical states fade but recur and can be built on. | William James — The Principles of Psychology (1890) | reading record | archive/reading — james, the principles of psychology (1890).md | E | other | read at the primary source | James distinguishes knowledge by acquaintance, which cannot be conveyed in words, from knowledge about a thing, and names the felt fringe of vague tendencies around every thought, including a feeling of where a thought is going before it arrives. (Basis: thinker's words (Winnicott 1971, ch. 1).) |

### One person and their parts

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bowing to the Gatekeeper | the book's form | `abridged` p. 42 | E | systemic and family | from the book's drafts | When a protective part such as anger, anxiety, contempt, or performance is active, the reader meets it, honors it, asks what it is protecting, and does not try to override it; the draft says a part met with respect will usually relax. (Basis: draft's words (abridged ch14, form 4).) |
| IFS and the gatekeeper met with a bow (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | systemic and family | from the book's drafts | The survey sorts Internal Family Systems and the book's gatekeeper as its one parts model, in which the facilitator addresses the protective part and never the underlying ground directly, since the ground 'emerges spontaneously when parts open space for it'. (Basis: survey's words over the draft's and Schwartz's.) |
| Internal Family Systems (Richard Schwartz) | thinker | `notes/internal family systems.md` | B | systemic and family | read at the primary source | Internal Family Systems treats the psyche as naturally multiple, meets protective parts with respect and asks what they are protecting, and holds that a calm, curious Self emerges when the parts make room rather than being built. (Basis: mixed: Schwartz quoted from No Bad Parts on the page; the executed draft's borrowing quoted.) |

### A text or tool with no one present

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| David Peter Lawrence — the treatise as spiritual exercise | thinker | `notes/david peter lawrence.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | held at second hand | Lawrence reads the Pratyabhijñā treatises as philosophical argument and spiritual exercise at once, so that working through the argument is itself a way into the recognition it argues for. (Basis: vault summary of a web-verified survey (his book unread).) |
| Douglas Hofstadter — the book that performs its insight | thinker | `notes/douglas hofstadter.md` | E | other | held at second hand | Hofstadter's Gödel, Escher, Bach performs its insight through dialogues, canons, and self-reference and never hands the reader a system to apply, which the vault holds as the contrast case to a book that hands over a toolkit. (Basis: vault summary, quoting a sentence of the mesh grid (an outside evaluation of the book's thinkers).) |
| Facilitation of facilitation — writing Book I for facilitators | the author's practice | the reframe conversation (`reframe-conversation-full-2026-08-13.md`), lines 476, 576, 1358 (author's turns) | B | learning theory | from the author's record | The author describes his own work as 'a unique facilitation of facilitation' and proposes a first draft written for facilitators, so that the duty to prove authority shifts into 'just a facilitation of facilitation'. (Basis: author's words (transcript lines 476 and 1358).) |
| Kapleau's printed dokusan transcript — a book standing in for a teacher | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | Kapleau published a Zen teacher's actual replies to ten Westerners so that students without a competent teacher could 'commence' the practice, bounding what a book can do at beginning it. (Basis: thinker's words (Kapleau's editor's introduction).) |
| Ludwig Wittgenstein — philosophy as therapy and the ladder | thinker | `notes/ludwig wittgenstein.md` | E | other | read at the primary source | Wittgenstein treats philosophy as a therapy that dissolves confusions by arranging what is already in plain view, notes that what matters most is hidden by its familiarity, and ends the Tractatus by asking the reader to throw away the ladder once climbed. (Basis: thinker's words (Investigations, Tractatus, On Certainty quoted).) |
| Rainer Maria Rilke — trained looking and letters to a young poet | thinker | `notes/rainer maria rilke.md` | C | improvisation, play, and the arts | held at second hand | Rilke's thing-poems came from a trained practice of sustained looking at objects, which his Malte names 'Ich lerne sehen' (I am learning to see), and his letters to a young poet tell the poet that nobody can advise or help him, that he should go into himself, and that he should live the questions now. (Basis: thinker's words (German quoted, verified in the generative pass).) |
| Søren Kierkegaard — indirect communication | thinker | `notes/soren kierkegaard.md` | E | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | Kierkegaard wrote pseudonymous books built so a reader cannot lean on the author's authority: each life-view is given to an invented author, the result is withheld so the reader must reproduce it in his own existence (double reflection), and a book may end by taking itself back. (Basis: vault summary from the Stanford Encyclopedia and Storm's commentary.) |
| The book asks for attention before it asks for action | practice note | `notes/the book asks only for attention until it has given the reader something to act with.md` | E | learning theory | from the book's drafts | The book's closing instructions ask the reader only to notice what is already present until the practice chapters, and assign a task ('Choose one. | The book's room offered beside the person | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | other | from the book's drafts | The executed draft offers the reader a room to walk into as the second party beside them, an arrangement rather than a person. (Basis: draft's words (abridged p. 45).) |
| The book's text-carried companions — guild, margin faculty, Doubting Hand, verso column | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | other | from the book's drafts | The descriptive sources design companions carried by the page — a guild of thinkers and a margin faculty that annotate, a Doubting Hand that argues and visibly loses, a column of walkers who confirm, and a quick pencil — and no draft executes them. (Basis: corpus's words (atlas §IX.7, §XI.1c; walked pp. 19, 24–25).) |

(Basis: vault's record of the author's decisions.) |
| The daily heartbeat — Morning Scan and Evening Synthesis | the author's practice | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md`, lines 32–39 | E | other | from the author's record | The author designed a personal knowledge system with 'a daily heartbeat that wakes me to survey', preceded by a September 2025 Tana design that ran a Morning Scan of what resonated overnight and an Evening Synthesis. (Basis: author's words ([D], 19 June 2026) and the provenance summary.) |
| The letter's addressee — the friend written to | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | from the author's record | The first telling is written as letters to one friend, and the pilot letter was sent to two real readers, so the book's native genre is a letter read alone that expects an answer. | The reader of the answerable last line | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | from the book's drafts | The descriptive sources plan a last line written so that the reader can answer it, ending 'welcome home', while the executed draft ends with the imperative 'Step in.' | The Second Chair device — reading toward someone | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | from the book's drafts | The descriptive sources design the book as one half of a pair, its loneliest passages built to be read toward someone, with study in pairs as its lineage. (Basis: corpus's words (walked pp. 20, 25).) |
| The sending and the answerable word | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | dialogic | from the book's drafts | The vault holds that a word kept at the inner level can be perfected forever while a word spoken or sent can be answered, so the book's completion is its sending and its last line is written so that it can be answered. (Basis: corpus's words (atlas §VI) with the vault's reading.) |
| The six-phase psychedelic arc | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | transpersonal | from the book's drafts | The book walks its reader through preparation, onset, deepening, peak, descent, and integration with no substance involved, the pacing enacting the arc the chapters explain. | The two-book mirror and the author as first participant | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | The vault holds that the book for facilitators and the book for self-facilitation mirror each other on every shared concern, and that the author is the first participant who must undergo what the book asks of its reader.
