| The written, asynchronous therapist — a responder rather than a body | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | B | other | read at the primary source | In internet therapy that is largely written and asynchronous the alliance predicts outcome as strongly as face to face (r = .275 against .278), and every case still has 'a person on the other end who reads what the client wrote and answers it'. (Basis: authors' words (Flückiger et al. 2018) and the survey's.) |
| Theodor Adorno — the jargon of authenticity | thinker | `notes/theodor adorno.md` | E | critical and liberatory | held at second hand | Adorno's Jargon of Authenticity charges that words like encounter and authenticity radiate significance the sentence has not earned, console rather than change, and cannot be falsified, which the vault uses as a test the book's own prose must survive. (Basis: vault summary from the Stanford Encyclopedia and a secondary reading.) |
| Wittgenstein — Investigations, Tractatus, On Certainty | reading record | archive/reading — wittgenstein (investigations, tractatus, on certainty).md | E | other | read at the primary source | Philosophical Investigations treats philosophy as a therapy that leaves everything as it is and dissolves problems, the Tractatus asks the reader to throw away the ladder, and On Certainty's hinge propositions hold fast by practice rather than by truth. (Basis: thinker's words (Anscombe and Pears–McGuinness translations).) |
| Wolfgang Iser — the textual gap and evocation-then-negation | thinker | `notes/wolfgang iser.md` | E | existential and phenomenological | read at the primary source | Iser shows that a text can leave gaps the reader must fill and can evoke a familiar assumption and then negate it, turning the reader back onto his own preconceptions, while conceding that 'we do not know what happens to us' in such reading. (Basis: thinker's words (1972 essay quoted with pages).) |

### Two people, one helping

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| al-Qushayrī — states and stations on the Sufi path | thinker | `notes/al-qushayri.md` | D | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | Al-Qushayrī's Risāla teaches the aspirant to tell a state that descends as a gift from a station earned by effort, so that a visitation is not mistaken for an attainment, and it requires a master (shaykh) to form the novice over the whole road. (Basis: thinker's words (Knysh 2007 translation, quoted with page numbers).) |
| al-Qushayrī's Risāla on state and station (Knysh 2007) | reading record | archive/reading — al-qushayri, the risala on state and station (knysh 2007).md | D | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | The Risāla, read whole in Knysh's translation, defines states as gifts that descend and stations as earned by effort, teaches the distinction to the aspirant as a first-person tool, and requires a master for the novice's formation. (Basis: thinker's words, quoted from the Knysh translation itself (grade A).) |
| Carl Rogers — the necessary and sufficient conditions | thinker | `notes/carl rogers.md` | A | humanistic and person-centered | read at the primary source | Rogers holds that a helper who is congruent, feels unconditional positive regard, and communicates an empathic understanding of the client's own frame of reference supplies conditions that are necessary and sufficient for change, so that technique and diagnosis are not what does the work. (Basis: thinker's words (Rogers 1957 quoted).) |
| Donald Winnicott — holding, the capacity to be alone, and the transitional object | thinker | `notes/donald winnicott.md` | A | psychodynamic | read at the primary source | Winnicott holds that a person becomes able to be alone, to play, and to find their own gesture in the reliable, non-impinging presence of another who adapts less over time, survives attack without retaliating, and lets a transitional object stand between inner and outer without asking whether it was found or made. (Basis: thinker's words (1958, 1960, 1969, 1971 papers quoted).) |
| Engler's clinician — structural assessment as certifier | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | D | psychodynamic | read at the primary source | Engler's clinician assesses a meditator's level of personality organization, which neither the student nor the meditation teacher can see from inside, to tell felt emptiness from śūnyatā. (Basis: thinker's words (Engler 1984, pp. 37–43).) |
| Flückiger et al. — the alliance in adult psychotherapy | reading record | archive/reading — fluckiger, del re, wampold and horvath, the alliance in adult psychotherapy (2018).md | B | other | read at the primary source | A meta-analysis of 295 studies found the therapeutic alliance predicts outcome at r = .278 face to face and r = .275 in largely written internet therapy, and recommends addressing ruptures directly and assessing the alliance from the client's side. (Basis: authors' words (the article read whole).) |
| Gendlin — A Theory of Personality Change (1964) | reading record | archive/reading — gendlin, a theory of personality change (1964).md | A | experiential and Focusing-oriented | read at the primary source | Gendlin's 1964 theory defines the correctness of a word by its felt effect on the process (referent movement), lists four kinds of inward attention that are not focusing, and finds focusing alone possible but unsteady and much easier with an understanding listener. (Basis: thinker's words (read complete).) |
| Gendlin's understanding listener | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | experiential and Focusing-oriented | read at the primary source | Gendlin found that a person who circles alone for hours without movement can, with an understanding listener, get referent movement from saying only a little. (Basis: thinker's words (Gendlin 1964, fn. 13 and def. 13).) |
| Heidegger's clearing — preparing the room is enough | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | A | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | The vault reads Heidegger's clearing as prior to and receptive of whatever appears in it, so a facilitator's work is to prepare the room rather than to produce what shows up there. (Basis: vault's words drawing on Heidegger's term.) |
| Heinz von Foerster — second-order cybernetics | thinker | `notes/heinz von foerster.md` | E | other | read at the primary source | Von Foerster's second-order cybernetics puts the observer inside what is described, and his ethical rule for anyone acting on a system is to act so as to increase the number of choices. (Basis: thinker's words (1973 and 1991/92 essays quoted).) |
| Holding space for others and for oneself | the author's practice | `provenance/provenance third addendum.md`, lines 62–64 | A | humanistic and person-centered | from the author's record | The earliest record sighting of the author's stance, December 2024, describes 'your extraordinary capacity for holding space for others and the ongoing learning of how to hold space for yourself'. (Basis: collaborator's words ([C], 25 December 2024).) |
| Ignatius of Loyola — the Spiritual Exercises and discernment of spirits | thinker | `notes/ignatius of loyola.md` | B | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | Ignatius's Spiritual Exercises have a director give an exercitant a phased sequence of prayers and imaginative contemplations over four Weeks, receive the exercitant's inner movements without persuading them, and hand over rules for telling which movements to trust by their whole course and aftermath. (Basis: thinker's words (Puhl and Mullan translations, §§ quoted).) |
| Jessica Benjamin — thirdness and mutual recognition | thinker | `notes/jessica benjamin.md` | B | psychodynamic | held at second hand | Benjamin's relational psychoanalysis names the co-created space of mutual recognition between analyst and patient 'the third', whose collapse is a doer-and-done-to relation in which one person acts and the other only submits. (Basis: vault summary from the mesh grid (an outside evaluation of the book's thinkers) and general knowledge (her books unread).) |
| Laozi — the Daodejing on wu-wei, ziran, and subtraction | reading record | archive/reading — laozi, daodejing (the wu-wei, ziran, and subtraction chapters).md | A | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | The Daodejing's sage acts without forcing and teaches without words, the best ruler is barely known so that when the work is done the people say it happened of itself, and the way is followed by doing less every day. (Basis: text's words (Legge, Waley, Lau, Feng and English, checked against the Chinese).) |
| Laozi's sage who teaches without words | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | The Daodejing's sage practises the teaching that uses no words, and the best ruler is barely known, so that when the work is done the people say it happened of itself. (Basis: text's words (Daodejing 2 and 17).) |
| Luria (Vital) — tzimtzum, the withdrawal that makes room | reading record | archive/reading — luria (vital), etz chaim gate 1 on tzimtzum.md | E | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | The Etz Chaim describes the Infinite contracting and withdrawing to leave an empty space in which a world that is not simply God can exist, so the first creative act is a withdrawal. (Basis: text's words (Sefaria translation and Hebrew).) |
| Martin Heidegger — aletheia, releasement, and the clearing | thinker | `notes/martin heidegger.md` | A | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | Heidegger names truth as unconcealment (aletheia) and holds that a turn cannot be manufactured, so what remains possible is preparation and releasement (Gelassenheit) toward things; the vault uses his clearing for the rule that preparing the room is enough. (Basis: mixed: his retraction of 'truth' verified in the crossover research; four refusals derived by the vault.) |
| Maturana and Varela — perturb, never instruct | thinker | `notes/maturana and varela.md` | A | other | read at the primary source | Maturana and Varela hold that a living system is structurally determined, so an interaction can trigger a change in it but never specify that change, which the vault states as the rule that a helper or a book can perturb a person and never install anything. (Basis: thinker's words (The Tree of Knowledge quoted).) |
| Peter Crone — living inside a sentence you did not choose | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | C | cognitive and behavioral | held at second hand | The corpus quotes Crone's diagnosis that 'you are living inside a sentence you didn't choose', which the survey sorts with Katie and Singer as seeing through a believed thought. (Basis: corpus's words (arc-gloss ch7).) |
| Plato — the midwife and recollection (Meno, Theaetetus) | reading record | archive/reading — plato, meno and theaetetus on drawing-out and wonder (c. 380 bce).md | B | dialogic | read at the primary source | Socrates, as a midwife barren of wisdom, questions a learner so that the learner brings forth what they already hold, as the slave boy reaches a geometric truth by questions alone, and he tests whether what is born is real or a 'wind-egg'. (Basis: thinker's words (Jowett and Fowler translations).) |
| Rogers — the necessary and sufficient conditions (1957) | reading record | archive/reading — rogers, the necessary and sufficient conditions (1957).md | A | humanistic and person-centered | read at the primary source | The 1957 paper states six conditions — contact, client incongruence, therapist congruence, unconditional positive regard, empathic understanding of the client's frame, and its minimal communication — as necessary and sufficient, and generalizes them to any helping relationship. (Basis: thinker's words (paper read whole).) |
| Rogers's therapist as receiver | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | humanistic and person-centered | read at the primary source | Rogers's therapist receives the client's frame with congruence, unconditional positive regard, and empathy the client perceives, and may voice meanings the client is scarcely aware of, but not name from an external scheme. (Basis: thinker's words (Rogers 1957).) |
| States and stations (ḥāl and maqām) — survey entry | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | D | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | The survey sorts the Sufi stations as a staged arc and the distinction between a state that visits and a station that is built as a guard, and uses it to read the author's own recognition as a visited state. (Basis: survey's words over al-Qushayrī's.) |
| The discrimination family — Engler on self and no-self (1984) | reading record | archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md | D | psychodynamic | read at the primary source | Engler argues that 'you have to be somebody before you can be nobody', that insight meditation is probably contraindicated where a cohesive self is lacking, and that only a clinician's structural assessment can tell felt emptiness from śūnyatā, which neither student nor teacher can see from inside. (Basis: thinker's words, quoted from the 1984 article itself (grade A).) |
| The discrimination family — Ignatius's rules for discernment | reading record | archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md | B | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | Ignatius's rules for discerning spirits judge an inner movement by the whole course of thought and its aftermath rather than by how consoling it feels, forbid changes during desolation, and route the decisive disclosure to a confessor, with the director deciding which rules to give and when. (Basis: thinker's words (Puhl and Mullan, §§313–336).) |
| The discrimination family — Kapleau and Yasutani on makyō and dokusan | reading record | archive/reading — the discrimination family (ignatius §§313–336, engler 1984, kapleau's three pillars).md | D | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | In The Three Pillars of Zen, Yasutani tells students to ignore visions (makyō) and keep sitting, insists that awakening is not self-authenticating and must be checked in private interview (dokusan), and Kapleau publishes the interview transcripts so readers without a teacher can 'commence' the practice. (Basis: thinker's words, quoted from the Anchor edition itself (grade A).) |
| The four movements — recognition, relation, participation, stewardship | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | Both planned books move through recognition, relation, participation, and stewardship, and differ only in whether the fourth is stewardship of the field (Book I) or of the self (Book II). (Basis: model's words (reframe transcript, locator 16932).) |
| The Fractal of Facilitation — one relation at every scale | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | The reframe conversation holds that one relation, facilitation, repeats at seven or eight scales — facilitator and participant, practitioner and self, author and reader, AI and author, corpus and future self, tradition and generations, ecology and civilization — each tested by whether the facilitating thing increases the other's capacity to participate or takes it away. (Basis: model's words (reframe transcript, locator 14914), held in the-conversation-catalog.md.) |
| The helper's restraint — the affirmative-way harvest | practice model | `notes/relational-field.md` | A | humanistic and person-centered | held at second hand | Twenty-one notes, stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, hold the helper's non-doing as the active ingredient: the helper's core verb is to let (let learn, let be, let grow), restraint is trained, the helper has no doctrine to install, and the surest sign of success is that the person says 'we did it ourselves'. (Basis: vault's words from a 2026-08-21 brainstorm naming Winnicott, Rogers, Buber, Noddings, Heidegger's leaping-ahead, Laozi, Plato, and Montessori.) |
| The Ignatian director — former, gatekeeper, certifier, companion | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | B | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | The Ignatian director accompanies the exercitant over the Weeks or nine months, decides whether and when to give the rules for discernment, receives the decisive disclosure (or routes it to a confessor), and stands 'in the centre like a balance' without persuading. (Basis: thinker's words (Spiritual Exercises, Annotations and §§8–10, 17, 326).) |
| The Ignatian Exercises — director, Weeks, and rules for discernment | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | B | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | The survey sorts Ignatius's director, who arranges conditions and withholds direction, as a container model, the four Weeks as a staged arc, and the rules for discernment as a guard that needs a second person to run. (Basis: survey's words over Ignatius's.) |
| The paradoxes by role — influence without domination | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | The paradoxes family holds that every role in the book (author, facilitator, researcher, system-designer) carries one tension, which it calls 'one knot': 'participatory sovereignty requires influence without domination'; each role fails by control or by abdication, and the tension is to be lived with rather than solved. (Basis: model's words (reframe), held in the-paradoxes.md.) |
| The reframe conversation's facilitation craft (Book I's nascent notes) | practice model | `notes/relational-field.md` | B | participatory and emergent | held at second hand | Fifteen notes, stated but not yet substantiated by a reading, set out a facilitation craft: facilitation as stewardship of conditions, the facilitator inside the field rather than outside it, attention as contagious and therefore an intervention, the facilitator's disappearance as success, and 'do not solve what the participant has not yet had the opportunity to encounter'. (Basis: model's words (reframe transcript); all fifteen cite only the conversation.) |
| The Sufi shaykh and the novice (al-Qushayrī) | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | D | contemplative, theistic | read at the primary source | In al-Qushayrī's Risāla a master educates the novice in conduct over the whole path ('he who does not have a master, Satan is his leader'), yet concedes the disciple's own inner criterion when the disciple states it, and the state itself descends as a gift that no one can earn. (Basis: thinker's words (Knysh 2007, pp. 78, 106, 207, 405–406).) |
| The Vijñāna Bhairava — Bhairava answering the Goddess with 112 practices | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | C | contemplative, nondual | held at second hand | In the Vijñāna Bhairava the Goddess asks and Bhairava answers with 112 brief centering practices (dhāraṇās), such as attending to the gap between two breaths, given inside that intimacy. (Basis: vault paraphrase and the corpus's gloss (atlas §IX.6c).) |
| Wilhelm Reich — character analysis | thinker | `notes/wilhelm reich.md` | D | somatic | held at second hand | Reich's character analysis treats defense as a settled arrangement of posture, breathing, and muscle tone that outlasts the threat and cannot be argued away, and works on it directly in the body so that what it protected becomes reachable. (Basis: vault summary from secondary knowledge (Character Analysis unread).) |
| Winnicott — the four moves kept distinct | reading record | archive/reading — winnicott, the four moves kept distinct (1958–1971).md | A | psychodynamic | read at the primary source | Four papers keep Winnicott's moves distinct: being alone in another's presence, a good-enough environment that adapts less over time, an object that survives destruction by not retaliating, and a transitional object whose found-or-made question is never asked, with holding as the reliable frame beneath them. (Basis: thinker's words (four papers read whole).) |
| Winnicott's facilitating environment — former, receiver, companion, survivor | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | psychodynamic | read at the primary source | Winnicott's good-enough other adapts almost fully and then less, so the person's own capacity grows; stays present without impinging, so the person can be alone and find their own gesture; and survives being attacked by not retaliating, which makes the other real. (Basis: thinker's words (Winnicott 1958, 1960, 1969, 1971 ch. 1).) |
| Zen dokusan and the makyō instruction | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | D | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | The survey sorts Zen's private interview with a teacher as a container, and its makyō instruction — ignore visions and keep sitting — as a guard that refuses to let any experience certify itself. (Basis: survey's words over Yasutani's (Kapleau).) |
| Zen dokusan — the teacher who certifies and assigns koans | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | D | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | In private interview (dokusan) a Zen teacher such as Yasutani tells a student whether an experience is awakening (kensho) or a vision to ignore (makyō), and only a teacher who has given dokusan assigns a koan. (Basis: thinker's words (Kapleau, Three Pillars, Part One §III and fn. 43).) |

### Two people as equals

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Abhinavagupta's friend who says 'this is that very man' | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | C | contemplative, nondual | held at second hand | In Abhinavagupta's commentary a friend completes the recognition by saying 'this is that very man', where Utpaladeva's own verse has the beloved simply stand unrecognized until recognition dawns. (Basis: Abhinavagupta's commentarial formula as the vault's reading reports it.) |
| Anam Cara — the soul friend the book declines to supply | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | contemplative, theistic | from the book's drafts | The draft names the Celtic soul friend John O'Donohue retrieved and says: 'You may not have an Anam Cara right now… The book will not give you one. But it will, by the end, equip you to recognize one if they arrive, and to be one — first to yourself, then to others.' (Basis: draft's words (abridged p. 9).) |
| Austin's uptake | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | read at the primary source | Austin holds that performing an act such as a warning involves securing uptake, the hearer taking what is said in a certain sense, and that a performative in a poem is 'hollow or void'. (Basis: thinker's words (How to Do Things with Words, Lectures II and IX).) |
| Bakhtin's answer-word | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | read at the primary source | Bakhtin holds that a word in living conversation is oriented toward a future answer-word, provoking and anticipating it, and that any understanding is imbued with response. (Basis: thinker's words ('Discourse in the Novel', p. 280, via scholarship; Speech Genres p. 68).) |
| Buber — I and Thou and 'Elements of the Interhuman' | reading record | archive/reading — buber, i and thou and elements of the interhuman (1923, 1965).md | A | dialogic | read at the primary source | I and Thou, read whole, holds that the relation comes first and constitutes the I, that the meeting can be prepared for with the whole being but comes by grace and is lost once used as a means, and (via a secondary profile) that a helper makes the other present, confirms them, and keeps a primary distance. (Basis: thinker's words (Smith translation) and a secondary profile (Yaron 1993).) |
| Buber's Thou — answerer, summoner, and grace | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | dialogic | read at the primary source | Buber's Thou is met rather than described, addresses and summons the person, and comes by grace: 'The Thou meets me through grace — it is not found by seeking'. (Basis: thinker's words (I and Thou, Part One).) |
| Chavruta — study in pairs | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | dialogic | held at second hand | In chavruta two students read a shared text aloud, translate it, take it apart, and argue over it, and Orit Kent decomposes the partnership into listening and articulating, wondering and focusing, and supporting and challenging. (Basis: vault's summary of Kent; the corpus's relay (walked IX, p. 20).) |
| De Jaegher and Di Paolo — participatory sense-making | thinker | `notes/de jaegher and di paolo.md` | A | participatory and emergent | read at the primary source | De Jaegher and Di Paolo show that a live interaction can take on a fleeting autonomy in which two people make meaning neither could make alone, and that this needs real-time mutual adjustment that a text cannot give. (Basis: thinker's words (2007 article and 2008 chapter quoted).) |
| Levinas's face — the summoner | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | dialogic | read at the primary source | Levinas holds that the face of another speaks and 'opens the primordial discourse whose first word is obligation', calling a person in their uniqueness to answer where no one can substitute for them. (Basis: thinker's words (Totality and Infinity pp. 66, 201; Otherwise than Being p. 59).) |
| Looping to Zero | the book's form | `abridged` p. 42 | E | contemplative, nondual | from the book's drafts | In the middle of a conversation, the reader notices having drifted into performance, defense, or an agenda and returns to presence without an agenda, often silently; the draft says the other person will feel the difference. (Basis: draft's words (abridged ch14, form 8).) |
| Martin Buber — I–Thou meeting | thinker | `notes/martin buber.md` | A | dialogic | read at the primary source | Buber distinguishes meeting another as a Thou, with the whole of oneself, from using or describing them as an It, and holds that the meeting can be prepared for by availability but never produced by technique, since every means turns the Thou into an It. (Basis: thinker's words (I and Thou read whole, Smith translation).) |
| Offering a question to facilitate the other's own conclusion | the author's practice | the reframe conversation (`reframe-conversation-full-2026-08-13.md`), line 2509 (author's turn) | A | dialogic | from the author's record | The author says he offered a question 'as a way to explore and facilitate your natural conclusion here too', letting his conversation partner reach the idea he had already arrived at rather than stating it. (Basis: author's words (transcript line 2509).) |
| Relational tai chi — sustaining the pulse in live conversation | the author's practice | `provenance/provenance third addendum.md`, lines 52–55 | A | contemplative, nondual | from the author's record | The May 2026 record names the capacity the author was then working to develop as sustaining spanda, the pulse, 'in live, unstructured conversation', which he called relational tai chi, trusting that 'I'll know as I'm speaking how it's feeling for me.' (Basis: author's words ([D], May 2026) within the addendum's summary.) |
| Reveal Your Experience and revealing impact | the author's practice | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md`, lines 118–121 | B | humanistic and person-centered | from the author's record | Among the Authentic Relating moves the record shows the author working with are Reveal Your Experience (speaking in the first person, in the present tense, about what is felt in the body) and revealing impact ('when you said X, I felt Y'). (Basis: provenance reconstruction's summary of the author's record.) |
| Sovereignty in relationship | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | A | dialogic | held at second hand | The vault holds sovereignty as a quality of relationship rather than its opposite: being able to recognize, choose, and respond without collapsing into someone else's script, avoiding both isolation and dependence. (Basis: model's words (reframe conversation), taken up by the author.) |
| The 'what remains here?' question | the author's practice | the reframe conversation (`reframe-conversation-full-2026-08-13.md`), line 974 (author's turn) | A | dialogic | from the author's record | The author says he always likes to ask 'what remains here', inviting a partner to describe the remainder of a domain being explored, 'without attaching myself to it', okay with whatever comes or does not. (Basis: author's words (transcript line 974).) |
| The answerability triad — Austin, Bakhtin, Levinas | reading record | archive/reading — the answerability triad (austin, bakhtin, levinas).md | E | dialogic | read at the primary source | Austin holds that a performative succeeds only when the hearer takes it up, Bakhtin that a word is built toward its answer and each person's deed cannot be done by anyone else, and Levinas that another's face summons a person into an obligation no one else can take. (Basis: thinkers' words (Austin, Bakhtin, Levinas quoted; some lines via scholarship).) |
| The book's witness who receives without interpreting | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | humanistic and person-centered | from the book's drafts | The draft tells the reader, after a peak, to talk to someone who can witness without intruding and who receives what arose 'without rushing to interpret it, normalize it, pathologize it, or spiritualize it', because 'a witness is not a commentator'. (Basis: draft's and corpus's words (abridged p. 37; first-telling p. 9).) |
| The participation law's independent arrivals | reading record | archive/reading — the participation law's independent arrivals (von foerster, maturana-varela, garfinkel, schön, de jaegher-di paolo, vervaeke).md | E | participatory and emergent | read at the primary source | Von Foerster, Maturana and Varela, Garfinkel, Schön, De Jaegher and Di Paolo, and in part Vervaeke each reach, from outside the book's traditions, the rule that order, knowing, and meaning are made by participants in the doing rather than delivered to a spectator. (Basis: thinkers' words, some quoted from their own texts (grade A) and some through secondary sources (grade B).) |
| The participatory dyad (De Jaegher and Di Paolo) | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | A | participatory and emergent | read at the primary source | Two people coordinating in real time can generate meaning neither has alone, through live mutual regulation that even a short delay deforms. (Basis: thinker's words (2007, pp. 493, 497).) |
| Utpaladeva's beloved, present and unrecognized | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | E | contemplative, nondual | read at the primary source | Utpaladeva's founding image has the beloved stand before the maiden, doing nothing, until she recognizes who he is. (Basis: thinker's words (IPK IV.1.17, Torella p. 218).) |
| Wiping the Mirror | the book's form | `abridged` p. 42 | E | psychodynamic | from the book's drafts | The reader notices when they are reacting to someone as a projection of another person, such as a parent, a partner, or a figure from the past, and looks again at the person actually present. (Basis: draft's words (abridged ch14, form 7).) |

### A small group

| Holding | Kind | Where | Who decides | Mental model | Standing | The researcher's summary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 'It's not me, it's what's moving through me right now' | the author's practice | the reframe conversation (`reframe-conversation-full-2026-08-13.md`), line 376 (author's turn) | B | humanistic and person-centered | from the author's record | The author names the quality facilitation has, which he wants built into the book's own framework so it does not become one man's master doctrine: 'it's not me it's what's moving through me right now'. (Basis: author's words (transcript line 376).) |
| Calling the Room | the book's form | `abridged` p. 42 | E | dialogic | from the book's drafts | The reader says aloud what is happening between the people present, as in 'I notice we've gotten tense' or 'I notice you seem far away', so that an unspoken dynamic between the people becomes something they can notice together. (Basis: draft's words (abridged ch14, form 9).) |
| Circling — noticing the relational field | the author's practice | `provenance/provenance of the corpus round two.md`, lines 101–110 and 122 | B | humanistic and person-centered | from the author's record | The record shows the author attending a circling event in April 2025 and working with circling's noticing of the relational field, the practice of attending to and naming what is happening between the people present. (Basis: provenance reconstruction's summary.) |
| David Bohm — On Dialogue | reading record | archive/reading — bohm, on dialogue (1996).md | A | dialogic | read at the primary source | Bohm's dialogue has a group suspend its assumptions, neither acting on them nor suppressing them, so that a shared stream of meaning can flow among the people and something new can emerge, as distinct from discussion, negotiation, or seeking consensus. (Basis: thinker's words (the core essay read whole).) |
| Deida (and Wineland) in their own words — the edge and the men's circle | reading record | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md | D | other | read at the primary source | Deida's The Way of the Superior Man has a man lean just beyond his edge of fear, meet weekly with close men friends who assign him a behavioral experiment, and agree a consequence for freezing, on the ground of a doctrine of masculine and feminine polarity. (Basis: thinker's words: Deida quoted from his book itself (grade A), Wineland only through an interview and his publisher's copy (grade B).) |
| Deida and Wineland — the withheld gift, the closed body, and the edge | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | D | other | read at the primary source | The survey sorts Deida and Wineland's model — suffering as a closed body or a withheld gift, the cure to open and give at one's edge — as polarity plus privation, the one coach model whose move is an act at a threshold, nearest the participation law. (Basis: survey's words, later checked against Deida's text.) |

| Gurdjieff's school as former | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | D | other | held at second hand | The survey lists the Fourth Way school, with a teacher who sees what the pupil cannot, as a second person that shapes a person over time. | Keith Johnstone — Impro | thinker | `notes/keith johnstone.md` | B | improvisation, play, and the arts | read at the primary source | Johnstone trains people to stop blocking: to be obvious rather than clever, to accept and build on a partner's offer, and to notice the status transactions always going on in posture and voice, with a teacher who takes the blame for failure so that students feel safe. | Kurt Lewin — action research and the T-group seed | thinker | `notes/kurt lewin.md` | B | participatory and emergent | read at the primary source | Lewin's action research changes a social system on purpose, watches the effect, and revises, and his 1946 workshop found that participants learned most from hearing the trainers' evening review of the group's own recorded dynamics, the origin of the T-group, a group whose subject is its own functioning. (Basis: thinker's words (1934 first edition and 1945 symposium; graded B overall, one step below a full reading of the text itself).) |

(Basis: survey's words over Erhard and Jensen's.) |

(Basis: author's words ([D]) within the provenance reconstruction's summary.) |
| Hübl's collective trauma group and the never-formed gap | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | B | transpersonal | held at second hand | The survey sorts Hübl's group work as a container that is opened-with by necessity, and his account of inherited trauma as a third kind of privation: an absence where something should have developed and did not. (Basis: survey's words over the vault's secondary account.) |
| Hübl's group as former | companion role | `ops/second person ecology.md` | B | transpersonal | held at second hand | The survey lists Hübl's collective trauma group as a second person that shapes people over time and that, on his account, alone can metabolize what was never individual. (Basis: survey's words; his work unread.) |

(Basis: author's words (transcript line 266).) |
| Robbins in his own words (Awaken the Giant Within, Unlimited Power) | reading record | archive/reading — robbins and deida in their own words (awaken the giant within, unlimited power, the way of the superior man).md | D | cognitive and behavioral | read at the primary source | Robbins's books teach changing state through physiology and focus, then Neuro-Associative Conditioning with a replacement pattern, and for global change a shift of identity by decision, while conceding that willpower alone and state change alone do not last. (Basis: thinker's words, quoted from both books themselves (grade A).) |
| Robbins's state change (survey entry) | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | D | cognitive and behavioral | read at the primary source | The survey sorts Robbins's direct change of state as the one family at odds with the author's account: opened-by in its pure form, which the middle voice has to earn its way past. (Basis: survey's words, later checked against Robbins's books.) |
| The Racket — a fixed complaint with a hidden payoff | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | D | existential and phenomenological | read at the primary source | The Racket names a persistent way of being plus a complaint that something should not be the way it is, kept in place by a payoff such as being right or dominating others, and the Forum's move is to expose the payoff and recognize the pattern rather than to cure it. (Basis: Landmark's syllabus and Denison's ethnography; the corpus's formula 'Complaint + Payoff'.) |
| The routing surfaces recounted — the Landmark Forum (Speaking Being, syllabus, ethnography) | reading record | archive/reading — the routing surfaces recounted (speaking being, moreno, the scharmer count).md | D | existential and phenomenological | read at the primary source | The Landmark Forum, as read in the transcript sample of Speaking Being, Landmark's own syllabus, and Denison's ethnography, has participants separate what happened from the story they tell about it, recognize their rackets (a complaint plus a payoff) and what they cost, and on the third day invent a new possibility by declaration. (Basis: participants' and leaders' words from a 1989 transcript, a syllabus, and an ethnography (grade B, one step below a reading of the founder's own text).) |
| Thomas Hübl — collective trauma integration | thinker | `notes/thomas hubl.md` | B | transpersonal | held at second hand | Hübl works with groups, with a facilitator and over time, on trauma held collectively and across generations, treating what was never processed as an absence that descendants inherit and that only company can metabolize. (Basis: vault summary (no primary work read).) |
| Tony Robbins — state change and Neuro-Associative Conditioning | thinker | `notes/tony robbins.md` | D | cognitive and behavioral | read at the primary source | Robbins teaches changing one's state quickly through physiology and focus, then conditioning a replacement pattern, and, for lasting change, shifting one's identity by decision, on the premise that the person must be the source of their own change. (Basis: thinker's words (Awaken the Giant Within and Unlimited Power quoted).) |
| Werner Erhard — est, the Forum, and the leadership course | thinker | `notes/werner erhard.md` | D | existential and phenomenological | read at the primary source | Erhard's trainings hold that a person's world is generated by the conversation they are in, and have participants examine and drop the grip of their existing frames, separate what happened from their story about it, recognize their rackets (a fixed complaint with a hidden payoff), and create a new context by declaration. (Basis: mixed: Erhard and Jensen quoted; the corpus's sentences quoted.) |

(Basis: vault summary from secondary knowledge.) |

(Basis: vault summary (no primary work read).) |
| Van Gennep's rite of passage inside the house | practice model | `ops/practice-model ecology.md` | E | ritual and anthropological | held at second hand | The survey lists van Gennep's three-part rite of passage (separation, threshold, reincorporation) as a staged arc the book's house carries inside it. (Basis: survey's words; the gatekeeper note's inference.) |

(Basis: thinker's words (the essay 'In and Out of Time' read in full in one of the vault's research sweeps).) |

| J. L. Mehta — the longest way back | thinker | `notes/j l mehta.md` | E | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | Mehta, the founding scholar of the India–Heidegger encounter, held that the way to what is closest is the longest way back through the foreign, and so refused shortcuts to fusing traditions. | James Marcia — identity statuses | thinker | `notes/james marcia.md` | E | developmental | held at second hand | Marcia measured Erikson's identity stage with an interview that crosses exploration against commitment and defines foreclosure as commitment made without exploring alternatives, which the book states as its Foreclosure formula without credit. | Maurice Merleau-Ponty — the lived body | thinker | `notes/maurice merleau-ponty.md` | E | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | Merleau-Ponty holds that the lived body is the perceiving subject rather than an object or an instrument, which the vault keeps as a refusal of using the body as a test that certifies change. | Merleau-Ponty — the body-subject | reading record | archive/reading — merleau-ponty, phenomenology of perception on the body-subject (1945).md | E | existential and phenomenological | held at second hand | Merleau-Ponty holds that the lived body is the perceiving subject itself, constitutive of perception rather than an object or tool, with the reversibility of two hands touching and the flesh as the ground of there being a world. | Michel Henry — auto-affection | thinker | `notes/michel henry.md` | E | existential and phenomenological | read at the primary source | Henry holds that life manifests itself as a self-undergoing with no gap between what shows and what is shown, which the vault holds against the Śaiva tradition's two-term account of reflexive awareness. | Nisbett and Wilson — telling more than we can know | reading record | archive/reading — nisbett and wilson, telling more than we can know (1977).md | E | other | read at the primary source | Nisbett and Wilson found that people often cannot report the causes of their own responses and may not notice that their attitudes have changed, while they do know their current sensations. | Reinhard May — Heidegger's hidden sources | reading record | archive/reading — reinhard may, heidegger's hidden sources (1996).md | E | other | held at second hand | May's study, read through Parkes and others, documents a Daoist and Zen debt in Heidegger's thinking of Being, Nothing, the clearing, and the Way, but none in aletheia and none to Kashmir Śaivism. | Schwitzgebel — the unreliability of naive introspection | reading record | archive/reading — schwitzgebel, the unreliability of naive introspection (2008).md | E | other | read at the primary source | Schwitzgebel argues that people are often grossly wrong about their current emotional and bodily experience even on careful reflection, holds introspection necessary for a full life all the same, and finds no robust evidence yet that training fixes it.
